Scaling limits of tree-valued branching random walks
Thomas Duquesne, Robin Khanfir, Shen Lin, Niccolò Torri

Abstract
We consider a branching random walk (BRW) taking its values in the 𝚋 \mathtt{b} -ary rooted tree 𝕎 𝚋 \mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}} (i.e. the set of finite words written in the alphabet { 1 , … , 𝚋 } \{1,\ldots,\mathtt{b}\} , with 𝚋 ≥ 2 \mathtt{b}\!\geq\!2 ). The BRW is indexed by a critical Galton–Watson tree conditioned to have n n vertices; its offspring distribution is aperiodic and is in the domain of attraction of a γ \gamma -stable law, γ ∈ ( 1 , 2 ] \gamma\!\in\!(1,2] . The jumps of the BRW are those of a nearest-neighbour null-recurrent random walk on 𝕎 𝚋 \mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}} (reflection at the root of 𝕎 𝚋 \mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}} and otherwise: probability 1 / 2 1/2 to move closer to the root of 𝕎 𝚋 \mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}} and probability 1 / ( 2 ​ 𝚋 ) 1/(2\mathtt{b}) to move away from it to one of the 𝚋 \mathtt{b} sites above). We denote by ℛ 𝚋 ​ ( n ) \mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}(n) the range of the BRW in 𝕎 𝚋 \mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}} which is the set of all sites in 𝕎 𝚋 \mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}} visited by the BRW. We first prove a law of large numbers for # ​ ℛ 𝚋 ​ ( n ) \#\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}(n) and we also prove that if we equip ℛ 𝚋 ​ ( n ) \mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}(n) (which is a random subtree of 𝕎 𝚋 \mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}} ) with its graph-distance d 𝚐𝚛 d_{\mathtt{gr}} , then there exists a scaling sequence ( a n ) n ∈ ℕ (a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}} satisfying a n → ∞ a_{n}\!\rightarrow\!\infty such that the metric space ( ℛ 𝚋 ​ ( n ) , a n − 1 ​ d 𝚐𝚛 ) (\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}(n),a_{n}^{-1}d_{\mathtt{gr}}) , equipped with its normalised empirical measure, converges to the reflected Brownian cactus with γ \gamma -stable branching mechanism: namely, a random compact real tree that is a variant of the Brownian cactus introduced by N. Curien, J-F. Le Gall and G. Miermont in [ 7 ] . Keywords Branching random walks ⋅ \cdot Galton–Watson tree ⋅ \cdot Scaling limit ⋅ \cdot Superprocess ⋅ \cdot Brownian snake ⋅ \cdot Brownian cactus ⋅ \cdot Real tree Mathematics Subject Classification 60J80 ⋅ \cdot 60G50 ⋅ \cdot 60G52 ⋅ \cdot 60F17



1
Introduction

Since the seventies, branching random walk (BRW) is an area of research that is intensively studied and is linked to travelling wave solutions of
semi-linear partial differential equations (FKPP) or various models of statistical mechanics (Generalized random energy model, Mandelbrot’s cascades, Gaussian free field):
we refer to the book of Z. Shi
[
31
]
for an overview of this topic; we also refer to the works of S. Gouëzel, I. Huerter, S. Lalley and T. Sellke

[
20
,
17
,
19
,
16
]
for the study of BRW in hyperbolic spaces and to
T. Liggett
[
27
]
and to I. Benjamini and S. Müller
[
4
]
for branching random walks on trees.
In most of the previous works, BRWs are indexed by an infinite supercritical
Galton–Watson tree (GW-trees) and questions focus on various survival events or extremal behaviours of BRWs.

In this paper, we consider instead a BRW that takes its values
in the
\mathtt{b}
-ary tree
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
and that is indexed by a critical
Galton–Watson tree conditioned to have
n
vertices.
The jumps of the BRW are those of a
nearest-neighbour null-recurrent RW on
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
. Namely,
at the root of
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
(that is denoted by
\varnothing
), it is reflected and elsewhere,
with probability
1/2
, it jumps to the neighbour closer to the root of
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
and with probability
1/(2\mathtt{b})
, it moves further from the root of
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
and it jumps to one of the
\mathtt{b}
sites above.
We study the range
\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}(n)
of this BRW when
n\!\rightarrow\!\infty
.
More precisely, we first show that
\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{n}}\#\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}(n)
converges in probability to a constant; this law of large numbers is the analogue of the results due to J-F. Le Gall and L.
[
24
,
25
]
who treat the cases of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-valued BRWs that are indexed by critical GW-trees conditioned to have
n
vertices.
We then prove that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}(n)
, seen as a subtree of the
\mathtt{b}
-ary tree, converges, when it is suitably rescaled, to a continuum random tree called the reflected Brownian cactus with
\gamma
-stable branching mechanism: namely, a random compact real tree that is a
variant of the Brownian cactus introduced by N. Curien, J-F. Le Gall and G. Miermont in
[
7
]
.

This limit theorem for
\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}(n)
is related to earlier works on scaling limits of the range
of tree-valued critical or near-critical biased random walks (RWs): in particular we refer to D.
[
11
]
who deals with near-critical biased RWs on
\mathtt{b}
-ary trees, to Y. Peres and O. Zeitouni
[
30
]

who show that the distance to the root of a critical biased RW in a Galton–Watson environment is diffusive, to A. Dembo and N. Sun
[
8
]
who study the cases of critical biased RWs on
N
-type GW-trees,
to E. Aïdékon and L. de Raphélis
[
2
]
who improve Y. Peres and O. Zeitouni’s result and who show that the range of the same RW
converges when suitably rescaled, to a variant of the Brownian CRT, and to X. Chen and
G. Miermont
[
6
]
who show that rescaled Brownian bridges and loops in hyperbolic spaces converge to the Brownian CRT. Their work is based on a previous results due to P. Bougerol and T. Jeulin
[
5
]
. Independently, A. Stewart shows in his PhD Thesis
[
32
]
that the rescaled simple RW bridges on a
d
-regular tree (
d\geq 3
) converge to the Brownian CRT.

Let us describe more precisely the results that we obtain.
We consider a (rooted and ordered) Galton–Watson tree
\tau
with offspring distribution
\mu
that satisfies the following: we fix
\gamma\!\in\!(1,2]
and we assume

(\mathbf{H})\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\!\!(\mathrm{H}_{1}):\;\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\mu(k)=1,\\
\!\!(\mathrm{H}_{2}):\;\textrm{$\mu$ is aperiodic {(namely, $\mu$ is not supported by a proper subgroup of $\mathbb{Z}$),}}\\
\!\!(\mathrm{H}_{3}):\textrm{Either $\gamma\!=\!2$ and $\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k^{2}\mu(k)\!<\!\infty$, or $\gamma\!\in\!(1,2)$ and $\mu$ is in }\\
\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\,\textrm{the domain of attraction of a $\gamma$-stable law. }\end{array}\right.

(1)

Note that
(\mathrm{H}_{1})
implies that a.s. the total number of vertices
\#\tau
is finite;
(\mathrm{H}_{2})

implies that for all large enough integers
n
,
\mathbf{P}(\#\tau\!=\!n)\!>\!0
. We
translate
(\mathrm{H}_{3})
into the following assertion: let
(L_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be an i.i.d. sequence of

(\{\!-1\}\!\cup\!\mathbb{N})
-valued random variables such that
\mathbf{P}(L_{n}\!=\!k)\!=\!\mu(k+1)
,

k\!\geq\!-1
and let
X
be a real random variable whose law is spectrally positive

\gamma
-stable; it is characterised by its Laplace exponent:
\log\mathbf{E}[\exp(-\lambda X)]\!=\!\lambda^{\gamma}
,
\lambda\!\in\![0,\infty)
. Then under
(\mathrm{H}_{3})
, there exists a nondecreasing
\tfrac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}
-regularly varying sequence
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

\frac{a_{n}}{n}\big(L_{1}+\ldots+L_{n}-n\big)\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{\underset{n\rightarrow\,\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}}X\;.

(2)

As we see below,
under
(\mathbf{H})
,
\tau
behaves regularly when it is conditioned to be large: namely, we see
that suitably rescaled versions of
\tau
under
\mathbf{P}(\,\cdot\,|\,\#\tau\!=\!n)
converge in distribution
when
n\!\rightarrow\!\infty
.

Conveniently, we view
\tau
as a family tree whose ancestor is the root and where
siblings are ordered by birth-rank. The depth-first exploration of
\tau
is
the sequence of vertices
(u_{k})_{0\leq k<\#\tau}
that is defined recursively as follows:

u_{0}
is the root and for all
k\!\in\!\{0,\ldots,\#\tau\!-\!2\}
, let
v
be the most recent
ancestor of
u_{k}
having at least one unexplored child (note that possibly
v\!=\!u_{k}
);
then
u_{k+1}
is the unexplored child of
v
with least birth-rank. Our first result is
the following law of large numbers for the size of the range of the
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
-valued
\tau
-indexed critical branching random walk (see Sections
2
and
3
for more precise definitions).

Theorem 1.1

Let
\tau
be a Galton–Watson tree with offspring distribution
\mu
that satisfies
(\mathbf{H})
. Recall that
(u_{k})_{0\leq k<\#\tau}
stands for the depth-first exploration of
\tau
. Conditionally given
\tau
, let
(Y_{v})_{v\in\tau}
be a
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
-valued
\tau
-indexed critical branching random walk starting at
Y_{\mathtt{root}}\!=\!\varnothing
. Then, there exists a constant
c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}\!\in\!(0,\infty)
that only depends on
\mu
and
\mathtt{b}
such that

\forall\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty),\quad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{P}\Big(\tfrac{1}{n}\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\big|\#\big\{Y_{u_{l}};0\!\leq\!l\!<\!k\big\}\,-\,c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}\,k\big|\,>\varepsilon\,\Big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n\Big)=0\;,

(3)

In particular, for all
\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty)
, we get

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{P}\big(\,\big|\frac{1}{n}\#\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}-c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}\big|\!>\!\varepsilon\,\big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n\big)\!=\!0
, where
\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}
stands for the range
\{Y_{v};v\!\in\!\tau\}
of the branching random walk.

Let
\tau
,
(Y_{v})_{v\in\tau}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}\!=\!\{Y_{v};v\!\in\!\tau\}
be as in
Theorem
1.1
. Observe that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}
is a subtree of
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
. Our second main result is a limit theorem for rescaled versions of the metric spaces
(\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}},d_{\mathtt{gr}})
where

d_{\mathtt{gr}}
stands for the graph-distance. To state it, let us first recall a limit theorem for
(\tau,d_{\mathtt{gr}})
.
Set
H_{k}(\tau)\!=\!d_{\mathtt{gr}}(\mathtt{root},u_{k})
for all
k\!\in\!\{0,\ldots,\#\tau\!-\!1\}
, that is the height process of
\tau
. Note that

H(\tau)\!=\!\big(H_{k}(\tau)\big)_{k\in\{0,\ldots,\#\tau-1\}}
entirely codes
\tau
. Then, Theorem 3.1 in D.
[
10
]
asserts the following:

assume
(\mathbf{H})
as in (
1
) and let
(a_{n})
be as in (
2
); then there is a nonnegative continuous process
H=(H_{s})_{s\in[0,1]}
such that

\big(\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}H_{\lfloor ns\rfloor}(\tau)\big)_{\!s\in[0,1]}\quad\textit{under}\quad\mathbf{P}\big(\,\cdot\,\big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n\big)\;\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\;H,

(4)

weakly on
\mathbf{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
. When
\gamma\!\!=\!2
,
H
is the normalised Brownian excursion and this result is due to Aldous (see Theorem 23 in Aldous
[
3
]
). When
\gamma\!\in\!(1,2)
,
H
is the normalised excursion of the
\gamma
-stable height process that is a local-time function of a

\gamma
-stable spectrally positive Lévy process.

The metric space that is the limit of
(\tau,\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}d_{\mathtt{gr}})
as
n\!\rightarrow\!\infty

is derived from the normalised excursion of the
\gamma
-stable height process
H
as follows: for all

s_{1},s_{2}\!\in\![0,1]
,
we set

d_{H}(s_{1},s_{2})\!=\!H_{s_{1}}+H_{s_{2}}-2\min_{s_{1}\wedge s_{2}\leq s\leq s_{1}\vee s_{2}}H_{s}.

We easily check that a.s.
d_{H}
is a pseudo-metric on
[0,1]
. We introduce the relation
\sim_{H}
on
[0,1]
by setting
s_{1}\sim_{H}s_{2}
if and only if
d_{H}(s_{1},s_{2})\!=\!0
;
clearly,
\sim_{H}
is an equivalence relation and the normalised
\gamma
-stable Lévy tree is taken as the quotient space
T_{H}\!=\![0,1]/\!\sim_{H}
, equipped with the distance induced by
d_{H}
that we keep denoting
d_{H}
. We denote by
p_{H}\!:\![0,1]\rightarrow T_{H}
the canonical projection. Note that
p_{H}
is continuous; therefore
T_{H}
is compact and connected. Moreover,
T_{H}
is a real tree, namely, a metric space such that all pairs of points are joined by a unique simple arc that turns out to be a geodesic (see Definition
4.6
for more details). We set
r_{H}\!:=\!p_{H}(0)
that is viewed as the root of
T_{H}
and we equip
T_{H}
with the measure
\mu_{H}
that is the image of the Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]
via
p_{H}
, namely,
\int_{T_{H}}\!f\,d\mu_{H}\!=\!\int_{0}^{1}\!f(p_{H}(s))\,ds
, for all
continuous
f\!:\!T_{H}\!\rightarrow\!\mathbb{R}
. The convergence (
4
)
then implies the following one.

\big(\tau,\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}d_{\mathtt{gr}},\mathtt{root},\tfrac{1}{n}\mathtt{m}\big)\quad\textrm{under}\quad\mathbf{P}\big(\,\cdot\,\big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n\big)\;\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\;(T_{H},d_{H},r_{H},\mu_{H})

(5)

where
\mathtt{m}\!=\!\sum_{v\in\tau}\delta_{v}
stands for the counting measure on
\tau
. Here the convergence holds weakly on the space
\mathbb{M}
of isometry classes of pointed measured compact metric spaces equipped with the Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov distance

{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}
that makes it a Polish space, as proved in Theorem 2.5 of R. Abraham, J-F. Delmas and P. Hoscheit
[
1
]
. (See (
74
) for a precise definition of
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}
and see (
75
) for more details.) For more details on Lévy trees see J-F. Le Gall and Y. Le Jan
[
23
]
and D. and J-F. Le Gall
[
13
,
14
]
(see also Section
4.4
).

The limit of rescaled versions of the metric spaces
(\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}},d_{\mathtt{gr}})
is constructed as
follows: as proved in D. and J-F. Le Gall
[
14
]
(Lemma 6.4 p. 600, that is recalled in Lemma
4.32
), conditionally given
H
, there exists a Hölder-continuous centered Gaussian process
\sigma\!\in\!T_{H}\!\longmapsto\!W_{\sigma}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}
whose covariance is characterised by

\mathbf{E}\big[\big|W_{\sigma_{1}}\!-W_{\sigma_{2}}\big|^{2}\big|\,H\big]\!=\!d_{H}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
, for all
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\!\in\!T_{H}
. Then, we set

\forall\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\!\in\!T_{H},\quad d_{H,W}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})=|W_{\sigma_{1}}|+|W_{\sigma_{2}}|-2\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\min_{\hskip 8.19447pt\sigma\in\llbracket\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rrbracket}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!|W_{\sigma}|,

where
\llbracket\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rrbracket
is the unique geodesic that joins
\sigma_{1}
to

\sigma_{2}
in
T_{H}
. In Lemma
4.22
, we prove that
d_{H,W}
is a pseudo-metric on
T_{H}
;
we then define the equivalence relation
\sim_{H,W}
on
T_{H}
by setting
\sigma_{1}\sim_{H,W}\sigma_{2}
if and only if

d_{H,W}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\!=\!0
and we denote by
T_{H,W}\!=\!T_{H}/\!\sim_{H,W}
the quotient metric space and we keep denoting by

d_{H,W}
the resulting metric; we denote by
\pi_{H,W}\!:\!T_{H}\!\rightarrow\!T_{H,W}
the canonical projection that is continuous. Thus
T_{H,W}
is compact and connected, and

(T_{H,W},d_{H,W})
is a real tree (see Section
4.6
, especially Proposition
4.34
, for more properties of
T_{H,W}
).
It turns out that this kind of spaces has been introduced in N. Curien, J.-F. Le Gall and G. Miermont
[
7
]
(see also J.-F. Le Gall
[
22
]
for a different purpose);
they coined the name Brownian cactus, so we call
(T_{H,W},d_{H,W})
the normalised reflected Brownian cactus with
\gamma
-stable branching mechanism.
We next set
r_{H,W}\!=\!\pi_{H,W}(r_{H})
that is viewed as the root of
T_{H,W}

and we equip
T_{H,W}
with the measure
\mu_{H,W}
that is the image
of
\mu_{H}
via
\pi_{H,W}
: namely,
\int_{T_{H,W}}\!f\,d\mu_{H,W}\!=\!\int_{T_{H}}\!f(\pi_{H,W}(\sigma))\,\mu_{H}(d\sigma)
, for all
continuous
f\!:\!T_{H,W}\!\rightarrow\!\mathbb{R}
. Our second result is the following limit theorem.

Theorem 1.2

Let
\tau
be a Galton–Watson tree with offspring distribution
\mu
that satisfies
(\mathbf{H})
as in (
1
). We denote by
\mathtt{m}\!=\!\sum_{v\in\tau}\delta_{v}
the counting measure on
\tau
and we denote by
d_{\mathtt{gr}}
the graph distance on
\tau
.
Conditionally given
\tau
, let
(Y_{v})_{v\in\tau}
be a
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
-valued
\tau
-indexed critical branching random walk starting at
Y_{\mathtt{root}}\!=\!\varnothing
.
We denote by
\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}\!=\!\{Y_{v};v\!\in\!\tau\}
the range of
Y
, by
\mathbf{m}^{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathtt{occ}}\!=\!\sum_{v\in\tau}\delta_{Y_{v}}
the occupation measure of
Y
and by
d_{\mathtt{gr}}
the graph distance on
\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}
.
We denote by
(T_{H},d_{H},r_{h},\mu_{H})
the normalised
\gamma
-stable Lévy tree and by

(T_{H,W},d_{H,W},r_{H,W},\mu_{H,W})
the normalised reflected Brownian cactus with
\gamma
-stable branching mechanism as defined above. Let
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be as in (
2
).
Then, the following limit holds weakly on
(\mathbb{M},\delta_{\mathtt{GHP}})^{2}

\displaystyle\Big(\big(\tau,\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}d_{\mathtt{gr}},\mathtt{root},\tfrac{1}{n}\mathtt{m}\big)\,,\,\big(\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}},\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}d_{\mathtt{gr}},\varnothing,\tfrac{1}{n}\mathbf{m}^{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathtt{occ}}\big)\Big)\;\textrm{under $\;\mathbf{P}\big(\cdot\big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n\big)$ }

(6)

\displaystyle\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}}\;\Big(\big(T_{H},d_{H},r_{H},\mu_{H}\big)\,,\,\big(T_{H,W},d_{H,W},r_{H,W},\mu_{H,W}\big)\Big).

Moreover, denote by

\mathbf{m}^{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathtt{count}}\!=\!\sum_{x\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}}\delta_{x}
the counting measure on
\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}}
; then, for all
\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty)
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{P}\Big(\,d^{{}_{\,(n)}}_{{\mathtt{Prok}}}\big(\tfrac{c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}}{n}\mathbf{m}^{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathtt{occ}},\tfrac{1}{n}\mathbf{m}^{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathtt{count}}\big)\!>\!\varepsilon\,\Big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n\Big)\!=\!0\;,

where
c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}
is as in Theorem
1.1
and where
d^{{}_{\,(n)}}_{{}^{\mathtt{Prok}}}
stands for the Prokhorov distance on the space of finite measures on
(\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}},\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}d_{\mathtt{gr}})
. It implies that the following limit holds jointly with (
6
):

\big(\mathcal{R}_{\mathtt{b}},\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{a_{n}}}d_{\mathtt{gr}},\varnothing,\tfrac{1}{n}\mathbf{m}^{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathtt{count}}\big)\;\textrm{under $\;\mathbf{P}\big(\cdot\big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n\big)$ }\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}}\;\big(T_{H,W},d_{H,W},r_{H,W},c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}.\mu_{H,W}\big).

Theorem
1.1
is the analogue of Proposition 5 and Theorem 7 in J-F. Le Gall and L.
[
25
]
established for
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-valued BRWs. Let us mention that our strategy of proof is similar: we define a specific invariant shift for infinite tree-valued BRWs and we use the subadditive ergodic theorem; the constant
c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}
is interpreted as the probability that the invariant BRW visits its starting point only once.

The proof of Theorem
1.2
is distinct from that of Theorem
1.1
. On one hand, it relies on general arguments on weak limits of random metrics (see Proposition
4.4
). As an application of these results, we prove that the range of critical biased RWs
on
N
-type supercritical GW-trees converges to the tree coded by a reflected Brownian motion (see Corollary
4.16
). This result is derived from a much more difficult
result due to A. Dembo and N. Sun
[
8
]
that asserts that the distance from the root of the RW converges, when suitably rescaled, to a reflected Brownian motion. The same idea allows to recover previous scaling limits for the range of RWs on supercritical GW-trees such as in D.
[
11
]
(critical biased RWs on
\mathtt{b}
-ary trees) or in E. Aïdékon and L. de Raphélis
[
2
]
(biased RWs and RWs in random environment on single-type GW-trees). We refer to the end of Section
4.2
for more details.
On the other hand, the proof of Theorem
1.2
uses limit theorems for discrete snakes that have been obtained by S. Janson and J-F. Marckert in
[
18
]
in the Brownian case and by C. Marzouk
[
29
]
in the stable cases.

Organization of the paper

The paper is organised as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce notations on trees with an infinite line of ancestors that constitute a natural state-space for invariant tree-valued BRWs.
In Section
3.1
, we define the kinds of BRWs that we study; Section
3.2
is devoted to metric properties of the range of the so-called free BRWs. In Section
3.3
, we state a coupling for BRWs that is a key argument in the proof of Theorem
1.1
. In Section
3.4
, we prove estimates that are used mostly to prove that
c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}\!>\!0
. Theorem
1.1
is proved in Sections
3.5
and
3.6
. In Section
4.1
, we prove general convergence results for random metrics. In Section
4.2
we apply these results to get scaling limits for the range of RWs on
N
-type GW-trees (see Corollary
4.16
). In Section
4.3
we introduce snake metrics and we prove specific results.
In Sections
4.4
and
4.5
, we recall definitions and properties on stable Lévy trees and Lévy snakes. Section
4.6
is devoted to basic properties of reflected Brownian cactuses.
Theorem
1.2
is proved in Section
5
.

2
Tree with a possibly infinite line of ancestors.

Words.

Recall that
\mathbb{N}
stands for the set of nonnegative integers
\{0,1,2,\ldots\}

and that
\mathbb{N}^{*}\!=\!\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\}
. Let
A
be a set with more than two elements that is viewed as an alphabet. We denote by

\mathbb{W}_{\!A}
the finite words written with alphabet
A
: namely,

\mathbb{W}_{\!A}\!=\!\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}A^{n}\;.

(7)

Here,
A^{0}
is taken as
\{\varnothing\}
,
\varnothing
being the empty word.
Let
u\!=\!(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\!\in\!\mathbb{W}_{\!A}
be distinct from
\varnothing
. We set
|u|\!=\!n
that is
the
height
of
u
, with the convention that
|\varnothing|\!=\!0
.
We next set
\overleftarrow{u}\!=\!(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})
that is interpreted as the
parent of
u

(if
n\!=1
, then
\overleftarrow{u}\!=\!\varnothing
).
More generally for all
p\!\in\!\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we set

u_{|p}\!=\!(a_{1},\ldots,a_{p})
, with the convention:
u_{|0}\!=\!\varnothing
.
For all
v\!=\!(b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})\!\in\!\mathbb{W}_{\!A}
, we set

u\ast v\!=\!(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})
that is
the
concatenation
of
u
with
v
, with the convention that

\varnothing\ast u\!=\!u\ast\varnothing\!=\!u
. We shall also define the most recent common ancestor of

u
and
v
in
\mathbb{W}_{\!A}
as
u\wedge v\!=\!u_{|p}\!=\!v_{|p}
where
p\!=\!\max\{k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}\,:u_{|k}\!=\!v_{|k}\}
.
We shall consider three cases:

\bullet

A=\mathbb{N}^{*}
; in that case we use the notation
\mathbb{U}\!:=\!\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
,
the letter
U
being for Ulam.

\bullet

A=\{1,\ldots,\mathtt{b}\}
,
\mathtt{b}
being an integer
\geq 2
; we use the notation

\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}\!:=\!\mathbb{W}_{\!\{1,\ldots,\mathtt{b}\}}
;
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
is the
\mathtt{b}
-ary tree
.

\bullet

A=[0,1]
; we call
\mathbb{W}_{[0,1]}
the
free tree
.

Definition 2.1

Rooted ordered trees
can be viewed as subsets
t\!\subset\!\mathbb{U}
that satisfy the following.

(a)

\varnothing\!\in\!t
.

(b)

If
u\!\in\!t\backslash\{\varnothing\}
, then
\overleftarrow{u}\!\in\!t
.

(c)

For all
u\!\in\!t
, there exists
k_{u}(t)\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
such that
u\!\ast\!(i)\!\in\!t

if and only if
1\!\leq\!i\!\leq\!k_{u}(t)
.

We denote by
\mathbb{T}
the set of rooted ordered trees.
\hfill\square

The quantity
k_{u}(t)
is interpreted as the
number of children of
u
and
u\!\ast\!(i)
is the

i
-th child of
u
,
1\!\leq\!i\!\leq\!k_{u}(t)
.
If
k_{u}(t)\!=\!0
, then there is no child stemming from
u
and assertion
(c)
is empty.
We next set the shift of
t
at
u
by
\theta_{u}t\!=\!\{v\!\in\!\mathbb{U}:u\!\ast\!v\!\in\!t\}
that is also a rooted ordered tree: it is viewed as the
subtree of the descendants stemming from
u
.
Unless otherwise specified, all the random variables that are mentioned in this paper are defined on the same probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbf{P})
.

Definition 2.2

We equip
\mathbb{T}
with the sigma-field
\mathscr{F}(\mathbb{T})
generated by the sets
\{t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}\!:\!u\!\in\!t\}
,
u\!\in\!\mathbb{U}
. A
Galton–Watson tree with offspring distribution
\mu
(a
GW(
\mu
)-tree
, for short) is a
(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{F}(\mathbb{T}))
-measurable r.v.
\tau\!:\!\Omega\!\rightarrow\!\mathbb{T}
that satisfies the following.

\bullet

k_{\varnothing}(\tau)
has law
\mu
.

\bullet

For all
k\!\geq\!1
such that
\mu(k)\!>\!0
, the subtrees
\theta_{(1)}\tau,\ldots,\theta_{(k)}\tau
under
\mathbf{P}(\,\cdot\,|k_{\varnothing}(\tau)\!=\!k)
are independent with the same law as
\tau
under
\mathbf{P}
.
\hfill\square

Recall that
\tau
is a.s. finite if and only if
\mu
is critical or subcritical:
\sum_{k\geq 1}k\mu(k)\!\leq\!1
.

Bilateral words.

We shall consider branching random walks seen from the spatial and genealogical position of a tagged individual.
To that end, it is convenient to introduce ordered trees that are rooted possibly
at a negative generation and we also introduce their local limits that may have an infinite line of ancestors.
It is therefore convenient to introduce words indexed by possibly negative numbers.

To simplify, we set
\mathbb{Z}_{-}\!=\!\mathbb{Z}\backslash\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\overleftarrow{\mathbb{Z}}\!=\!\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}
.
An
A
-
bilateral word
is a (possibly infinite) sequence
u\!=\!(a_{k})_{k_{1}<k\leq k_{2}}
where

k_{1}\!\in\!\overleftarrow{\mathbb{Z}}
and
k_{2}\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
are such that
k_{2}\!\geq\!k_{1}
and where

a_{k}\!\in\!A
for all
k_{1}\!<\!k\!\leq\!k_{2}
. We denote by
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!A}
the set of bilateral words, including the empty word denoted by
\varnothing

(if
k_{1}\!\geq\!k_{2}
, then we agree on
u\!=\!\varnothing
).
If
u\neq\varnothing
, we introduce the following notation.

\displaystyle|u|_{-}\!=\!k_{1},\,\text{be the {depth} of $u$,}

(8)

\displaystyle|u|\!=\!k_{2},\,\text{be the {relative height} of $u$ (note that it may take negative values),}

\displaystyle\overleftarrow{u}\!=\!(a_{k})_{k_{1}<k\leq k_{2}-1}\,\text{be the {parent} of $u$,}

\displaystyle u_{|_{(l_{1},l_{2}]}}\!=\!(a_{k})_{k_{1}\vee l_{1}<k\leq k_{2}\wedge l_{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathtt{end}(u)=a_{k_{2}}\,,

for all
l_{1}\!\in\!\overleftarrow{\mathbb{Z}}
,
l_{2}\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
such that
l_{1}\leq l_{2}
. To simplify, we also set

\forall l\!\in\!\mathbb{Z},\quad u_{|l}=u_{|(-\infty,l]}.

(9)

Let us stress that a bilateral word has at most a finite number of letters
a_{k}
indexed by positive indices,
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, while it can have infinitely many letters indexed by negative indices,
k\!\in\!\overleftarrow{\mathbb{Z}}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{-}
. Note that
\varnothing
has neither relative height nor depth. Note that if
l_{1}\!\leq\!k_{1}
and
k_{2}\leq l_{2}
, then
u|_{(l_{1},l_{2}]}\!=\!u
.

\bullet

Shift.
For all
l\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote the
l
-
shift operator

\varphi_{l}\!:\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!A}\!\rightarrow\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!A}
by:

\varphi_{l}(u)=(a_{k+l})_{k_{1}-l<k\leq k_{2}-l},

Note that
|\varphi_{l}(u)|_{-}\!=\!|u|_{-}\!-l
, that
|\varphi_{l}(u)|\!=\!|u|-l
and that

\varphi_{l}(\varnothing)\!=\!\varnothing
. Clearly,
\varphi_{l}\circ\varphi_{l^{\prime}}\!=\!\varphi_{l+l^{\prime}}
,
\varphi_{l}
is bijective and
\varphi_{l}\circ\varphi_{-l}
is the identity map.

\bullet

Concatenation.
For all
u\!=\!(a_{k})_{k_{1}<k\leq k_{2}}\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!A}
and all
v\!=\!(b_{k})_{1\leq k\leq k_{3}}\!\in\!\mathbb{W}_{\!A}
, we define

u\!\ast\!v\!=\!(c_{l})_{k_{1}<k\leq k_{2}+k_{3}}\quad\textrm{where}\quad c_{k}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}a_{k}&\textrm{If $k_{1}\!<\!k\!\leq\!k_{2}$,}\\
b_{k-k_{2}}&\textrm{If $k_{2}\!<\!k\!\leq\!k_{2}+k_{3}$.}\end{array}\right.

(10)

The bilateral word
u\!\ast\!v
is the concatenation of a bilateral word
u
on the left with
a null depth word
v
on the right. Note that
|u\ast v|_{-}\!=\!|u|_{-}
, that

|u\ast v|\!=\!|u|+|v|
and that
u\ast\varnothing\!=\!u
.

\bullet

Convergence in
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!A}
.
Assume that
(A,d_{A})
is a Polish space.
We equip
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!A}
with the following local convergence.

Let
u^{{}_{(p)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!A}
,
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
; the sequence of words
u^{{}_{(p)}}_{{}^{\!}}
converges to
u
if
|u^{{}_{(p)}}_{{}^{\!}}|_{-}\!\rightarrow\!|u|_{-}
in
\overleftarrow{\mathbb{Z}}

and if for all
l\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
and for all
\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,1)
, there exists
p_{l,\varepsilon}\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
such that for all
p\!\geq\!p_{l,\varepsilon}
,
|u^{{}_{(p)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!|\!=\!|u|
,
(-l)\!\vee\!|u^{{}_{(p)}}_{{}^{\!}}\!|_{-}\!=\!(-l)\!\vee\!|u|_{-}\,
and

\,\max_{(\!-l)\vee|u|_{-}\!\leq k\leq|u|}d_{A}\big(u^{{}_{(p)}}_{{}^{k}}\!,u^{{}_{\!}}_{{}^{k}}\big)<\varepsilon
.

It is easy to see that this convergence corresponds to a Polish metric and we equip
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!A}
with the corresponding Borel sigma-field.
Note that the shifts operators
\varphi_{l}
are homeomorphisms with respect to local convergence.
If
A\!=\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we shall use the notation
\overline{\mathbb{U}}\!:=\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
.
If
A\!=\!\{1,\ldots,\mathtt{b}\}
, we shall use the notation
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\mathtt{b}}\!:=\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!\{1,\ldots,\mathtt{b}\}}
. Note that in these cases,
\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\{1,\ldots,\mathtt{b}\}
are equipped with the discrete topology.

Trees with infinite line of ancestors.

Definition 2.3

A non-empty subset
R\!\subset\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!A}
is a subtree of
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!A}
if it satisfies the following.

(a)

There exists
|R|_{-}\!\in\!\overleftarrow{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
|v|_{-}\!=\!|R|_{-}
for all
v\!\in\!R\backslash\{\varnothing\}
.

(b)

For all
v\!\in\!R\backslash\{\varnothing\}

such that
|v|\!>\!|R|_{-}
,
\overleftarrow{v}\!\in\!R
.

(c)

For all
u
and
v
in
R
, there exists
l\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
such that
u_{|l}\!=\!v_{|l}
(see (
9
)).
\hfill\square

We call
|R|_{-}
the
depth of the subtree
R
.
If
|R|_{-}\!>\!-\infty
, then
(b)
implies that the empty word
\varnothing
is an element of
R

and
|R|_{-}+1\!=\!\min_{v\in R\backslash\{\varnothing\}}|v|
,

(c)
is always fulfilled since for all
l\!\leq\!|R|_{-}
and all
v\in R
, we get
v_{|l}\!=\!\varnothing
. If
|R|_{-}\!=\!-\infty
, then
\varnothing\!\notin\!R
.

\bullet

Common ancestor.

Recall from (
9
) the notation
u_{|l}
. Let
R\!\subset\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!A}
be a subtree as in Definition
2.3
. We define the
common ancestor
of

u,v\!\in\!R
by

u\wedge v=\!u_{|b(u,v)}\!\!=v_{|b(u,v)}\quad\textrm{where}\quad b(u,v)\!:=\!\max\{l\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}:\,u_{|l}\!\!=\!v_{|l}\},

(11)

that is well-defined thanks to Definition
2.3

(c)
.

\bullet

Graph distance on subtrees of
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!A}
.

A subtree
R\!\subset\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!A}
as in Definition
2.3
corresponds to the following graph-tree: its set of vertices is
R
and its set of edges is
\{\{v,\overleftarrow{v}\};v\!\in\!R:|v|\!>\!|R|_{-}\}
. We easily observe that the graph distance
d_{\mathtt{gr}}
on
R
is given by

\forall x,y\!\in\!R,\quad d_{\mathtt{gr}}(x,y)=|x|+|y|-2|x\wedge y|\;.

(12)

\bullet

Ordered trees with a possibly infinite line of ancestors.

We next extend Definition
2.1
to ordered trees with a possibly infinite line of ancestors as follows.

Definition 2.4

Recall the notation
\overline{\mathbb{U}}\!:=\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}\!
.
A subset
t\!\subset\!\overline{\mathbb{U}}
is an ordered tree (with a possibly infinite line of ancestors) if it is a subtree of
\overline{\mathbb{U}}
as in Definition
2.3
satisfying

(a)
,
(b)
and
(c)
with
A\!=\!\mathbb{N}^{*}

and if it furthermore satisfies the condition that any word has a finite number of children, that is,

(d)\,

\forall u\!\in\!t
,
k_{u}(t)\!\!:=\!\#\{v\!\in\!t\!:\!\overleftarrow{v}\!=\!u\}\!<\!\infty
and
\{1,\ldots,k_{u}(t)\}\!=\!\{\mathtt{end}(v);v\!\in\!t\!\!:\!\overleftarrow{v}\!=\!u\}
if
k_{u}(t)\!\geq\!1
.

We shall consider that the singleton
\{\varnothing\}
is the only tree with one point.
We denote by
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
the set of ordered trees.
\hfill\square

For all
k\!\in\!\overleftarrow{\mathbb{Z}}
,
we set
\mathbb{T}_{k}\!=\!\{\{\varnothing\}\}\cup\{t\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}:|t|_{-}\!=\!k\}
. Note that
\mathbb{T}_{0}\!=\!\mathbb{T}
, where
\mathbb{T}
is as in Definition
2.1
and observe that
\varphi_{-k}(\mathbb{T})\!=\!\mathbb{T}_{k}
when
k>-\infty
.

\bullet

Lexicographical order and successor of a vertex.
Let
t\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}
. By Definition
2.3

(c)
, the vertices of
t
are totally ordered by the
lexicographical order

\leq_{t}
that is formally defined as follows. Let
u,v\!\in\!t\backslash\{\varnothing\}
; recall from (
11
) the definition of
b(u,v)
and from (
8
) the definition of
\mathtt{end}(\cdot)
; then,

u\leq_{t}v\quad\textrm{{if and only if}}\quad\mathtt{end}(u_{|b(u,v)+1})\leq\mathtt{end}(v_{|b(u,v)+1})\;.

(13)

Note that
\leq_{t}
actually depends on
t
: it is not defined on the whole set of bilateral words
\overline{\mathbb{U}}
but only on
t
(indeed, to define
\leq_{t}
, branching points have to be well-defined, which requires possibly infinite words to share a prefix). If
\varnothing\!\in\!t
, then
\varnothing
is the
\leq_{t}
-least element of
t
. We denote by
<_{t}
the strict order associated with
\leq_{t}
. We also introduce the following related notation: for all
u\!\in\!t
, the
successor

\mathtt{scc}(u)
of
u
is defined as the
\leq_{t}
-least element of
\{v\!\in\!t\!:\!u\!<_{t}\!v\}
if this set is not empty, otherwise we simply take
\mathtt{scc}(u)\!=\!u
.

\bullet

Subtree.
Let
t\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}
and
u\in t
. The subtree
\theta_{u}t
stemming from
u
is defined as follows.

\theta_{u}t\!=\!\{v\!\in\!\mathbb{U}:u\ast v\!\in\!t\},

(14)

where we recall from (
10
) the definition of the concatenation
\ast
of a bilateral word on the left with a null-depth word on the right. Note that
|\theta_{u}t|_{-}\!=\!0
, namely:
\theta_{u}t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
, where
\mathbb{T}
is as in Definition
2.1
.

Pointed labelled trees.

To deal with branching random walks, we introduce labelled trees where the label of a vertex is viewed as its position in space. More precisely, let
(E,d_{E})
be a Polish metric space.
We define the space of
pointed
E
-labelled trees
as follows. For all
k\!\in\!\overleftarrow{\mathbb{Z}}
, we set:

\mathbb{T}^{\bullet}_{k}(E)\!=\!\{\partial\}\!\cup\!\big\{\mathbf{t}\!=\!\big(t,\varrho\,;\mathbf{x}\!=\!(x_{v})_{v\in t}\big)\!:t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}_{k},\,\varrho\!\in\!t,\,x_{v}\!\in\!E,v\!\in\!t\big\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)\!=\!\!\bigcup_{k\in\overleftarrow{\mathbb{Z}}}\!\mathbb{T}^{\bullet}_{k}(E),

where
\partial
stands for a cemetery point.
Here, the label of
v\!\in\!t
is
x_{v}\!\in\!E
that is viewed as the spatial position of
v
.
If there is no label, we simply write
\mathbb{T}^{\bullet}_{k}
and
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
.

\bullet

Shift operator on labelled trees.
Shift operators act naturally on the space of pointed
E
-labelled trees as follows:
let
l\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
; we set
\varphi_{l}(\partial)\!=\!\partial
and for all

\mathbf{t}\!=\!(t,\varrho;\mathbf{x})\!\in\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)\backslash\{\partial\}
,
we set

\varphi_{l}(\mathbf{t})\!=\!(\varphi_{l}(t),\varphi_{l}(\varrho);\mathbf{x}\!\circ\!\varphi_{-l}),\quad\textrm{where}\quad\mathbf{x}\!\circ\!\varphi_{\!-l}\!=\!(x_{\varphi_{\!-l}(w)})_{w\in\varphi_{l}(t)}.

\bullet

Truncation.
We next define a natural truncation procedure for pointed labelled trees along the line of ancestors of the distinguished point.

Definition 2.5

Let
\mathbf{t}\!=\!\big(t,\varrho\,;\mathbf{x}\!=\!(x_{v})_{v\in t}\big)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
.
Let
p\!\in\!\overleftarrow{\mathbb{Z}}
and
q\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}\!\cup\!\{\infty\}
be such that
p\!<\!q
. We define the following.

-

If
(p,q]\cap\big(|\varrho|_{{}_{\!-}},|\varrho|\big]\!=\!\emptyset
, then we set
[\mathbf{t}]^{q}_{p}\!=\!\partial
.
We also set
[\partial]^{q}_{p}\!=\!\partial
.

-

If
(p,q]\cap\big(|\varrho|_{{}_{\!-}},|\varrho|\big]\!\neq\!\emptyset
,
then we set
[\mathbf{t}]_{p}^{q}\!=\!(t^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime};\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

where

\varrho^{\prime}\!=\!\varrho_{|(p,q]},\quad t^{\prime}\!=\!\big\{v_{|(p,q]};\,v\!\in\!t:v_{|p}\!=\!\varrho_{|p}\big\}\;\textrm{and}\;x^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}\!=\!x_{v},

where
v\!\in\!t
is
such that
v^{\prime}\!=\!v_{|(p,q]}
and
v_{|p}\!=\!\varrho_{|p}
(recall notation
v_{|p}
from (
9
)).

We simply set
[\mathbf{t}]_{p}
instead of
[\mathbf{t}]_{p}^{\infty}
. If
\mathbf{t}\!\in\!\mathbb{T}^{\bullet}_{k}(E)
, then note that
[\mathbf{t}]_{p}^{q}\!\in\!\mathbb{T}^{\bullet}_{k\vee p}(E)
. We use a similar notation for pointed trees without label.
\hfill\square

\bullet

Local convergence on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
.

For all
\mathbf{t}\!=\!(t,\varrho;\mathbf{x})
,
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\!=\!(t^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime};\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
in

\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
, we first set

\Delta(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{t}^{\prime})\!=\!{\bf 1}_{\{(t,\varrho)\neq(t^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime})\}}+{\bf 1}_{\{(t,\varrho)=(t^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime})\}}\max_{v\in t}\big(1\!\wedge\!d_{E}(x_{v},x^{\prime}_{v})\big)

with
\Delta(\mathbf{t},\partial)\!=\!1
and
\Delta(\partial,\partial)\!=\!0
. We easily check that
\Delta
is a metric on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
. Then, we define the local convergence as follows.

Let
\mathbf{t}_{n}\!=\!(t^{(n)}\!,\varrho^{(n)};\mathbf{x}^{(n)})\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
; the sequence
(\mathbf{t}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is said to converge locally to
\mathbf{t}\!=\!(t,\varrho;\mathbf{x})\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
if for all
\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,1)
and all
q\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, there exists

n_{q,\varepsilon}\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
such that for all integers
n\!\geq\!n_{q,\varepsilon}
,

\Delta\big([\mathbf{t}^{(n)}]_{-q}^{q},[\mathbf{t}]_{-q}^{q}\big)\!<\!\varepsilon
(when there is no label, it simply means that
[\mathbf{t}^{(n)}]_{-q}^{q}\!=\![\mathbf{t}]_{-q}^{q}\,
).

Local convergence corresponds for instance to the following metric:

\forall\mathbf{t},\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\!\in\!\mathbb{T}^{\bullet}\!(E),\quad{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{loc}}\big(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\big)=\sum_{q\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-q-1}\Delta\big([\mathbf{t}]_{-q}^{q},[\mathbf{t}^{\prime}]_{-q}^{q}\big),

with
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{loc}}(\mathbf{t},\partial)\!=\!1
and
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{loc}}(\partial,\partial)\!=\!0
. We easily check that
(\mathbb{T}^{\bullet}\!(E),{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{loc}})
is Polish and we note that shift operators are isometries.

Definition 2.6

Let
(t,\varrho)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
be distinct from
\partial
.

(a)

We set
\mathtt{cent}(t,\varrho)\!=\!\varphi_{|\varrho|}(t,\varrho)
that is the

centering
map: it shifts trees so that their distinguished point is at relative height
0
.

(b)

We next set
\mathtt{scc}(t,\varrho)\!=\!\mathtt{cent}(t,\mathtt{scc}(\varrho))
where we recall that

\mathtt{scc}(\varrho)
stands for the vertex of
t
coming next in the lexicographical order as defined by (
13
).
We call
\mathtt{scc}(\cdot)
the
successor map
.

(c)

Observe that there is a unique pointed tree
(t^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime})\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
and a unique one-to-one map
\psi\!:\!t^{\prime}\!\rightarrow\!\{\varrho_{|p};p\!\leq\!|\varrho|\}\cup\{v\!\in\!t\!:\!\varrho\!<_{t}\!v\}
such that
\psi(\varrho^{\prime})\!=\!\varrho
, that is increasing with respect to the lexicographical order and
that preserves the relative height; we set
[(t,\varrho)]^{+}\!=\!(t^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime})

that is called the
right-part
of
(t,\varrho)
. Intuitively, the difference between
(t^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
and
\{\varrho_{|p};p\!\leq\!|\varrho|\}\cup\{v\!\in\!t\!:\!\varrho\!<_{t}\!v\}
is that
(t^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
respects the convention
(c)
in Definition
2.1
we have imposed on rooted ordered trees. We also set

\mathtt{scc}^{+}(t,\varrho)\!=\![\mathtt{scc}(t,\varrho)]^{+}\;.

The map
\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\cdot)
is called the
right-successor
.

By convenience, we set
\mathtt{cent}(\partial)\!=\!\mathtt{scc}(\partial)\!=\!\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\partial)\!=\!\partial
.
\hfill\square

Note that

[\mathtt{cent}(t,\varrho)]^{+}\!=\!\mathtt{cent}\big([(t,\varrho)]^{+}\big)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathtt{scc}^{+}(t,\varrho)\!=\!\mathtt{scc}^{+}\!\big([(t,\varrho)]^{+}\big)\;.

(15)

Let us state a technical result about the continuity of the maps
\mathtt{cent},[\cdot]^{+},\mathtt{scc}
and
\mathtt{scc}^{+}
.

Lemma 2.7

The maps
\mathtt{cent}(\cdot)
and
[\,\cdot\,]^{+}
are
locally continuous and the maps
\mathtt{scc}(\cdot)
and
\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\cdot)
are locally continuous at the pointed trees
(t,\varrho)
such that
\mathtt{scc}(\varrho)\!\neq\!\varrho
.

Proof.
Since shift-operators are
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{loc}}
-isometries,
the continuity of
\mathtt{cent}
follows from the continuity of

(t,\varrho)\!\rightarrow\!|\varrho|
that is a direct consequence of the definition of local convergence;
[\,\cdot\,]^{+}
is locally continuous because
[[\mathbf{t}]_{-q}^{q}]^{+}\!\!=\![[\mathbf{t}]^{+}]_{-q}^{q}
and to complete the proof, it is then sufficient to prove that
(t,\varrho)\!\mapsto\!(t,\mathtt{scc}(\varrho))
is locally continuous at trees such that
\mathtt{scc}(\varrho)\!\neq\!\varrho
.

To that end, let
(t_{n},\varrho_{n})\!\rightarrow\!(t,\varrho)
locally in
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!\!
. Set
p_{0}\!=\!|\mathtt{scc}(\varrho)|
and suppose that
\varrho\!\neq\!\mathtt{scc}(\varrho)
, which implies
|\varrho\!\wedge\!\mathtt{scc}(\varrho)|\!=\!p_{0}\!-\!1
. Let
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
be such that

-p\!<\!p_{0}\!-\!1\!<\!|\varrho|+1\!<\!p
; there is
n_{p}\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\!\geq\!n_{p}
,
(t^{\prime}_{n},\varrho_{n}^{\prime})\!:=\![(t_{n},\varrho_{n})]_{{}^{-p}}^{{}_{p}}
is equal to
(t^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime})\!:=\![(t,\varrho)]_{{}^{-p}}^{{}_{p}}
; thus, the successor of
\varrho^{\prime}_{n}
in

t^{\prime}_{n}
is equal to the successor of
\varrho^{\prime}
in
t^{\prime}
. Consequently,

[(t_{n},\mathtt{scc}(\varrho_{n}))]_{{}^{-p}}^{{}_{p}}\!=\!(t^{\prime}_{n},\mathtt{scc}(\varrho_{n}^{\prime}))\!=\!(t^{\prime},\mathtt{scc}(\varrho^{\prime}))\!=\![(t,\mathtt{scc}(\varrho))]_{{}^{-p}}^{{}_{p}}
, which entails the desired result.
\hfill\blacksquare

Infinite pointed Galton–Watson trees.

For all
t\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}
and for all
u\!\in\!t
, recall from (
14
) the definition of the subtree
\theta_{u}t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
.

Definition 2.8

Let
(r(j,k))_{k\geq j\geq 1}
be a probability measure
on the octant
\{(j,k)\!\in\!(\mathbb{N}^{*})^{2}\!:\!j\!\leq\!k\}
.
Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\!=\!(\tau^{*}\!,\varrho)\!:\!\Omega\!\rightarrow\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!
be a
Borel-measurable random pointed tree such that a.s.
|\varrho|\!=\!0
and
|\tau^{*}|_{-}\!\!=\!-\infty
. We introduce the following notation.

\forall p\!\in\!\mathbb{N},\quad\varrho(p)=\varrho_{|\,]-\infty,-p]},\quad\mathtt{Sp}\!=\!\{\varrho(p)\,;p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}\}\;\,\textrm{and}\;\,\partial\mathtt{Sp}\!=\!\big\{u\!\in\!\tau^{*}\backslash\mathtt{Sp}:\overleftarrow{u}\!\in\!\mathtt{Sp}\big\}.

Then,
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}
is an
infinite pointed Galton–Watson tree with offspring distribution
\mu
and dispatching measure
r
if the
r.v.
S\!:=\!\big(\mathtt{end}(\varrho(p)),k_{\varrho(p+1)}(\tau^{*})\big)_{\!p\in\mathbb{N}}
are i.i.d. with law
r
and if conditionally given
S
,
the subtrees
(\theta_{u}\tau\,;\,u\!\in\!\partial\mathtt{Sp})
are independent GW(
\mu
)-trees.

We shall deal with the following special cases that are well-defined if
m_{\mu}\!:=\!\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\mu(k)\!<\!\infty
.

(i)

If
r(j,k)\!=\!\mu(k)/m_{\mu}
, for all
k\!\geq\!j\!\geq\!1
, then we say that
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}
is an
infinite pointed GW(
\mu
)-tree
(an IPGW(
\mu
)-tree for short).

(ii)

If
r(j,k)\!=\!{\bf 1}_{\{j=1\}}\overline{\mu}(k)
, for all
k\!\geq\!j\!\geq\!1
,
where
\overline{\mu}(k)\!=\!m_{\mu}^{-1}\!\sum_{l\geq k}\mu(l)
for all
k\!\geq\!1
, then
we say that
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}
is the
right part of an infinite pointed GW(
\mu
)-tree
(an IPGW
+
(
\mu
)-tree for short).

Note that if
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}
is an IPGW(
\mu
)-tree, then
[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}]^{+}\!
is an IPGW
+
(
\mu
)-tree.
\hfill\square

IPGW-trees are related to GW-trees via the
many-to-one
principle (or the
one-point decomposition of GW-trees
) that asserts
the following: let
\mu
be a probability distribution on
\mathbb{N}
such that
m_{\mu}\!:=\!\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\mu(k)\!<\!\infty
.
Let
\tau
be a GW(
\mu
)-tree and let
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}
be an IPGW(
\mu
)-tree as in Definition
2.8
. Then for all Borel-measurable functions
F\!:\!\mathbb{N}\times\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!\rightarrow\![0,\infty)
,

\mathbf{E}\Big[\sum_{v\in\tau}F\big(|v|;\varphi_{|v|}(\tau,v)\big)\Big]=\sum_{p\geq 0}m_{\mu}^{p}\,\mathbf{E}\big[F(p\,;[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}]_{-p})\big]\;,

(16)

where we recall from SLDefinition
2.5
the notation
[\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}]_{-p}
for the pointed
tree
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}
truncated above the ancestor of
\varrho
at generation
-p
.
Based on this identity, the following proposition
shows that IPGW trees are local limits of critical GW-trees conditioned to be large and seen from a uniformly chosen vertex. This result is part of the folklore; its proof derives from (
16
) and it is left to the reader.

Proposition 2.9

Let
\mu
be a probability distribution on
\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\mu(k)\!=\!1
. We assume that
\mu
is aperiodic.
Let
\tau
be a GW(
\mu
)-tree; let
\mathbf{u}
be uniformly distributed on the set of vertices of
\tau
. Let
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}
be an IPGW(
\mu
)-tree as in Definition
2.8
.
Then

\mathtt{cent}(\tau,\mathbf{u})\quad\textrm{under}\quad\mathbf{P}(\,\cdot\,|\,\#\tau\!=\!n)\;\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\;\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}

weakly on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
with respect to local convergence.

We use the previous proposition to prove the following one.

Proposition 2.10

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\mu(k)\!=\!1
. Recall
\mathtt{scc}(\cdot)

and
\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\cdot)
from Definition
2.6
. Then, the law of IPGW(
\mu
)-trees (resp. IPGW
+
(
\mu
)-trees) is preserved by
\mathtt{scc}(\cdot)
(resp. by
\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\cdot)
).

Proof.
Let us first mention that a different proof of the result for
\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\cdot)
is given in Proposition 2 in Le Gall and L.
[
25
]
. Then, note that the result for
\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\cdot)
is implied by the result for
\mathtt{scc}(\cdot)
by (
15
) and since the right-part of a IPGW(
\mu
)-tree is an IPGW
+
(
\mu
)-tree.
Let us prove the result for
\mathtt{scc}(\cdot)
. Let
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\!=\!(\tau^{*}\!,\varrho)
be an IPGW(
\mu
)-tree and let
\tau
be a GW(
\mu
)-tree; let
\mathbf{u}
be uniformly distributed on the set
of vertices of
\tau
; denote by
v_{*}
the last vertex of
\tau
with respect to the lexicographical order. Set
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\!=\!\mathtt{scc}(\mathbf{u})
if
\mathbf{u}\!\neq\!v_{*}
and
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\!=\!\varnothing
if
\mathbf{u}\!=\!v_{*}
: clearly,
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
is uniformly distributed on
\tau
and
\mathbf{P}(\mathtt{scc}(\mathbf{u})\!\neq\!\mathbf{u}^{\prime}|\,\#\tau\!=\!n)\!=\!1/n
.
Let
F\!:\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!\rightarrow\![0,\infty)
be locally continuous and bounded. By Definition
2.6

(b)
,

\mathtt{scc}(\tau,\mathbf{u})\!=\!\mathtt{cent}(\tau,\mathtt{scc}(\mathbf{u}))
. Thus,

\big|\,\mathbf{E}[F(\mathtt{scc}(\tau,\mathbf{u}))|\#\tau\!=\!n\big]\!-\!\mathbf{E}\big[F(\mathtt{cent}(\tau,\mathbf{u}^{\prime}))|\#\tau\!=\!n\big]\,\big|\!\leq\!2\lVert F\rVert_{\infty}/n.

First suppose that
\mu
is aperiodic.
Then the previous inequality and Proposition
2.9
imply that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{E}\big[F(\mathtt{scc}(\tau,\mathbf{u}))|\#\tau\!=\!n\big]\!=\!\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{E}\big[F(\mathtt{cent}(\tau,\mathbf{u}^{\prime}))|\#\tau\!=\!n\big]\!=\!\mathbf{E}[F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})].

Moreover, since it is clear that a.s.
\mathtt{scc}(\varrho)\!\neq\!\varrho
,
Lemma
2.7
and Proposition
2.9
entail

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{E}\big[F(\mathtt{scc}(\tau,\mathbf{u}))|\#\tau\!=\!n\big]\!=\!\mathbf{E}[F(\mathtt{scc}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}))],

which completes the proof when
\mu
is aperiodic.

Let us consider a general
\mu
. For all
\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,1)
, we set
\mu_{\varepsilon}\!:=\!\varepsilon\delta_{1}+(1\!-\!\varepsilon)\mu
. Namely,
\mu_{\varepsilon}
is a critical aperiodic offspring distribution. Let
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}_{\varepsilon}
be an infinite GW(
\mu_{\varepsilon}
)-tree. We easily check that
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}_{\varepsilon}\!\rightarrow\!\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}
locally as
\varepsilon\!\rightarrow\!0
. The local continuity of
\mathtt{scc}(\cdot)
(Lemma
2.7
) entails the desired result.
\hfill\blacksquare

3
Tree-valued branching random walks.

3.1
Definitions.

Let
E
be a (Polish) space of labels. Let
(q(y,dy^{\prime}))_{y\in E}
be a transition kernel and
let
\varpi
be a Borel probability measure on
E
.
For all pointed tree
\mathbf{t}\!=\!(t,u)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!\!
, we define the law
Q_{\varpi,\mathbf{t}}
on
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
of the
q
-branching random walk with genealogical tree
t
and such that
\varpi
is the law of the spatial position of the distinguished individual
u
.

To that end, we first assume that
t
is finite and we introduce the following notation: for any

v,w\!\in\!t
, we denote by
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
the set of vertices on the shortest path joining
v
to
w
in the tree
t
. If
w\!\neq\!v
, then we denote by
\overleftarrow{w}^{v}
the unique
v^{\prime}\!\in\!\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
that is at graph-distance
1
from
w
;
we call
\overleftarrow{w}^{v}
the
v
-parent of
w
.

Then, the r.v.
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\!=\!(t,u;(Y_{v})_{v\in\tau})\!:\Omega\!\rightarrow\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)
has law

Q_{\varpi,\mathbf{t}}
if

\textrm{
the joint law of $(Y_{v})_{v\in t}$ is}\quad\,\varpi(dy_{u})\!\!\!\prod_{v\in t\backslash\{u\}}\!\!\!q(y_{\overleftarrow{v}^{u}},dy_{v}),

(17)

The definition (
17
) can be extended to the case in which
\mathbf{t}
is infinite. For such purpose it is enough to note that

[\boldsymbol{\Theta}]_{p}^{q}
has law
Q_{{\varpi},{[(t,\varrho)]_{p}^{q}}}

(see Definition
2.5
for the truncation
[\cdot]_{p}^{q}
).

Let us note that

\mathbf{t}_{n}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}\mathbf{t}\;\,\textrm{locally in $\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}$}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad Q_{\varpi,\mathbf{t}_{n}}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}Q_{\varpi,\mathbf{t}}\;\,\textrm{weakly on $\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(E)$.}

When
\varpi\!=\!\delta_{y}
for some
y\!\in\!E
, we simply write

Q_{y,\mathbf{t}}
instead of
Q_{\delta_{y},\mathbf{t}}
. Since
y\mapsto q(y,A)
is Borel-measurable for all Borel subsets
A
of
E
, it is easy to check that
y\mapsto Q_{y,\mathbf{t}}

is also Borel measurable and that
Q_{\varpi,\mathbf{t}}\!=\!\int_{E}\!\varpi(dy)\,Q_{y,\mathbf{t}}
.

As an immediate consequence of the definition, we also get the following: fix
l\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
and set
(t^{\prime},u^{\prime})\!=\!(\varphi_{l}(t),\varphi_{l}(u))
.

If
\boldsymbol{\Theta}=(t,u;(Y_{v})_{v\in t})
has law
Q_{\varpi,(t,u)}
,
then
\big(t^{\prime},u^{\prime};(Y_{\varphi_{-l}(w)})_{w\in t^{\prime}}\big)
has law
Q_{\varpi,(t^{\prime},u^{\prime})}
.

(18)

Definition 3.1

We fix
\mathbf{t}\!=\!(t,u)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
. We shall consider mostly the four following cases.

(i)

E\!=\!\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
, the
\mathtt{b}
-ary tree equipped with the local convergence;
\varpi\!=\!\delta_{\varnothing}
and
q(x,dy)\!=\!p^{+}_{\mathtt{b}}(x,dy)
where for all measurable
f:\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}\!\rightarrow\![0,\infty)
,

\int_{\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}}\!\!\!\!p_{\mathtt{b}}^{+}(x,dy)\,f(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\tfrac{1}{2}f(\overleftarrow{x})+\tfrac{1}{2\mathtt{b}}\sum_{1\leq i\leq\mathtt{b}}\,f\big(x\!\ast\!(i)\big)&\textrm{if $x\!\neq\!\varnothing$ }\\
\tfrac{1}{\mathtt{b}}\sum_{1\leq i\leq\mathtt{b}}f((i))&\textrm{if $x\!=\!\varnothing$.}\end{array}\right.

We denote by
Q^{{}_{+\mathtt{b}}}_{{}^{\mathbf{t}}}
the law of the
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
-valued branching random walk with transition kernel
p_{{}^{\mathtt{b}}}^{{}_{+}}
and with "initial" position
\varnothing
in
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
.

(ii)

E\!=\!\mathbb{W}_{[0,1]}
, the free tree equipped with the local convergence;
\varpi\!=\!\delta_{\varnothing}
and
q(x,dy)\!=\!p^{+}(x,dy)
where for all measurable
f:\mathbb{W}_{[0,1]}\!\rightarrow\![0,\infty)
,

\int_{\mathbb{W}_{[0,1]}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!p^{+}(x,dy)\,f(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\tfrac{1}{2}f(\overleftarrow{x})+\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\!ds\,f\big(x\!\ast\!(s)\big)&\textrm{if $x\!\neq\!\varnothing$ }\\
\int_{0}^{1}\!ds\,f\big((s)\big)&\textrm{if $x\!=\!\varnothing$.}\end{array}\right.

We denote by
Q^{{}_{+}}_{{}^{\mathbf{t}}}
the law of the
\mathbb{W}_{\![0,1]}
-valued branching random walk with transition kernel
p^{+}
and with "initial" position
\varnothing
in
\mathbb{W}_{\![0,1]}
.

(iii)

E\!=\!\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}\!:=\!\{x\!\in\!{\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\mathtt{b}}}:|x|_{\!-}\!=\!-\infty\}
, equipped with the local convergence; we fix
\mathrm{o}\!\in\!\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
and we take

\varpi=\delta_{\mathrm{o}}
and

q(x,dy)\!=\!p_{\mathtt{b}}(x,dy)
where for all
x\!\in\!{\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}}
and for all measurable
f:\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}\!\rightarrow\![0,\infty)
,

\int_{\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}}\!\!\!\!p_{\mathtt{b}}(x,dy)\,f(y)=\tfrac{1}{2}f(\overleftarrow{x})+\tfrac{1}{2\mathtt{b}}\sum_{1\leq i\leq\mathtt{b}}\,f\big(x\!\ast\!(i)\big)\;.

(19)

We denote by
Q^{{}_{\mathtt{b}}}_{{}^{\mathrm{o},\mathbf{t}}}
the law of the
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
-valued branching random walk with transition kernel
p_{\mathtt{b}}
and with "initial" position
\mathrm{o}
in
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
.

(iv)

E\!=\!\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\![0,1]}\!:=\{x\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\![0,1]}:|x|_{\!-}\!=\!-\infty\}
, equipped with the local convergence; we fix
\mathrm{o}\!\in\!\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\![0,1]}
; we take

\varpi\!=\!\delta_{\mathrm{o}}
and

q(x,dy)\!=\!p(x,dy)
, where for all
x\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{[0,1]}
and for all measurable
f:\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\![0,1]}\!\rightarrow\![0,\infty)
,

\int_{\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\![0,1]}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!p(x,dy)\,f(y)=\tfrac{1}{2}f(\overleftarrow{x})+\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\!ds\,f\big(x\!\ast\!(s)\big).

We denote by
Q^{{}_{\!}}_{{}^{\mathrm{o},\mathbf{t}}}
the law of the
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\![0,1]}
-valued branching random walk with transition kernel
p
and with "initial" position
\mathrm{o}
in
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\![0,1]}
: we shall refer to this branching random walk as the
free branching random walk
.
\hfill\square

Remark 3.2

Note that if
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\!=\!(t,u;(X_{v})_{v\in t})
has law
Q^{{}_{\mathtt{b}}}_{{}^{\mathrm{o},\mathbf{t}}}

(or
Q^{{}_{\!}}_{{}^{\mathrm{o},\mathbf{t}}}
) then
|\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\!:=\!(t,u;(|X_{v}|)_{v\in t})
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-valued branching random walk whose spatial motion is that of the simple symmetric
random walk. Similarly, if
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
has law
Q^{{}_{+\mathtt{b}}}_{{}^{\mathbf{t}}}
(or
Q^{{}_{+}}_{{}^{\mathbf{t}}}
), then
|\boldsymbol{\Theta}|
is an
\mathbb{N}
-valued branching random walk whose spatial motion is that of the simple symmetric
random walk reflected at
0
.
\hfill\square

Definition 3.3

We define the
\mathtt{b}
-contraction map

\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}\!:\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{[0,1]}\!\rightarrow\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
as follows. For all
r\!\in\!(0,\infty)
, we set

\lceil r\rceil\!=\!\min\{k\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}\colon r\!\leq\!k\}
and by convenience we take
\lceil 0\rceil\!=\!1
.
Then, for all

x\!=\!(a_{k})_{|x|_{-}<k\leq|x|}\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{[0,1]}
, we define

\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(x)=\big(\lceil\mathtt{b}a_{k}\rceil)\big)_{|x|_{-}<k\leq|x|}\in\,\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!\mathtt{b}}\;.

The
\mathtt{b}
-contraction map is measurable and preserves the depth and the relative height of words.
\hfill\square

Remark 3.4

Note that
\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}
transforms respectively the kernel
p(x,dy)
into
p_{\mathtt{b}}(x,dy)
and the kernel
p^{+}(x,dy)
into
p_{\mathtt{b}}^{+}(x,dy)
.
It naturally extends to
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{[0,1]}
-labelled pointed trees as follows: if
\Theta\!=\!(t,\varrho;(x_{v})_{v\in t})\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{[0,1]})
, we set
\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\Theta)\!=\!(t,\varrho;(\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(x_{v}))_{v\in t})
. Clearly,
\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\Theta)
is a
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
-labelled pointed tree and we easily check that the map is measurable.
Moreover, if
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\!=\!(t,\varrho;(X_{v})_{v\in t})
has law
Q^{{}_{+}}_{{}^{\mathbf{t}}}
(resp.
Q^{{}_{\!}}_{{}^{\mathrm{o},\mathbf{t}}}
) then

\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})
has law
Q^{{}_{+\mathtt{b}}}_{{}^{\mathbf{t}}}
(resp.
Q^{{}_{\mathtt{b}}}_{{}^{\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\mathrm{o}),\mathbf{t}}}
).
\hfill\square

3.2
Metric properties of the range of free branching random walks.

We gather basic facts
about the range of a free branching random walk in terms of the heights of the spatial positions in
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\![0,1]}
. Let
\mathbf{t}\!=\!(t,u)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
such that
|t|_{-}\!=\!-\infty
.
For all
w\!\in\!t
, we recall that
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
is the shortest path (with respect to the graph-distance) that joins vertex
v
to vertex
w
. Moreover, we set
\llbracket v,w\llbracket\,=\!\llbracket v,w\rrbracket\backslash\{w\!\}
,

\rrbracket v,w\rrbracket\!=\!\llbracket v,w\rrbracket\backslash\{v\!\}
and
\rrbracket v,w\llbracket=\!\llbracket v,v\rrbracket\backslash\{v,w\!\}
; we also denote by
\rrbracket\!-\!\infty,v\rrbracket
the lineage of
v
: namely,

\rrbracket\!-\!\infty,v\rrbracket\!=\!\{v_{|l}\,;l\!\leq\!|v|\}
.

We next decompose a free branching random walk by first describing the heights of the vertices as a
\mathbb{Z}
-valued branching random walk
(\mathbf{h}_{v})_{v\in t}
and then explaining how to embed

(\mathbf{h}_{v})_{v\in t}
randomly in
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{[0,1]}
.
More specifically, for all
v\!\in\!t
, let
\mathbf{h}_{v}\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
be such that

\forall v\!\in\!t,\quad|\mathbf{h}_{v}\!-\!\mathbf{h}_{\overleftarrow{v}}|=1\;\quad\textrm{and}\quad\inf_{\;\,w\in\llbracket v,u\rrbracket}\!\!\!\!\mathbf{h}_{w}\;\underset{{}^{|v|\rightarrow-\infty}}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}-\infty.

(20)

Let
(U_{v})_{v\in t}
be a family of independent r.v. that are uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
. With
v\!\in\!t
, we associate a spatial position in
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\![0,1]}
as follows. Since
\mathbf{h}
takes arbitrary negative
values on the lineage of
v
, for all integers
k\!\leq\!\mathbf{h}_{v}
,

\textrm{there is a unique $v(k)\!\in\,\rrbracket\!-\!\infty,v\rrbracket$ such that}\quad\mathbf{h}_{v(k)}\!=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\min_{\hskip 8.19447ptw\in\llbracket v(k),v\rrbracket}\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathbf{h}_{w}=\,k\,>\mathbf{h}_{\overleftarrow{v(k)}}\;.

(21)

Namely,
\{\overleftarrow{v(k)};k\!\leq\!\mathbf{h}_{v}\}
is the set of vertices in the lineage of
v
where
\mathbf{h}
reaches a new infimum. In particular, note that
v\!=\!v(\mathbf{h}_{v})
. Then, we set

\forall v\!\in\!t,\quad X_{v}=(U_{v(k)})_{k\leq\mathbf{h}_{v}}\;.

(22)

By construction
|X_{v}|\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{v}
,
v\!\in\!t
.
Then,
(t,u;(X_{v})_{v\in t})
is a
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\![0,1]}
-valued branching random walk satisfying

X_{v}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\!\!\!X_{\overleftarrow{v}}\ast(U_{v})&\textrm{if $\;|X_{v}|\!-\!|X_{\overleftarrow{v}}|\!=\!1$,}\\
\quad\overleftarrow{X_{\overleftarrow{v}}}&\textrm{if $\;|X_{v}|\!-\!|X_{\overleftarrow{v}}|\!=\!-1$.}\end{array}\right.

We view
(X_{v})_{v\in t}
as a version of free branching random walk conditionally given the relative heights
(\mathbf{h}_{v})_{v\in t}
of the spatial positions.
The following proposition is a key point to analyse the metric of the range of free branching random walks.

Proposition 3.5

Let
\mathbf{t}\!=\!(t,u)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
,
(\mathbf{h}_{v},U_{v},X_{v})_{v\in t}
be as above.
Then,
\{X_{v};v\!\in\!t\}
is a subtree of
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\![0,1]}
as in Definition
2.3
and if we denote by
d_{\mathtt{gr}}
it graph-distance, we get

\textrm{$\mathbf{P}$-a.s.\penalty\ for all $v,w\!\in\!t$,}\qquad d_{\mathtt{gr}}(X_{v},X_{w})=|X_{v}|+|X_{w}|-2\!\!\!\!\min_{\;\,v^{\prime}\in\llbracket v,w\rrbracket}\!\!\!\!|X_{v^{\prime}}|\;.

(23)

Proof.
We fix
v,w\!\in\!t
. By
(
12
) we known that
d_{\mathtt{gr}}(X_{v},X_{w})\!=\!|X_{v}|+|X_{w}|\!-\!2|X_{v}\wedge X_{w}|
. Since
t
is countable, to prove (
23
) it is enough to show that a.s.
|X_{v}\wedge X_{w}|\!=\!\min_{v^{\prime}\in\llbracket v,w\rrbracket}|\mathbf{h}_{v^{\prime}}|
. We prove it in two steps.

Step 1.
Recall from (
21
) the definition of the
v(k)
,
k\!\leq\!\mathbf{h}_{v}
and define similarly the
w(k)
,
k\!\leq\!\mathbf{h}_{w}
. We then set

k=\max\big\{\ell\!\leq\!\mathbf{h}_{v}:v(\ell)\!\in\,\rrbracket\!-\!\infty,v\!\wedge\!w\rrbracket\big\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad m=\max\big\{\ell\!\leq\!\mathbf{h}_{w}:w(\ell)\!\in\,\rrbracket\!-\!\infty,v\!\wedge\!w\rrbracket\big\}.

Let us prove that
k\!=\!\min_{\llbracket v,v\wedge w\rrbracket}\mathbf{h}
.
If
k\!<\!\mathbf{h}_{v}
, then
v(k+1)\!\in\,\rrbracket v\wedge w,v\rrbracket
and
\overleftarrow{v}(k+1)\!\in\!\llbracket v\wedge w,v\rrbracket
; then
\mathbf{h}_{\overleftarrow{v}(k+1)}\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{v(k+1)}\!-\!1=\!k\!=\!\min_{\llbracket v,v\wedge w\rrbracket}\mathbf{h}
.
If
k\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{v}
, then, by (
21
),
\mathbf{h}_{v}\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{v(k)}\!=\!\min_{\llbracket v,v(k)\rrbracket}\mathbf{h}
and since
v(k)\!\in\rrbracket-\infty,v\wedge w\rrbracket
, we also get
k\!=\!\min_{\llbracket v,v\wedge w\rrbracket}\mathbf{h}
. Similarly, we get
m\!=\!\min_{\llbracket w,v\wedge w\rrbracket}\mathbf{h}
. By definition (
22
),

X_{v}=X_{v(k)}\ast\big(U_{v(k+1)},\ldots,U_{v(\mathbf{h}_{v})}\big)\quad\textrm{and}\quad X_{w}=X_{w(m)}\ast\big(U_{w(m+1)},\ldots,U_{w(\mathbf{h}_{w})}\big)

(24)

with the observation that
X_{v}\!=\!X_{v(k)}
(resp.
X_{w}\!=\!X_{w(m)}
) if
k\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{v}
(resp. if
m\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{w}
). Without loss of generality, we can assume that
k\!\leq\!m
. Then,
v(k\!-\!i)\!=\!w(k\!-\!i)
, for all
i\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
and

X_{w(m)}=X_{v(k)}\ast\big(U_{w(k+1)},\ldots,U_{w(m)}\big)\;.

(25)

Step 2.
We conclude the proof by proving that

X_{v}\wedge X_{w}\!=\!X_{v(k)}\;.

(26)

Suppose first that
k\!<\!m
and that
k\!<\!\mathbf{h}_{v}
. Then
w(k+1)\!\in\,\rrbracket\!-\!\infty,v\wedge w\rrbracket
but
v(k+1)\!\in\,\rrbracket v\wedge w,v\rrbracket
. Thus,
w(k+1)\!\neq\!v(k+1)
and since the
(U_{v^{\prime}})_{v^{\prime}\in t}
are independent with a diffuse law, a.s.
U_{w(k+1)}\!\neq\!U_{v(k+1)}
. By (
24
) and (
25
), we get (
26
).
If
k\!<\!m
and
k\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{v}
, then
X_{v}\!=\!X_{v(k)}
and we immediately get (
26
) by (
24
) and (
25
).

Next, suppose that
k\!=\!m
. Thus
X_{w(m)}\!=\!X_{v(k)}
by (
25
); if
k\!=\!m\!<\!\mathbf{h}_{v}\wedge\mathbf{h}_{w}
, then
v(k+1)\!\in\,\rrbracket v\wedge w,v\llbracket
and
w(k+1)\!=\!w(m+1)\!\in\,\rrbracket v\wedge w,w\rrbracket
; therefore
w(k+1)\!\neq\!v(k+1)
, which implies that a.s.
U_{w(k+1)}\!\neq\!U_{v(k+1)}
and (
26
) consequently.
If
k\!=\!m
and
k\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{v}
(resp.
m\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{w}
), then
X_{v}\!=\!X_{v(k)}\!=\!X_{w(m)}
(resp.
X_{w}\!=\!X_{w(m)}\!=\!X_{v(k)}
) and (
24
) and (
25
) also entail (
26
).
This completes the proof of (
26
).
\hfill\blacksquare

We shall use Proposition
3.5
under the following form.

Corollary 3.6

Let
t^{\prime}\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
be a finite rooted ordered tree. Let
(t^{\prime},\varnothing;(Y_{v})_{v\in t^{\prime}})
be a
\mathbb{W}_{\![0,1]}
-valued branching random walk with law
Q^{+}_{\mathbf{t}^{\prime}}
as in Definition
3.1

(ii)
. Then, a.s. for all
v,w\!\in\!t^{\prime}
, we get
d_{\mathtt{gr}}(Y_{v},Y_{w})\!=\!|Y_{v}|+|Y_{w}|\!-\!2\min_{\;\,v^{\prime}\in\llbracket v,w\rrbracket}|Y_{v^{\prime}}|
.

Proof.
For all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}_{-}
we set
\varrho_{k}\!=\!(a_{n})_{n\leq k}
, where
a_{n}\!=\!1
for all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}_{-}
, and we also define

t\!=\!\{\varrho_{0}\!\ast\!u\,;\,u\!\in\!t^{\prime}\}\cup\{\varrho_{k};k\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}_{-}\}
. Then,

t\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
,
|t|_{-}\!=\!-\infty
and
t^{\prime}\!=\!\theta_{\!\varrho_{0}}t
. Let

(\mathbf{h}^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in t^{\prime}}
be distributed as an
\mathbb{N}
-valued branching random walk whose initial position
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}_{\varnothing}
is

0
and whose transition kernel
q(y,dy^{\prime})
is that of the simple symmetric random walk on
\mathbb{N}
reflected at
0
: namely,
q(0,dy^{\prime})\!=\!\delta_{1}
and
q(y,dy^{\prime})\!=\!\tfrac{{}_{1}}{{}^{2}}(\delta_{y-1}(dy^{\prime})+\delta_{y+1}(dy^{\prime}))
, for all integers
y^{\prime}\!\geq\!1
(see (
17
)).
We next set
\mathbf{h}_{\varrho_{0}\ast v}\!=\!\mathbf{h}^{\prime}_{v}
, for all
v\!\in\!t^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{h}_{\varrho_{k}}\!=\!k
, for all

k\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}_{-}
. Clearly
\mathbf{h}
satisfies (
20
). We assume that
(U_{v})_{v\in t}
is independent from
(\mathbf{h}_{v})_{v\in t}
and we define
(X_{v})_{v\in t}
as in (
22
). For all
v\!\in\!t^{\prime}
, we finally set

Y_{v}\!=\theta_{X_{\varrho_{0}}}X_{\varrho_{0}\ast v}
. Then, it is easy to see that
(t^{\prime},\varnothing,(Y_{v})_{v\in t^{\prime}})
has law
Q^{+}_{\mathbf{t}^{\prime}}
. Since
\mathbf{h}_{\varrho_{0}\ast v}\!\geq\!0
, for all
v\!\in\!t^{\prime}
, we get
d_{\mathtt{gr}}(X_{\varrho_{0}\ast v},X_{\varrho_{0}\ast w})\!=\!d_{\mathtt{gr}}(Y_{v},Y_{w})
and
|X_{\varrho_{0}\ast v}|\!=\!|Y_{v}|
, for all
v,w\!\in\!t^{\prime}
, which implies the desired result by (
23
).
\hfill\blacksquare

We next consider subranges of free branching random walks. More precisely, let
\mathbf{t}\!=\!(t,u)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
be such that
|t|_{\!-}\!=\!-\infty
; for all
v\!\in\!t
, let
\mathbf{h}_{v}\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
satisfy (
20
);
let
(U_{v})_{v\in t}
be a family of independent r.v. that are uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
and let
(X_{v})_{v\in t}
be derived from
(U_{v})_{v\in t}
as specified in (
21
) and (
22
).
Let
\mathbf{a}\subset t
and
v_{0}\!\in\!\mathbf{a}
be such that

\forall v\!\in\!\mathbf{a}\backslash\{v_{0}\},\;\textrm{$\overleftarrow{v}\!\in\!\mathbf{a}$, $\;v$ is a descendent of $v_{0}$ (namely $v\!=\!v_{0}\!\ast\!(\theta_{v_{0}}v)$) and $\mathbf{h}_{v}\!\geq\!\mathbf{h}_{v_{0}}$.}

(27)

Observe that for all
v\!\in\!\mathbf{a}
,
X_{v_{0}}
is a prefix of
X_{v}
and it makes sense to define “the subrange”:

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{a})=\big\{\theta_{X_{v_{0}}}\!X_{v}\,;\,v\!\in\!\mathbf{a}\big\}

that is a subtree of
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\![0,1]}
whose elements have a null depth.
We define the following.

\forall v,w\!\in\!t,\quad d(v,w)\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{v}+\mathbf{h}_{w}\!-\!2\min_{\llbracket v,w\rrbracket}\mathbf{h}\;.

(28)

Note that
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket\!\subset\!\mathbf{a}
; thus, the pseudo-metric
d
on
\mathbf{a}\!\times\!\mathbf{a}
only depends on
\mathbf{a}
and on
(\mathbf{h}_{v}\!-\!\mathbf{h}_{v_{0}})_{v\in\mathbf{a}}
. We define the relation
\sim
on
\mathbf{a}
by setting

v\sim w
if and only if
d(v,w)\!=\!0
and we introduce

T(\mathbf{a})=\mathbf{a}\,/\!\sim,\quad\mathtt{proj}\!:\!\mathbf{a}\rightarrow T(\mathbf{a}),\textrm{the canonical projection},\quad r=\mathtt{proj}(v_{0})

(29)

and we keep denoting
d
the (true) metric induced by
d
on
T(\mathbf{a})
. If
\mathbf{t}\!=\!(t,\varrho)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
and
(\mathbf{h}_{v},U_{v},X_{v})_{v\in t}
are as in Proposition
3.5
, then (
23
) implies that

(T(\mathbf{a}),d)
is a graph-tree that is isometric to the subtree
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{a})
. More precisely, let
x\!\in\!T(\mathbf{a})
and let
v,w\!\in\!\mathbf{a}
such that
\mathtt{proj}(v)\!=\!\mathtt{proj}(w)\!=\!x
; by (
23
), we get a.s.
X_{v}\!=\!X_{w}
and it makes sense to set
Z_{x}\!=\!\theta_{X_{v_{0}}}X_{v}
. Then (
23
) asserts that

Z:T(\mathbf{a})\!\rightarrow\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{a})
is an isometry:

\forall x,y\!\in\!T(\mathbf{a}),\quad d(x,y)=|Z_{x}|+|Z_{y}|\!-\!2|Z_{x}\!\wedge\!Z_{y}|=d_{\mathtt{gr}}(Z_{x},Z_{y})\;.

(30)

Thus, the graph-metric of the subtree
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{a})
only depends on
\mathbf{a}
and on
(\mathbf{h}_{v}\!-\!\mathbf{h}_{v_{0}})_{v\in\mathbf{a}}
. Next, the conditional law of
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{a})
given
T(\mathbf{a})
is characterized as follows.
For all
x\!\in\!T(\mathbf{a})\backslash\{r\}
, let
V_{x}
be the unique real number of
[0,1]
such that

Z_{x}\!=\!\overleftarrow{Z}_{x}\!\ast\!(V_{x})\;.

(31)

We easily check the following.

Conditionally given
T(\mathbf{a})
, the
V_{x}
are i.i.d.
[0,1]
-uniform r.v.

(32)

Recall from Definition
3.3
the
\mathtt{b}
-contraction map
\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}\!:\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{[0,1]}\!\rightarrow\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
.
Then, first note that
|Z_{x}\wedge Z_{y}|\!\leq\!|\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(Z_{x})\wedge\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(Z_{y})|
, where common ancestors
are taken respectively
in
\mathbb{W}_{[0,1]}
and in
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
. Moreover, (
32
) implies that

\mathbf{P}(|\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(Z_{x})\!\wedge\!\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(Z_{y})|\!-\!|Z_{x}\!\wedge\!Z_{y}|\!\geq\!k)\!\leq\!\mathtt{b}^{-k}
, for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
.
This inequality combined with the argument of the proof of Corollary
3.6
implies
the following lemma that will be used in Theorem
1.2
.

Lemma 3.7

Let
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
be a finite rooted ordered tree. Let
(t,\varnothing;(Y_{v})_{v\in t})
be a
\mathbb{W}_{\![0,1]}
-valued branching random walk with law
Q^{+}_{\mathbf{t}}
as in Definition
3.1

(ii)
. Recall the
\mathtt{b}
-contraction map
\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}\!:\!\mathbb{W}_{\![0,1]}\!\rightarrow\!\mathbb{W}_{\!d}
from Definition
3.3
and recall that
(t,\varnothing,(\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(Y_{v}))_{v\in t})
has law
Q^{+\mathtt{b}}_{\mathbf{t}}
as in Definition
3.1

(i)
. We denote
the graph distance on
\mathbb{W}_{\![0,1]}
and
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
in the same way by
d_{\mathtt{gr}}
. Then, for all
v,w\!\in\!t
, there exists an
\mathbb{N}
-valued r.v.
G_{v,w}
such that

2G_{v,w}\!=\!d_{\mathtt{gr}}(X_{v},X_{w})\!-\!d_{\mathtt{gr}}(\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(X_{v}),\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(X_{w}))\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbf{P}\big(G_{v,w}\geq k\big)\leq\mathtt{b}^{-k},\;k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}.

(33)

3.3
A coupling between
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
- and
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
-valued branching random walks.

This section is devoted to the proof of the following proposition.

Proposition 3.8

Let
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
be a finite rooted ordered tree as in Definition
2.1
(namely,
|t|_{-}\!=\!0
). We set
\mathbf{t}\!=\!(t,\varnothing)
. For all
n\!\in\!\{0,\ldots,\#t\!-\!1\}
, we set
t_{n}\!=\!\{v\!\in\!t:v\leq_{t}v_{n}\}
where
v_{n}
is the
n
-th smallest vertex of
t
with respect to the lexicographical order
\leq_{t}
.
Then, there exists
two branching random walks

\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathtt{b}}\!=\!(t,\varnothing;(Y_{v})_{v\in t})
and
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+}_{\mathtt{b}}\!=\!(t,\varnothing;(Y^{+}_{v})_{v\in t})
that satisfy the following.

(a)

\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+}_{\mathtt{b}}
is a
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
-valued branching random walk with law

Q^{+\mathtt{b}}_{\mathbf{t}}
as in Definition
3.1

(i)
.

(b)

\mathbf{o}_{\mathtt{b}}\!:=\!Y_{\varnothing}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{-}
-indexed sequence of independent r.v. that are uniformly distributed on
\{1,\ldots,\mathtt{b}\}
and conditionally given
\mathbf{o}_{\mathtt{b}}
,

\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathtt{b}}
is a
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
-valued branching random walk with law

Q^{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathbf{o}_{\mathtt{b}},\mathbf{t}}
as in Definition
3.1

(iii)
.

(c)

For all
c\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, there exists an event
B_{c}
such that
\mathbf{P}(B_{c})\!\leq\!2\mathtt{b}^{-c}(\#t)^{3}
and such that on
\Omega\backslash B_{c}
,

\forall n\!\in\!\{0,\ldots,\#t\!-\!1\},\;\,\big|\#\{Y_{v}\,;v\!\in\!t_{n}\}\!-\#\{Y^{+}_{v};v\!\in\!t_{n}\}\big|\leq\#\{v\!\in\!t\!:|Y^{+}_{v}|\!\leq\!c+1\}\;.

(34)

Overlap of independent trees.

We first prove a result concerning the overlap of independent trees that are randomly embedded in the
\mathtt{b}
-ary tree
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
.
More precisely, let
(\mathtt{T}_{u},r(u))
,
u\!\in\!S
be a finite family of rooted graph-trees (not necessarily ordered)
equipped with their graph-distance
d_{\mathtt{gr}}
.
To simplify notation, we set
|x|\!=\!{d_{\mathtt{gr}}}\,(r(u),x)
, for all
x\!\in\!\mathtt{T}_{u}
.
Let
x,y\!\in\!\mathtt{T}_{u}
; recall that
\llbracket x,y\rrbracket
stands for the shortest path joining
x
to
y

and that
x\wedge y
is the most recent common ancestor of
x
and
y
in
\mathtt{T}_{u}
rooted at
r(u)
. Namely,
\llbracket r(u),x\rrbracket\cap\llbracket r(u),y\rrbracket\!=\!\llbracket r(u),x\wedge y\rrbracket
. Let
x\!\in\!\mathtt{T}_{u}\backslash\{r(u)\}
; for all
i\!\in\!\{1,\ldots,|x|\}
we denote by
x_{i}
the unique ancestor of
x
at height
i
. Let
V^{u}_{x}
,
x\!\in\!\mathtt{T}_{u}
,
u\!\in\!S
be independent uniform r.v. on
\{1,\ldots,\mathtt{b}\}
.
Then, we define the random word:

Z^{u}_{x}\!=\!\big(V^{u}_{x_{1}},V^{u}_{x_{2}},\ldots,V^{u}_{x_{|x|-1}},V^{u}_{x}\big)

We also set
Z^{u}_{r(u)}\!=\!\varnothing
and we introduce the following random subsets of the
\mathtt{b}
-ary tree
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
:

\forall u\!\in\!S,\quad\mathtt{R}_{u}:=\big\{Z^{u}_{x};\,x\!\in\!\mathtt{T}_{u}\big\}\;.

For all
c\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we also set
M_{c}\!=\!\sum_{u\in S}\#\{x\!\in\!\mathtt{T}_{u}:|x|\!\leq\!c\}
.

Lemma 3.9

We keep the notation from above. Let
c\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
. Let
w_{u}\!\in\!\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
,
u\!\in\!S
. Then, there exists an event
A_{c}
of probability

\mathbf{P}(A_{c})\leq\mathtt{b}^{-c}M_{c}^{2}
such that on
\Omega\backslash A_{c}
,

0\leq\sum_{u\in S}\!\#\mathtt{R}_{u}\;-\#\Big(\bigcup_{u\in S}w_{u}\!\ast\!(\mathtt{R}_{u})\Big)\leq M_{c}\;.

(35)

Proof.
Let
u
and
u^{\prime}
be distinct elements of
S
; let
x\!\in\!\mathtt{T}_{u}
and
y\!\in\!\mathtt{T}_{u^{\prime}}

be such that
|x|\!=\!|y|\!=\!{c}
. We introduce the event

A(x,y)\!=\!\big\{(w_{u}\!\ast\!Z^{u}_{x})\!\wedge\!(w_{u^{\prime}}\!\ast\!Z^{u^{\prime}}_{y})\!\in\!\{w_{u}\!\ast\!Z^{u}_{x},w_{u^{\prime}}\!\ast\!Z^{u^{\prime}}_{y}\}\big\}
. Note that on
A(x,y)
,
w_{u}\!\wedge\!w_{u^{\prime}}\!\in\!\{w_{u},w_{u^{\prime}}\}
; so without loss of generality, we can suppose that
|w_{u}|\!\leq\!|w_{u^{\prime}}|
and we easily check that
\mathbf{P}(A(x,y)|Z^{u^{\prime}}_{y})\!=\!\mathtt{b}^{-c}
and thus
\mathbf{P}(A(x,y))\!=\!\mathtt{b}^{-c}
.
We then set for all
u\!\in\!S
,

\mathtt{T}_{u}(c)=\{x\!\in\!\mathtt{T}_{u}:|x|\!=\!c\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad A_{c}=\bigcup\big\{A(x,y)\,;\;x\!\in\!\mathtt{T}_{u}(c),y\!\in\!\mathtt{T}_{u^{\prime}}(c),u,u^{\prime}\!\in\!S,\;\textrm{distinct}\big\}.

Thus,
\mathbf{P}(A_{c})\leq\mathtt{b}^{-c}\sum_{u,u^{\prime}\in S,u\neq u^{\prime}}\#\mathtt{T}_{u}(c)\#\mathtt{T}_{u^{\prime}}(c)\leq\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{2}}\mathtt{b}^{-c}(\sum_{u\in S}\#\mathtt{T}_{u}(c))^{2}\leq\mathtt{b}^{-c}M_{c}^{2}
.

The first inequality in (
35
) is true everywhere on
\Omega
.
For the second inequality in (
35
), we argue deterministically on
\Omega\backslash A_{c}
.
Let
u\!\in\!S
. We first set
R_{u}(c)\!=\!\{w_{u}\ast Z^{u}_{x};x\!\in\!\mathtt{T}_{u}(c)\}
.
Let
u^{\prime}\!\in\!S
be distinct from
u
; suppose that

W\!\in\!R_{u}(c)
and that
W^{\prime}\!\in\!R_{u^{\prime}}(c)
; by definition of
A_{c}
,
W\wedge W^{\prime}\!\notin\!\{W,W^{\prime}\}
and
\theta_{W}(w_{u}\ast\mathtt{R}_{u})\cap\theta_{W^{\prime}}(w_{u^{\prime}}\ast\mathtt{R}_{u^{\prime}})\!=\!\emptyset
. Moreover, if
W,W^{\prime}\!\in\!R_{u}(c)
are distinct,
since
|W|\!=\!|W^{\prime}|\!=\!c+|w_{u}|
, we also get
W\wedge W^{\prime}\!\notin\!\{W,W^{\prime}\}

and thus
\theta_{W}(w_{u}\ast\mathtt{R}_{u})\cap\theta_{W^{\prime}}(w_{u}\ast\mathtt{R}_{u})\!=\!\emptyset
.
Consequently, on
\Omega\backslash A_{c}
, the subsets
\theta_{W}(w_{u}\ast\mathtt{R}_{u})
,
W\!\in\!R_{u}(c)
,

u\!\in\!S
, are pairwise disjoint.

Now observe that
(w_{u}\ast\mathtt{R}_{u})\backslash(\bigcup_{W\in R_{u}(c)}\theta_{W}(w_{u}\!\ast\mathtt{R}_{u}))\!=\!\{w_{u}\!\ast\!Z^{u}_{x};|x|\!<\!c\}
and note that
\#\big(\{w_{u}\!\ast\!Z^{u}_{x};|x|\!<\!c\}\big)\!\leq\!\#\big(\{x\!\in\!\mathtt{T}_{u}\!:|x|\!<\!c\}\big)
.
Thus, on
\Omega\backslash A_{c}
, we get

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{u\in S}\#\mathtt{R}_{u}\Big)-M_{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in S}\!\!\big(\#\mathtt{R}_{u}-\#\{x\!\in\!\mathtt{T}_{u}:|x|\!<\!c\}\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in S}\!\!\big(\#(w_{u}\ast\mathtt{R}_{u})-\#\big(\{w_{u}\!\ast\!Z^{u}_{x};|x|\!<\!c\}\big)\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in S}\#\Big(\!\!\!\!\!\!\bigcup_{\hskip 8.19447ptW\in R_{u}(c)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\theta_{W}(w_{u}\!\ast\!\mathtt{R}_{u})\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\#\Big(\bigcup_{u\in S}\!\!\!\!\!\!\bigcup_{\hskip 8.19447ptW\in R_{u}(c)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\theta_{W}(w_{u}\!\ast\!(\mathtt{R}_{u}))\Big)\leq\#\Big(\bigcup_{u\in S}w_{u}\!\ast\!(\mathtt{R}_{u})\Big),

that implies the desired result.
\hfill\blacksquare

Path coupling.

We first state the following elementary coupling.

Lemma 3.10

Let
K\!:\!\mathbb{N}\!\rightarrow\!\mathbb{N}
be such that
K(0)\!=\!0
and
K(2p+1)\!=\!K(2p+2)\!=\!2p+2
, for all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
. Let
(\mathbf{h}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-valued simple symmetric random walk such that a.s.
\mathbf{h}_{0}\!=\!0
.
For all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, we set
I_{n}\!=\!\inf_{0\leq k\leq n}\mathbf{h}_{k}
and
\mathbf{h}^{+}_{n}\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{n}+K(-I_{n})
. Then,

\mathbf{h}^{+}
is an
\mathbb{N}
-valued simple symmetric random walk reflected at
0
.

Proof.
For all
n\!\geq\!1
, set
\xi_{n}\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{n}\!-\!\mathbf{h}_{n-1}
; the r.v. are i.i.d. and uniform on
\{1,-1\}
.
Observe that
\mathbf{h}^{+}_{n}\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{n}\!-\!I_{n}+{\bf 1}_{\{I_{n}\;\mathrm{odd}\}}
. It is easy to check that
\mathbf{h}^{+}_{n+1}\!-\!\mathbf{h}^{+}_{n}\!=\!\xi_{n+1}{\bf 1}_{\{\mathbf{h}^{+}_{n}\geq 1\}}+{\bf 1}_{\{\mathbf{h}^{+}_{n}=0\}}
, which implies the desired result.
\hfill\blacksquare

The next lemma state the branching random walk version of this coupling.

Lemma 3.11

Let
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
be a rooted ordered tree as in Definition
2.1
(namely,
|t|_{-}\!=\!0
). Let
(t,\varnothing;(\mathbf{h}_{u})_{u\in t})
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-valued branching random walk
whose spatial motion is that of a simple symmetric random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
and whose initial position is
\mathbf{h}_{\varnothing}\!=\!0
.
Recall the function
K
from Lemma
3.10
and set

\forall u\in t,\quad I_{u}=\min_{u^{\prime}\in\llbracket\varnothing,u\rrbracket}\mathbf{h}_{u^{\prime}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbf{h}^{+}_{u}=\mathbf{h}_{u}+K(-I_{u})\;.

(36)

Then,
(t,\varnothing;(\mathbf{h}^{+}_{u})_{u\in t})
is a branching random walk
whose spatial motion is that of an
\mathbb{N}
-valued simple symmetric random walk reflected at
0
and whose initial position is
\mathbf{h}^{+}_{\varnothing}\!=\!0
.

Proof.
We denote by
q_{+}(x,dy)
the transition kernel of the
\mathbb{N}
-valued simple symmetric random walk reflected at
0
: namely,
q_{+}(0,dy)\!=\!\delta_{1}(dy)
and
q_{+}(x,dy)\!=\!\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{2}}(\delta_{x-1}(dy)+\delta_{x+1}(dy))
, if
x\!\geq\!1
. For all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, we set
t_{|n}\!=\!\{u\!\in\!t\!:\!|u|\!\leq\!n\}
and we assume the following property

(P_{n}):

the spatial motion of
the branching random walk
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{n}\!=\!(t_{|n},\varnothing;(\mathbf{h}^{+}_{u})_{u\in t_{|n}})
is that of a
\mathbb{N}
-valued simple symmetric random walk reflected at
0
with initial position

\mathbf{h}^{+}_{\varnothing}\!=\!0
.

We next set
S\!=\!\{u\!\in\!t\!:\!|u|\!=\!n+1\}
and
\xi_{u}\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{u}\!-\!\mathbf{h}_{\overleftarrow{u}}
for all
u\!\in\!S
; the r.v.
(\xi_{u})_{u\in S}
are independent from
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{n}
and they are
also i.i.d. and uniform on
\{1,-1\}
; by definition of
\mathbf{h}^{+}\!
, we also get

\mathbf{h}^{+}_{u}\!=\!\mathbf{h}^{+}_{{}^{\overleftarrow{u}}}+\xi_{u}{\bf 1}_{{}^{\!\{\mathbf{h}^{+}_{{\overleftarrow{u}}}\geq 1\}}}+{\bf 1}_{{}^{\!\{\mathbf{h}^{+}_{\overleftarrow{u}}=0\}}}
. This entails that
the r.v.
\mathbf{h}^{+}_{u}
,
u\!\in\!S
are conditionally independent given
