\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{n}
and that the conditional law of
\mathbf{h}^{+}_{u}
is
q_{+}(\mathbf{h}^{+}_{\overleftarrow{u}},dy)
. This shows that
(P_{n})
implies
(P_{n+1})
, which recursively proves the lemma since
(P_{0})
holds true trivially.
\hfill\blacksquare

The coupling of
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
and
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
.

Let
t\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
be a finite rooted ordered tree as in Definition
2.1
(namely,
|t|_{-}\!=\!0
). We shall use the notation
\mathbf{t}\!=\!(t,\varnothing)
for the pointed tree.
We denote by
\varrho\!=\!(\ldots,1,\ldots,1)
the infinite sequence of
1
indexed by
\mathbb{Z}_{-}
.
For all
l\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}_{-}
,
\varrho_{|l}
is the infinite sequence of
1
indexed by the integers
\!\leq\!l
. We then set

t^{*}\!=\!\big\{\varrho_{|l}\,;\;l\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}_{-}\big\}\cup\big\{\varrho\!\ast\!u\,;\;u\!\in\!t\big\}\;.

Then we define
\mathbf{t}^{*}\!:=\!(t^{*}\!,\varrho)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
and we observe that
|t^{*}|_{-}\!=\!-\infty
.

Let
(t^{*}\!,\varrho\,;(\mathbf{h}^{*}_{v})_{v\in t^{*}}\!)
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-valued branching random walk whose transition kernel is that of a simple symmetric random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
and whose “initial” position is

\mathbf{h}^{*}_{{}^{\varrho}}\!=\!0
. Let
(U^{*}_{v})_{v\in t^{*}}
be independent r.v. that are uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
and that are independent from
(\mathbf{h}^{*}_{v})_{v\in t^{*}}
. We define
(X^{*}_{v})_{v\in t^{*}}
as in (
21
) and (
22
): namely,
X^{*}_{v}\!=\!(U^{*}_{v(k)})_{k\leq\mathbf{h}^{*}_{v}}
, where
v(k)\!\in\,\rrbracket\!-\!\infty,v\rrbracket
,
\mathbf{h}_{v(k)}^{*}\!=\!k
and where
\{\overleftarrow{v}(k);k\!\leq\!\mathbf{h}_{v}^{*}\}
is the set of vertices of
\,\rrbracket\!-\!\infty,v\rrbracket
where
\mathbf{h}^{*}
reaches a new strict infimum. We set
\mathbf{o}\!=\!X^{*}_{{}^{\varrho}}
that is a
\mathbb{Z}_{-}
-indexed sequence of mutually independent r.v. that are uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
.
We easily see that conditionally given
\mathbf{o}
,
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{*}\!=\!(t^{*}\!,\varrho\,;(X^{*}_{v})_{v\in t^{*}}\!)
has law
Q_{\mathbf{o},\mathbf{t}^{*}}
as in Definition
3.1

(iv)
.
Then we set the following

\forall u\!\in\!t,\quad U_{u}\!=\!U^{*}_{\varrho\ast u},\;X_{u}\!=\!X^{*}_{\varrho\ast u},\;\mathbf{h}_{u}\!=\!\mathbf{h}^{*}_{\varrho\ast u},\;Y_{u}\!=\!\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(X_{u}),

and we also set
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\!=\!(t,\varnothing;(X_{u})_{u\in t})
and
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathtt{b}}\!=\!(t,\varnothing;(Y_{u})_{u\in t})
, where we recall the definition of the
\mathtt{b}
-contraction map
\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}
from Remark
3.3
.
Clearly, conditionally given
\mathbf{o}
,
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
has law
Q_{\mathbf{o},\mathbf{t}}
as in Definition
3.1

(iv)
and by Remark
3.3
, the conditional law of

\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathtt{b}}
is
Q^{{}_{\mathtt{b}}}_{{}^{\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\mathbf{o}),\mathbf{t}}}
as in Definition
3.1

(iii)
. Namely,
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathtt{b}}
is distributed as in Proposition
3.8

(b)
.

Let
(U^{\prime}_{u})_{u\in t}
be i.i.d. r.v. that are uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
. We suppose that
(U^{\prime}_{u})_{u\in t}
is independent from
(U^{*}_{v})_{v\in t^{*}}
(and thus from
(U_{u})_{u\in t}
) and from
(\mathbf{h}^{*}_{v})_{v\in t^{*}}
.
We next construct
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathtt{b}}^{+}
thanks to
(U^{\prime}_{u},U_{u},\mathbf{h}_{u})_{u\in t}
as follows:
For all
u\!\in\!t
, we define

I_{u}
and
\mathbf{h}^{+}_{u}
by (
36
) in Lemma
3.11
and we introduce the following notation.

\forall p\!\in\!\mathbb{N},\;\,S_{p}\!=\!\big\{u\!\in\!t\!:-p\!=\!I_{u}\!<\!I_{\overleftarrow{u}}\big\},\;\,S\!=\!\bigcup_{p\in\mathbb{N}}S_{p},\;\,U^{+}_{u}\!=\!\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}U^{\prime}_{u}&\textrm{if $u\!\in\!\bigcup_{p\in\mathbb{N}}S_{2p+1}$, }\\
U_{u}&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

(37)

The (disjoint) union
S
is clearly a finite set, since
t
is a finite tree.
Then,
(U^{+}_{u})_{u\in t}
are i.i.d.
[0,1]
-uniform r.v. that are independent from
(\mathbf{h}^{+}_{u})_{u\in t}
. Recall from Lemma
3.11
that
(t,\varnothing;(\mathbf{h}^{+}_{u})_{u\in t})
is a branching random walk
whose spatial motion is that of an
\mathbb{N}
-valued simple symmetric random walk reflected at
0
and whose initial position is
\mathbf{h}^{+}_{\varnothing}\!=\!0
. In particular,

\mathbf{h}_{u}^{+}\!\geq\!0
for all
u\!\in\!t
. We then define
(X^{+}_{u})_{u\in t}
and
(Y^{+}_{u})_{u\in t}
as follows.

\bullet

If
\mathbf{h}^{+}_{u}\!=\!0
, then we set
X^{+}_{u}\!=\!Y^{+}_{u}\!=\!\varnothing
.

\bullet

If
\mathbf{h}^{+}_{u}\!\geq\!1
, then for all
i\!\in\!\{1,\ldots,\mathbf{h}^{+}_{u}\}
, we denote by
u(i)
is the unique
v\!\in\!\llbracket\varnothing,u\rrbracket
such that

\mathbf{h}^{+}_{{\overleftarrow{v}}}\!<\!i\!=\!\mathbf{h}^{+}_{v}\!=\!\min_{\llbracket v,u\rrbracket}\mathbf{h}^{+}
and we set

X^{+}_{u}\!=\!(U^{{}_{+}}_{{\!u(1)}},\ldots,U^{{}_{+}}_{{\!\!u(\mathbf{h}^{+}_{u})}})\quad\textrm{and}\quad Y_{u}^{+}=\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(X^{+}_{u})\;.

(38)

We set
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+}\!=\!(t,\varnothing;(X^{+}_{u})_{u\in t})
and
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+}_{\mathtt{b}}\!=\!(t,\varnothing;(Y^{+}_{u})_{u\in t})
.
Then, observe that
\mathbf{h}^{+}_{u}\!=\!|X^{+}_{u}|
and that
X^{+}_{u}\!=\!X^{+}_{\overleftarrow{u}}\!\ast\!(U^{+}_{u})
if
\mathbf{h}^{+}_{u}\!=\!\mathbf{h}^{+}_{\overleftarrow{u}}+1
and note that

X^{+}_{u}
is the parent of
X^{{}_{+}}_{{}^{\overleftarrow{u}}}
if

\mathbf{h}^{{}_{+}}_{{}^{u}}\!=\!\mathbf{h}^{{}_{+}}_{{}^{\overleftarrow{u}}}\!-\!1
. Thus, it proves that
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+}
as law
Q^{+}_{\mathbf{t}}
as in Definition
3.1

(ii)
and by Remark
3.3
,
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+}_{\mathtt{b}}
is distributed as in Proposition
3.8

(a)
(namely its law is
Q^{+\mathtt{b}}_{\mathbf{t}}
as in Definition
3.1

(i)
).

Proof of Proposition
3.8
.

We keep the previous notations.
We fix
n\!\in\!\{0,\ldots,\#t\!-\!1\}
. Recall that

t_{n}\!=\!\{v\!\in\!t:v\leq_{t}v_{n}\}
where
v_{n}
is the
n
-th smallest
vertex of
t
in the lexicographical order
\leq_{t}
on
t
. Note that
t_{n}\!\in\!\mathbb{T}
, namely it is a rooted ordered tree with null depth. Recall from (
37
) the definition of
S
. For all
u\!\in\!S
, we set

\mathbf{a}_{u}=\big\{v\!\in\!t_{n}:v\!:=\!u\ast\theta_{u}v\;\textrm{ and}\;\min_{w\in\llbracket u,v\rrbracket}\mathbf{h}_{w}\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{u}\big\}\subseteq t\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{a}_{u})\!=\!\big\{\theta_{X_{u}}X_{v};v\!\in\!\mathbf{a}_{u}\big\}\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\![0,1]}\,.

Note that
\mathbf{a}_{u}
depends on
n
and that if
u\!\notin\!t_{n}
, then
\mathbf{a}_{u}
and
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{a}_{u})
are empty.
If
u\!\in\!t_{n}
, then the previous definitions make sense because
\mathbf{a}_{u}
satisfies (
27
) (with
u=v_{0}
) and
X_{u}
is necessarily
a prefix of
X_{v}
since
v
is a descendent of
u
and
I_{v}\!=\!I_{u}
.
Observe that
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{a}_{u})
is a subtree of
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\![0,1]}
as in Definition
2.3
.

Recall from (
28
) the notation
d(v,w)\!=\!\mathbf{h}_{v}+\mathbf{h}_{w}\!-\!2\min_{\llbracket v,w\rrbracket}\mathbf{h}
, for all
v,w\!\in\!t
,
that is a pseudo-metric on
t
. We denote by
v\!\sim\!w
the equivalence relation relation
d(v,w)\!=\!0
and as in (
29
), we set for all
u\!\in\!S
,

T(\mathbf{a}_{u})=\mathbf{a}_{u}/\!\!\sim,\quad\mathtt{proj}\!:\!\mathbf{a}_{u}\rightarrow T(\mathbf{a}_{u}),\textrm{the canonical projection},\quad r(u)=\mathtt{proj}(u)\;.

Note that
T(\mathbf{a}_{u})
only depends on
(\mathbf{h}_{v}\!-\!I_{u})_{v\in\mathbf{a}_{u}}
and that

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{a}_{u})
only depends on
(\mathbf{h}_{v}\!-\!I_{u};U_{v})_{v\in\mathbf{a}_{u}}
.
Let
x\!\in\!T(\mathbf{a}_{u})
;
by (
23
) in Lemma
3.5
, for all
v,w\!\in\!\mathbf{a}_{u}
such that
\mathtt{proj}(v)\!=\!\mathtt{proj}(w)\!=\!x
, we get
X_{v}\!=\!X_{w}
and it makes sense to set
Z^{u}_{x}\!=\!\theta_{X_{u}}X_{v}
; then,

Z^{u}\!:\!T(\mathbf{a}_{u})\!\rightarrow\!\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{a}_{u})
is an isometry given in (
30
).
As in (
31
), for all
x\!\in\!T(\mathbf{a}_{u})\backslash\{r(u)\}
we denote by
V^{u}_{x}
the unique real number of
[0,1]
such that
Z^{u}_{x}\!=\!\overleftarrow{Z}^{u}_{x}\ast(V^{u}_{x})
.

Then by
(
32
), for all

u\!\in\!S
, conditionally given
\mathbf{h}
, the
V^{u}_{x}
,
x\!\in\!T(\mathbf{a}_{u})
are i.i.d.
[0,1]
-uniform r.v. Since the subsets
(\mathbf{a}_{u})_{u\in S}

are pairwise disjoint and since
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{a}_{u})
only depends on
(\mathbf{h}_{v}-I_{u};U_{v})_{v\in\mathbf{a}_{u}}
, the
(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{a}_{u}))_{u\in S}
are conditionally independent given
\mathbf{h}
. Thus,
conditionally given
\mathbf{h}
, the
V^{u}_{x}
,
x\!\in\!T(\mathbf{a}_{u})\backslash\{r(u)\}
,
u\!\in\!S
, are i.i.d.
[0,1]
-uniform r.v.

Next observe that

\{X_{v};v\!\in\!t_{n}\}=\bigcup_{u\in S}X_{u}\!\ast\!(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{a}_{u})){\color[rgb]{0,140,69}\,.}

(39)

Therefore, using (
37
) and noting that
(X_{u})_{u\in S}
only depends on
\mathbf{h}
and on
(U^{*}_{\varrho_{|l}})_{l\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}}
that is independent from
(U_{u})_{u\in t}
,
we conclude that, conditionally given
\mathbf{h}
and
(X_{u})_{u\in S}
, the
V^{u}_{x}
,
x\!\in\!T(\mathbf{a}_{u})\backslash\{r(u)\}
,
u\!\in\!S
, are i.i.d.
[0,1]
-uniform r.v.
From the coupling defined in (
37
) and (
38
) we also derive easily the following.

\{X^{+}_{v};v\!\in\!t_{n}\}\!=\!\!\bigcup_{u\in S}X^{\prime}_{u}\!\ast\!(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{a}_{u})),\;\textrm{where
for all $u$ in $S$,}\;X^{\prime}_{u}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}U^{\prime}_{u}&\textrm{if $u\!\in\!\bigcup_{p\in\mathbb{N}}S_{2p+1}$, }\\
\varnothing&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

(40)

Since the
(U^{\prime}_{u})_{u\in t}
are independent from
\mathbf{h}
and from
(U_{u})_{u\in t}
,
conditionally given
\mathbf{h}
and
(U^{\prime}_{u})_{u\in S}
, the r.v.
V^{u}_{x}
,
x\!\in\!T(\mathbf{a}_{u})\backslash\{r(u)\}
,
u\!\in\!S
, are i.i.d.
[0,1]
-uniform. Denote by
\mathscr{G}
the sigma-field generated by
\mathbf{h}
and by
(X_{u},U^{\prime}_{u})_{u\in S}
. Therefore, we have proved the following.

Conditionally given
\mathscr{G}
, the
V^{u}_{x}
,
x\!\in\!T(\mathbf{a}_{u})\backslash\{r(u)\}
,
u\!\in\!S
, are i.i.d.
[0,1]
-uniform r.v.

(41)

Then we set

\forall u\!\in\!S,\quad\mathtt{R}_{u}\!=\!\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{a}_{u}))\quad\textrm{and}\quad\forall c\!\in\!\mathbb{N},\quad M_{c}(n)=\sum_{u\in S}\#\{x\!\in\!T(\mathbf{a}_{u}):d(r(u),x)\!\leq\!c\}\;.

Note that
M_{c}(n)\!\leq\!\#t_{n}\!\leq\!\#t
.
By (
41
), conditionally given
\mathscr{G}
we can apply Lemma
3.9
to
\mathtt{T}_{u}\!:=\!T(\mathbf{a}_{u})
and
w_{u}\!:=\!\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(X_{u})
,
u\!\in\!S
, to get an event
A_{c}(n)
such that
\mathbf{P}(A_{c}(n))\leq\mathtt{b}^{-c}(\#t)^{2}
and such that on
\Omega\backslash A_{c}(n)
,

0\leq\sum_{u\in S}\#\mathtt{R}_{u}\;-\#\big\{Y_{v};\,v\!\in\!t_{n}\big\}\overset{\textrm{by (\ref{ralibb})}}{=}\sum_{u\in S}\#\mathtt{R}_{u}\;-\#\Big(\bigcup_{u\in S}\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(X_{u})\!\ast\!(\mathtt{R}_{u})\Big)\leq M_{c}(n)\;.

Similarly, conditionally given
\mathscr{G}
, we apply Lemma
3.9
to
\mathtt{T}_{u}\!:=\!T(\mathbf{a}_{u})
and to
w_{u}\!:=\!\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(X^{\prime}_{u})
,
u\!\in\!S
, to get an event
A^{\prime}_{c}(n)
such that
\mathbf{P}(A^{\prime}_{c}(n))\leq\mathtt{b}^{-c}(\#t)^{2}
and such that on
\Omega\backslash A^{\prime}_{c}(n)
,

0\leq\sum_{u\in S}\#\mathtt{R}_{u}\;-\#\big\{Y^{+}_{v};\,v\!\in\!t_{n}\big\}\overset{\textrm{by (\ref{rarefll})}}{=}\sum_{u\in S}\#\mathtt{R}_{u}\;-\#\Big(\bigcup_{u\in S}\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(X^{\prime}_{u})\!\ast\!(\mathtt{R}_{u})\Big)\leq M_{c}(n)\;.

Then, we set
B_{c}(n)\!=\!A_{c}(n)\cup A^{\prime}_{c}(n)
; thus,
\mathbf{P}(B_{c}(n))\!\leq\!2\mathtt{b}^{-c}(\#t)^{2}
and on
\Omega\backslash B_{c}(n)
, we get
|\#\{Y_{v};v\!\in\!t_{n}\}-\#\{Y^{+}_{v};v\!\in\!t_{n}\}|\!\leq\!M_{c}(n)
.
Now observe that if
x\!\in\!T(\mathbf{a}_{u})
, then
\mathbf{h}^{+}_{x}\!=\!|X^{+}_{x}|\!=\!|X^{\prime}_{u}|+|Z^{u}_{x}|\!\leq\!1+d(r(u),x)
.
This implies
M_{c}(n)\!\leq\!\#\{v\!\in\!t;|Y^{+}_{v}|\!\leq\!c+1\}
. Thus, on
\Omega\backslash B_{c}(n)
, we get

\big|\#\{Y_{v}\,;v\!\in\!t_{n}\}-\#\{Y^{+}_{v};v\!\in\!t_{n}\}\big|\leq\#\{v\!\in\!t;|Y^{+}_{v}|\!\leq\!c+1\}\;.

We completes the proof of Proposition
3.8
by taking
B_{c}\!=\!\bigcup_{0\leq n<\#t}B_{c}(n)
.
\hfill\blacksquare

3.4
Estimates.

The goal of this section is to establish Proposition
3.16
below. To that end, we first state preliminary estimates.
Recall that
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}\!=\!\{x\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\mathtt{b}}\!:\!|x|_{-}\!=\!-\infty\}
. We denote by
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
the canonical process on the space
(\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}})^{\mathbb{N}}
equipped with product topology (that is Polish) and with the corresponding Borel sigma-field. In this section, let us denote by
P_{\!\mathrm{o}}
(instead of
Q^{{}_{\mathtt{b}}}_{{}^{\mathrm{o},\mathbb{N}}}
) the law of a Markov chain on
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
with transition kernel
p_{\mathtt{b}}
as defined in (
19
) and whose initial position is
\mathrm{o}
. The following result only contains some standard estimates.

Lemma 3.12

Let
\mathrm{o}\!\in\!\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
be such that
|\mathrm{o}|\!=\!0
.
For all
k,p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, we set

\mathrm{o}(p)\!=\!\mathrm{o}_{|]-\infty,-p]},\;\,\mathtt{Sp}\!=\!\{\mathrm{o}(p);p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}\},\;\,\mathbf{r}_{0}\!=\!0\;\,\textrm{and}\;\,\mathbf{r}_{k+1}\!=\!\inf\{n\!>\!\mathbf{r}_{k}:Y_{n}\!\in\!\mathtt{Sp}\}.

(42)

The following holds true.

(i)

P_{\!\mathrm{o}}
-almost surely for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{r}_{k}\!<\!\infty
and there exists
Z_{k}\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathrm{o}(Z_{k})\!=\!Y_{\mathbf{r}_{k}}
. Moreover,
(Z_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is an
\mathbb{N}
-valued birth-and-death Markov chain whose transition probabilities
(\rho(p,q))_{p,q\in\mathbb{N}}

are given as follows: for all
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,

\rho(p,p+1)\!=\!\tfrac{1}{2},\quad\rho(p,p\!-\!1)\!=\!\tfrac{1}{2\mathtt{b}},\quad\rho(p,p)\!=\!\tfrac{\mathtt{b}-1}{2\mathtt{b}}\;,

(43)

and
\rho(0,0)\!=\!\rho(0,1)\!=\!1/2
. Then,
Z
is transient which implies that almost surely
|\mathrm{o}\!\wedge\!Y_{n}|\!\rightarrow\!-\infty
and that
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is transient under
P_{\!\mathrm{o}}
.

(ii)

For all
x,y\!\in\!\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
, we set
G_{\mathtt{b}}(x,y)\!=\!\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}P_{x}\big(Y_{n}\!=\!y\big)
. Then, for all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
,

G_{\mathtt{b}}(x,y)=\tfrac{2\mathtt{b}}{\mathtt{b}-1}\mathtt{b}^{|x\wedge y|-|y|}\;.

(44)

(iii)

For all
y\!\in\!\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
, we set
\mathtt{H}_{y}\!=\!\inf\{n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}\!:\!Y_{n}\!=\!y\}
, with the convention that
\inf\emptyset\!=\!\infty
.
For all
s\!\in\![0,\infty)
, we set
g(s)\!=\!-\!\log\big(1\!-\!\sqrt{1\!-\!e^{-2s}})
. Then, for all
x\!\in\!\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
,
E_{x}[e^{-s(1+\mathtt{H}_{\overleftarrow{x}})}]\!=\!\exp(-g(s))
.
Moreover, there exists a constant
C\!\in\!(1,\infty)
such that

\textrm{$P_{\mathrm{o}}$-a.s.\penalty\ for all $k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}$ and for all $s\!\in\![0,1]$,}\quad E_{\mathrm{o}}\big[e^{-s(\mathbf{r}_{k+1}-\mathbf{r}_{k})}\big|Z\big]\geq e^{-C\sqrt{s}}\;.

(45)

Proof.
Since under
P_{\!\mathrm{o}}
,
(|Y_{n}|)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is distributed as a simple symmetric random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
, we easily see that
P_{\!\mathrm{o}}
-a.s. for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{r}_{k}\!<\!\infty
.
The strong Markov property at the stopping times
\mathbf{r}_{k}
implies that
(Z_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is an
\mathbb{N}
-valued birth-and-death Markov chain whose
transition probabilities
\rho(p,q)
are given by (
43
) which easily implies that

Z_{k}\rightarrow\!\infty
which entails
(i)
.

Let us prove
(ii)
.
For all
x\!\in\!\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
, we introduce the stopping times

\mathtt{H}_{x}\!=\!\inf\{n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}\!:\!Y_{n}\!=\!x\}
and

\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{x}\!=\!\inf\{n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}\!:\!Y_{n}\!=\!x\}
. First observe that for all
x,y\!\in\!\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
,
P_{\!x}(\mathtt{H}_{y}\!<\!\infty)\!=\!P_{\!x\wedge y}(\mathtt{H}_{y}\!<\!\infty)
. By adapting the argument of
(i)
, the height of the process
Y
restricted to
\rrbracket-\infty,y\rrbracket
is a birth-and-death process with transition
\rho(\cdot,\cdot)
and the Gambler’s ruin estimate implies that
P_{\!x\wedge y}(\mathtt{H}_{y}\!<\!\infty)\!=\!\mathtt{b}^{|x\wedge y|-|y|}
. Similarly, we get
P_{\!y}(\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{y}\!=\!\infty)\!=\!(\mathtt{b}\!-\!1)/2\mathtt{b}
.
The Markov property at resp.
\mathtt{H}_{y}
and
\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{y}
implies resp. that

G_{\mathtt{b}}(x,y)\!=\!P_{\!x}(\mathtt{H}_{y}\!<\!\infty)G_{\mathtt{b}}(y,y)
and that

G_{\mathtt{b}}(y,y)\!=\!1+P_{\!y}(\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{y}\!<\!\infty)\,G_{\mathtt{b}}(y,y)
, which entails (
44
).

Let us prove
(iii)
. Note that under
P_{\!x}
,
(|Y_{n}|)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is distributed as a simple symmetric
random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
with initial position
|x|
. Then
\mathtt{H}_{\overleftarrow{x}}
is the first time
(|Y_{n}|)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
reaches
|x|\!-\!1
; therefore, the law of
\mathtt{H}_{\overleftarrow{x}}
does not depend on
x
and we get

E_{x}[\exp(-s(\mathtt{H}_{\overleftarrow{x}}+1))]\!=\!\exp(-g(s))
by well-known arguments.
Next, let us work conditionally given
Z
; we set
x\!=\!Y_{\mathbf{r}_{k}+1}
; on the event
\{Z_{k}\!=\!Z_{k+1}\}
,
\overleftarrow{x}\!=\!\mathrm{o}(Z_{k})
and
\mathbf{r}_{k+1}\!-\!\mathbf{r}_{k}\!-\!1
is the time at which
the shifted random walk
(Y_{\mathbf{r}_{k}+1+n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
returns for the first time to
\overleftarrow{x}
;
thus, by Markov at
\mathbf{r}_{k}
and by the previous argument,
P_{\mathrm{o}}
-a.s. on the event
\{Z_{k}\!=\!Z_{k+1}\}
,
E_{\mathrm{o}}[\exp(\!-s(\mathbf{r}_{k+1}\!-\!\mathbf{r}_{k}))|Z]\!=\!\exp(\!-g(s))
. If
Z_{k}\!\neq\!Z_{k+1}
, then
\mathbf{r}_{k+1}\!-\!\mathbf{r}_{k}\!=\!1
. Consequently,

E_{\mathrm{o}}[\exp(\!-s(\mathbf{r}_{k+1}\!-\!\mathbf{r}_{k}))|Z]\!=\!\exp(\!-g(s){\bf 1}_{\{Z_{k}=Z_{k+1}\}}\!-\!s{\bf 1}_{\{Z_{k}\neq Z_{k+1}\}})
,
which implies (
45
) because there exists
C\!\in\!(1,\infty)
such that
g(s)\!\leq C\sqrt{s}
for all
s\!\in\![0,1]
and since
s\!\leq\!\sqrt{s}
for all
s\!\in\![0,1]
.
\hfill\blacksquare

Proposition 3.13

Let
\mathrm{o}\!\in\!\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
such that
|\mathrm{o}|\!=\!0
. Recall that
P_{\!\mathrm{o}}
stands for the canonical law of
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
-valued Markov chains with transition kernel
p_{\mathtt{b}}
defined by (
19
) and with initial position
\mathrm{o}
.
Let
f\!:\![0,\infty)\!\rightarrow\![0,1]
be such that
\sum_{p\in\mathbb{N}}\sqrt{f(p)}\!<\!\infty
.

\textrm{$P_{\!\mathrm{o}}$-a.s.}\quad\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f\big(\!-\!|\mathrm{o}\!\wedge\!Y_{n}|\big)\,<\infty\;.

Proof.
Recall from (
42
) in Lemma
3.12
notations
\mathrm{o}(p)
,

\mathtt{Sp}
and
\mathbf{r}_{k}
and recall from Lemma
3.12

(i)
the definition of the
\mathbb{N}
-valued birth-and-death process
(Z_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Let
\ell\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
. First observe that

W_{\ell}\!:=\!\!\!\sum_{0\leq n<\mathbf{r}_{\ell}}\!\!f\big(\!\!-\!|\mathrm{o}\wedge Y_{n}|\big)=\sum_{0\leq k<\ell}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathbf{r}_{k}\leq n<\mathbf{r}_{k+1}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!f(Z_{k})\;=\sum_{0\leq k<\ell}(\mathbf{r}_{k+1}\!-\!\mathbf{r}_{k})f(Z_{k}).

By the Markov property, the sequence of random times

\mathbf{r}_{k+1}\!-\!\mathbf{r}_{k}
are conditionally independent given
Z
. Thus by (
45
) in
Lemma
3.12

(iii)
, there is
C\!\in\!(1,\infty)
such that for all
s\!\in\![0,1]
,

\displaystyle E_{\!\mathrm{o}}\big[e^{-sW_{\ell}}\big|Z\big]\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\prod_{0\leq k<\ell}\mathbf{E}\big[e^{-s(\mathbf{r}_{k+1}-\mathbf{r}_{k})f(Z_{k})}\big|Z\big]\geq\exp\Big(\!\!-\!C\!\!\!\sum_{0\leq k<\ell}\!\!\!\sqrt{sf(Z_{k})}\Big)

(46)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\exp\Big(\!\!-\!C\sqrt{s}\sum_{p\in\mathbb{N}}N_{p}\sqrt{\!f(p)}\Big),\;\,\textrm{where $N_{p}\!=\!\#\big\{k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}\!:\!Z_{k}\!=\!p\big\}$.}

Next observe that
E_{\mathrm{o}}[N_{p}]\!=\!G_{\mathtt{b}}(\mathrm{o},\mathrm{o}(p))\!=\!2\mathtt{b}/(\mathtt{b}\!-\!1)
by (
44
) in Lemma
3.12

(ii)
.
Set
W\!=\!\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f\big(\!\!-\!|\mathrm{o}\!\wedge\!Y_{n}|\big)
; by letting
\ell\!\rightarrow\!\infty
in (
46
) and by Jensen’s inequality, we get

E_{\mathrm{o}}\big[e^{-sW}\big]\!\geq\!\exp\Big(\!\!-\!C\sqrt{s}\sum_{p\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{E}[N_{p}]\sqrt{\!f(p)}\Big)=\exp\Big(\!\!-\!C^{\prime}\sqrt{s}\sum_{p\in\mathbb{N}}\sqrt{\!f(p)}\Big)\;,

where
C^{\prime}\!=\!2\mathtt{b}C/(\mathtt{b}\!-\!1)
. Since
\sum_{p\in\mathbb{N}}\sqrt{\!f(p)}\!<\!\infty
, we get
\lim_{s\rightarrow 0+}\mathbf{E}\big[e^{-sW}\big]\!=\!1
which entails that
P_{\!\mathrm{o}}
-a.s.
W\!<\!\infty
. This completes the proof of the proposition.
\hfill\blacksquare

Lemma 3.14

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\mu(k)\!=\!1
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\tau}\!=\!(\tau,\varnothing)
be a random pointed tree such that a.s.
|\tau|_{\!-}\!=\!0
,
k_{\varnothing}(\tau)\!=\!1
and such that
\theta_{(1)}\tau
is a GW(
\mu
)-tree. Let
x\!\in\!\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
and let

\boldsymbol{\Theta}\!=\!(\tau,\varnothing\,;(X_{\!v})_{v\in\tau})
be a random
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
-valued branching random walk that has law
Q^{{}_{\mathtt{b}}}_{{}^{x,\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
conditionally given
\boldsymbol{\tau}
, as defined in Definition
3.1

(iii)
.
For all
y\!\in\!\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
we set

\xi(x,y)=\mathbf{P}\big(y\notin\big\{X_{\!v}\,;\,v\!\in\!\tau\backslash\{\varnothing\}\big\}\big)\;,

(47)

which turns out to be strictly positive. Then, we get
1\!-\!\xi(x,y)\!\leq\!G_{\mathtt{b}}(x,y)\!=\!\tfrac{2\mathtt{b}}{\mathtt{b}-1}\mathtt{b}^{\,|x\wedge y|-|y|}
.

Proof.
By a simple union bound we first get the following.

\mathbf{P}\big(y\!\in\!\big\{X_{\!v}\,;v\!\in\!\tau\backslash\{\varnothing\}\!\big\}\;\big|\,\boldsymbol{\tau}\big)\leq\mathbf{E}\Big[\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\hskip 8.19447ptv\in\tau\backslash\!\{\varnothing\!\}}\!\!\!\!\!\!{\bf 1}_{\{X_{v}=y\}}\;\Big|\,\boldsymbol{\tau}\Big]\leq\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\hskip 8.19447ptv\in\tau\backslash\!\{\varnothing\!\}}\!\!\!\!\!Q^{\mathtt{b}}_{x,\boldsymbol{\tau}}\big(X_{v}=y\big).

By definition of branching random walks,
Q^{{}_{\mathtt{b}}}_{{}^{x,\boldsymbol{\tau}}}(X_{v}\!=\!y)\!=\!P_{\!x}(Y_{|v|}\!=\!y)
, where
P_{\!x}
stands for the canonical law of the random walk that starts at
x
in
\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
and whose transition kernel is
p_{\mathtt{b}}
as defined in (
19
) in Definition
3.1

(iii)
.
Thus,
\mathbf{P}\big(y\!\in\!\big\{X_{\!v}\,;v\!\in\!\tau\backslash\{\varnothing\}\!\big\}\big)\leq\sum_{n\geq 1}\mathbf{E}[N_{n}]\,P_{\!x}(Y_{n}\!=\!y)
,
where
N_{n}\!=\!\#\{v\!\in\!\tau\!:\!|v|\!=\!n\}
. Since the offspring distribution
\mu
is critical,
\mathbf{E}[N_{n}]\!=\!1
.
Therefore, recalling Lemma (
3.12
)
(ii)
, we have that the right member is smaller or equal to
G_{\mathtt{b}}(x,y)
, which concludes the proof.
\hfill\blacksquare

Lemma 3.15

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\mu(k)\!=\!1
.
Let us suppose that there exists
\beta\!\in\!(0,\infty)
such that

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\mu(k)\,k(\log k)^{1+\beta}\!<\!\infty
.
For all
r\!\in\![0,1]
, we set

\psi(r)\!=\!\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}r^{k}\overline{\mu}(k+1)
, where we recall that for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\}
,

\overline{\mu}(k)
stands for
\sum_{\ell\geq k}\mu(\ell)
.
Then, there exists
C_{\beta,\mu}\!\in\!(0,\infty)
such that

\forall r\!\in\!(0,1/2),\qquad 0\leq 1-\psi(1-r)\leq\frac{C_{\beta,\mu}}{(\log 1/r)^{1+\beta}}\;.

(48)

Proof.
Let
Z
be an
\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\}
-valued random variable distributed according to
\overline{\mu}
. Observe that
C\!:=\!\mathbf{E}[(\log Z)^{1+\beta}]\!=\!\sum_{l\geq k\geq 1}\mu(l)(\log k)^{1+\beta}\!\leq\!\sum_{k\geq 1}\mu(k)k(\log k)^{1+\beta}\!<\!\infty
.
Then, for all
t\!>\!0
and for all
r\!\in\!(0,1/2)
, we get

\displaystyle 1\!-\!\psi(1\!-\!r)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\mathbf{E}\big[\big(1\!-\!(1\!-\!r)^{Z-1}\big){\bf 1}_{\{Z\leq t+1\}}\big]+\mathbf{E}\big[\big(1\!-\!(1\!-\!r)^{Z-1}\big){\bf 1}_{\{Z>t+1\}}\big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\!\!\!1\!-\!(1\!-\!r)^{t}+\mathbf{P}(Z\!>\!t+1)\;\leq\;2rt+\frac{\mathbf{E}[(\log Z)^{1+\beta}]}{(\log(1+t))^{1+\beta}}=2rt+C(\log(1+t))^{-1-\beta}.

We choose
1+t\!=\!r^{-1}(\log 1/r)^{-1-\beta}
and we easily get (
48
).
\hfill\blacksquare

Proposition 3.16

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{N}
such that

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\mu(k)=1\quad\textrm{and}\quad\exists\beta\!\in\!(1,\infty)\;\textrm{such that}\;\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k(\log k)^{1+\beta}\mu(k)<\!\infty.

(49)

Let
\mathrm{o}\!\in\!\mathbb{W}^{*}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
be such that
|\mathrm{o}|\!=\!0
and let
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\!\!:=\!(\tau^{*}\!,\,\varrho\,;\,(X_{v})_{v\in\tau^{*}})
be a
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
-valued branching random walk whose distribution is the following:
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\!=\!(\tau^{*}\!,\varrho)
is an IPGW
{}^{+}(\mu)
-tree as in Definition
2.8

(ii)
and conditionally given
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}
,
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
has law
Q^{{}_{\mathtt{b}}}_{{}^{\mathrm{o},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}}}
as in Definition
3.1

(iii)
.
Then, with positive probability
(X_{v})_{v\in\tau}
visits its initial position only once. Namely,

\kappa_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}:=\mathbf{P}\big(\mathrm{o}\!\notin\!\big\{X_{v}\,;v\!\in\!\tau^{*}\backslash\{\varrho\}\big\}\,\big)>0\;.

(50)

Proof.
Let us recall the following notation

\forall p\!\in\!\mathbb{N},\;\,\varrho(p)=\varrho_{|\,]-\infty,-p]},\;\,Y_{p}\!=\!X_{\varrho(p)},\quad\mathtt{Sp}\!=\!\{\varrho(p)\,;p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}\}\;\,\textrm{and}\;\,\partial\mathtt{Sp}\!=\!\big\{u\!\in\!\tau^{*}\backslash\mathtt{Sp}:\overleftarrow{u}\!\in\!\mathtt{Sp}\big\}.

By Definition
2.8

(ii)
of the right part of an infinite pointed GW(
\mu
)-tree,
the subtrees
(\theta_{u}\tau^{*})_{u\in\partial\mathtt{Sp}}
are i.i.d. GW(
\mu
)-trees and the r.v.
(k_{\varrho(p)}(\tau^{*}))_{p\in\mathbb{N}}
are independent,
k_{\varrho}(\tau^{*})
has law
\mu
and for all
p\!\geq\!1
,
k_{\varrho(p)}(\tau^{*})
has law
\overline{\mu}
that is defined by
\overline{\mu}(k)\!=\!\sum_{l\geq k}\mu(l)
,
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\overline{\mu}(0)\!=\!0
.
For all
u\!\in\!\partial\mathtt{Sp}
, we define
\Theta_{u}\!:=\!(\tau_{u};\varnothing;(X^{u}_{\!{v}})_{v\in\tau_{u}})
as follows:

-

\tau_{u}
is the unique tree such that
k_{\varnothing}(\tau_{u})\!=\!1
and
\theta_{(1)}\tau_{u}\!=\!\theta_{u}\tau^{*}
(see (
14
) for the definition of
\theta_{u}\tau^{*}
).

-
Since
\overleftarrow{u}\!\in\!\mathtt{Sp}
, there exists
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
such that
\overleftarrow{u}\!=\!\varrho(p)
and we set
X^{u}_{\!\varnothing}\!=\!Y_{p}
.

-
For all
v\!\in\!\theta_{u}\tau
,
X^{u}_{\!(1)\ast v}\!=\!X_{u\ast v}
(recall from (
10
)
the definition of the concatenation
u\ast v
).

Namely,
\Theta_{u}
is the restriction of the branching walk
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
to the subtree stemming from
u
, including the spatial position of
\overleftarrow{u}
. Conditionally given
Y\!=\!(Y_{p})_{p\in\mathbb{N}}
and

(k_{\varrho(p)}(\tau^{*}))_{p\in\mathbb{N}}
.
the branching random walks
\Theta_{u}
,
u\!\in\!\partial\mathtt{Sp}
, are independent; moreover, conditionally given
(Y_{p},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{u})
,
\Theta_{u}
has law
Q^{{}_{d}}_{{}^{Y_{p},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{u}}}
; here
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{u}
stands for
(\tau_{u},\varnothing)
and
p
is such that
\overleftarrow{u}\!=\!\varrho(p)
.
Then, for all
q\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we set

A_{q}\!=\!\big\{\mathrm{o}\!\notin\!\big\{X_{v}\,;v\!\in\!\tau^{*}\backslash\{\varrho\}\!:\!|v\!\wedge\!\varrho|\!\geq\!-q\big\}\big\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{\mathrm{o}}\!=\!\inf\{p\!\geq\!1\!:\!Y_{p}\!=\!\mathrm{o}\}.

The event
A_{q}
decreases as
q\!\rightarrow\!\infty
to the event that the
branching walk
(X_{v})_{v\in\tau^{*}}
only visits
\mathrm{o}
once. The previous independence properties then imply the following.

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(A_{q}\,|\,Y,\tau^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf 1}_{\{\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{\mathrm{o}}>q\}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\prod_{\hskip 16.38895ptu\in\partial\mathtt{Sp}:|u|>-q}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!Q^{\mathtt{b}}_{Y_{|\overleftarrow{u}|},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{u}}\!\big(\mathrm{o}\notin\{X^{u}_{v};v\!\in\!\tau_{u}\backslash\{\varnothing\}\}\big)

\displaystyle\textrm{and thus}\quad\mathbf{P}(A_{q}\,|\,Y,\mathtt{Sp}\cup\partial\mathtt{Sp})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf 1}_{\{\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{\mathrm{o}}>q\}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\prod_{\hskip 16.38895ptu\in\partial\mathtt{Sp}:|u|>-q}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\xi(Y_{|\overleftarrow{u}|},\mathrm{o}),

where we recall the notation
\xi(x,y)
from (
47
) in Lemma
3.14
.
Since
Y
and
the r.v.
k_{\varrho(p)}(\tau^{*})
are independent, we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(A_{q}\,|\,Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf 1}_{\{\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{\mathrm{o}}>q\}}\mathbf{E}\big[\xi(\mathrm{o},\mathrm{o})^{k_{\varrho}(\tau^{*})}\big]\!\!\!\prod_{1\leq p\leq q}\!\!\mathbf{E}\big[\xi(Y_{p},\mathrm{o})^{k_{\varrho(p)}(\tau^{*})-1}\big|Y\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf 1}_{\{\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{\mathrm{o}}>q\}}\phi\big(\xi(\mathrm{o},\mathrm{o})\big)\!\!\!\prod_{1\leq p\leq q}\!\!\psi\big(\xi(Y_{p},\mathrm{o})\big),

where we have set

\forall r\!\in\![0,1],\quad\phi(r)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}r^{k}\mu(k)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\psi(r)\!=\!\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}r^{k}\overline{\mu}(k+1)=\frac{1\!-\!\phi(r)}{1-r}.

Notice that as
q\!\rightarrow\!\infty
, the event
\{\mathtt{H}^{o}_{\mathrm{o}}>q\}
decreases to the event
\{\mathtt{H}^{o}_{\mathrm{o}}=\infty\}
, which has strictly positive probability by Lemma
3.12

(i)
. Then, we easily see that (
50
) holds true if

\sum_{p\in\mathbb{N}}\big(1\!-\!\psi\big(\xi(Y_{p},\mathrm{o})\big)\big)<\!\infty.

(51)

But by Lemma
3.14
,
1\!-\!\xi(Y_{p},\mathrm{o})\!\leq\!\tfrac{2\mathtt{b}}{\mathtt{b}-1}\mathtt{b}^{\,|Y_{p}\wedge\mathrm{o}|}
since

|\mathrm{o}|\!=\!0
. By (
48
) in Lemma
3.15
, there are two constants
C,C^{\prime}\!\in\!(0,\infty)

such that if
-|Y_{p}\wedge\mathrm{o}|\!\geq\!C
, we get

1\!-\!\psi\big(\xi(Y_{p},\mathrm{o})\big)\leq\frac{C_{\beta,\mu}}{\big(\log\big(\frac{\mathtt{b}-1}{2\mathtt{b}}\mathtt{b}^{-|Y_{p}\wedge\mathrm{o}|}\big)\big)^{1+\beta}}\,\leq\,\frac{\!\!C^{\prime}}{\big(1\!-\!|Y_{p}\wedge\mathrm{o}|\big)^{1+\beta}}

By Proposition
3.13
with
f(x)\!=\!(1+x)^{-1-\beta}
, if
\beta\!>\!1
, then a.s.
\sum_{p\geq 0}(1\!-\!|Y_{p}\wedge\mathrm{o}|)^{-1-\beta}\!<\!\infty
, which implies (
51
) since

|Y_{p}\wedge\mathrm{o}|\!\rightarrow\!-\infty
, as
p\!\rightarrow\!\infty
as stated in Lemma
3.12

(i)
. This completes the proof of the proposition.
\hfill\blacksquare

We conclude this section with a general estimate for recurrent biased random walks on a (deterministic) rooted ordered tree
T
that is infinite. More precisely, we fix

\lambda\!\in\!(1,\infty)
and we denote by
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
the
\lambda
-biased RW whose transition probabilities are given for all
x,y\!\in\!T
by

\mathbf{P}(Y_{n+1}\!=\!y\,|\,Y_{n}\!=\!x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\lambda}{\lambda+k_{x}(T)}&\textrm{if $y\!=\!\overleftarrow{x}$ and $x\!\neq\!\varnothing$,}\\
\frac{1}{\lambda+k_{x}(T)}&\textrm{if $x\!=\!\overleftarrow{y}$ and $x\!\neq\!\varnothing$, }\\
\frac{1}{k_{\varnothing}(T)}&\textrm{if $x\!=\!\overleftarrow{y}\!=\!\varnothing$}\end{array}\right.

(52)

and
\mathbf{P}(Y_{n+1}\!=\!y\,|\,Y_{n}\!=\!x)\!=\!0
otherwise. Here, recall that
k_{x}(T)
is the number of children of
x
in
T
. We also recall that
|x|
is the height of
x
in
T
, that
x\!\wedge\!y
is the most recent common ancestor of
x
and
y
and that
d_{\mathtt{gr}}
stands for the graph-distance on
T
:
d_{\mathtt{gr}}(x,y)\!=\!|x|+|y|\!-\!2|x\wedge y|
.

Lemma 3.17

We keep the above notations. We assume that
Y
is recurrent. Then, for all
\ell,n_{1},n_{2}\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
such that
n_{1}\!<\!n_{2}
, we get

\mathbf{P}\Big(|Y_{n_{1}}|+|Y_{n_{2}}|\!-\!2\!\!\!\!\!\min_{\;n_{1}\leq n\leq n_{2}}\!\!\!\!\!|Y_{n}|\,\geq\,2\ell+d_{\mathtt{gr}}(Y_{n_{1}},Y_{n_{2}})\Big)\leq(n_{2}\!-\!n_{1})\frac{\lambda\!-\!1}{\lambda^{\ell}\!-\!1}\;.

(53)

Proof.
For all
x\!\in\!T
, we set
\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{x}\!=\!\inf\{n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}\!:\!Y_{n}\!=\!x\}
and
\pi(x)\!=\!(\lambda+k_{x}(T))\lambda^{-|x|}
if
x\!\neq\!\varnothing
and
\pi(\varnothing)\!=\!k_{\varnothing}(T)
. Note that
\pi
is an invariant measure and
standard arguments on electrical networks and random walks on graphs imply that for all distinct
x,y\!\in\!T
,

\pi(x)\mathbf{P}(\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{x}\!<\!\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{y}\,|\,Y_{0}\!=\!y)
is the effective conductance between

x
and
y
(see R. Lyons and Y. Peres
[
28
]
p. 25). If
y\!\in\,\rrbracket\varnothing,x\rrbracket
, then
it easily implies
\mathbf{P}(\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{x}\!<\!\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{{}^{\overleftarrow{y}}}\,|\,Y_{0}\!=\!y)\!=\!(\lambda\!-\!1)/(\lambda^{|x|-|y|+1}\!-\!1)
. Similarly,
\mathbf{P}(\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{x}\!<\!\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{{}^{\varnothing}}\,|\,Y_{0}\!=\!\varnothing)\!=\!k_{\varnothing}(T)^{-1}(\lambda\!-\!1)/(\lambda^{|x|}\!-\!1)
. Thus, for all distinct
x,y\!\in\!T
such that

y\!\in\!\llbracket\varnothing,x\rrbracket
,

\mathbf{P}(\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{x}\!<\!\mathtt{H}^{\circ}_{{}^{\overleftarrow{y}}}\,|\,Y_{0}\!=\!y)\leq(\lambda\!-\!1)/(\lambda^{|x|-|y|}\!-\!1).

We next define the following sequence of stopping times
(\sigma_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
by setting

\sigma_{0}\!=\!0
and
\sigma_{k+1}\!=\!\inf\{n\!>\!\sigma_{k}:|Y_{n}|\!\leq\!(|Y_{0}|\!-\!k\!-\!1)_{+}\}
that are a.s. finite since
Y
is recurrent. We then consider the (possibly empty) event
A_{k}\!=\!\big\{\exists n\!\in\!\{\sigma_{k},\ldots,\sigma_{k+1}\!-\!1\}:Y_{n}\!\in\!\llbracket\varnothing,Y_{0}\rrbracket\;\textrm{and}\;|Y_{n}|\!\geq\!\ell+(|Y_{0}|\!-\!k\!-\!1)_{+}\big\}
. We fix
z\!\in\!T
such that
|z|\!\geq\!\ell+(|z|\!-\!k\!-\!1)_{+}
, and let
x,y\!\in\!\llbracket\varnothing,z\rrbracket
be such that
|x|\!=\!\ell+(|z|\!-\!k\!-\!1)_{+}
and
|y|\!=\!(|z|\!-\!k\!-\!1)_{+}
.
By the strong Markov property at time
\sigma_{k}
and by the previous inequality for hitting times we get

\mathbf{P}\big(A_{k}\cap\{Y_{0}\!=\!z\}\big)\leq(\lambda\!-\!1)/(\lambda^{\ell}\!-\!1)\mathbf{P}(Y_{0}\!=\!z)
.
Since on
A_{k}
, we have
|Y_{0}|\!\geq\!\ell+(|Y_{0}|\!-\!k\!-\!1)_{+}
, the previous inequality implies

\mathbf{P}(A_{k})\!\leq\!(\lambda\!-\!1)/(\lambda^{\ell}\!-\!1)
.

Next observe that
|Y_{0}|+|Y_{n}|\!-\!2\min_{0\leq m\leq n}|Y_{m}|\!-\!d_{\mathtt{gr}}(Y_{0},Y_{n})\!=\!2(|Y_{0}\!\wedge\!Y_{n}|\!-\!\min_{0\leq m\leq n}\!|Y_{m}|)
and that

\big\{\,|Y_{0}\!\wedge\!Y_{n}|\!-\!\!\!\min_{0\leq m\leq n}\!\!|Y_{m}|\geq\ell\,\big\}\subset A_{0}\cup A_{1}\cup\ldots\cup A_{n-1}\;.

Thus,
\mathbf{P}(|Y_{0}|+|Y_{n}|\!-\!2\min_{0\leq m\leq n}|Y_{m}|\!\geq\!2\ell+d_{\mathtt{gr}}(Y_{0},Y_{n}))\leq n(\lambda\!-\!1)/(\lambda^{\ell}\!-\!1)
and we get (
53
)
by the Markov property at time
n_{1}
.
\hfill\blacksquare

3.5
Law invariance.

In this section, we first define a successor map for
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{[0,1]}
-labelled trees that centers the spatial positions
and
the genealogical tree at the individual coming next in the lexicographical order, generalizing Definition
2.6
for the pointed
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{[0,1]}
-labelled trees.
We then show that free branching random walks are invariant in law under this successor map.

Definition 3.18

Let
\Theta\!=\!(t,\varrho\,;\mathbf{x}\!=\!(x_{v})_{v\in t})
be a labelled tree in
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{[0,1]})
or in
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}\!(\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!\mathtt{b}})
.

(a)

We set
\mathtt{cent}(\Theta)\!=\!(\varphi_{|\varrho|}(t),\varphi_{|\varrho|}(\varrho);(\varphi_{|x_{\varrho}|}(x_{\varphi_{-|\varrho|}(v)}))_{v\in\varphi_{|\varrho|}(t)})
.
We keep calling
\mathtt{cent}(\cdot)
the

centering
map. As already mentioned, the spatial marks are also shifted.

(b)

We next set
\mathtt{scc}(\Theta)\!=\!\mathtt{cent}(t,\mathtt{scc}(\varrho);\mathbf{x})
where we recall that

\mathtt{scc}(\varrho)
stands for the vertex of
t
coming next in the lexicographical order as defined by (
13
).
We keep calling
\mathtt{scc}(\cdot)
the
successor map
.

(c)

Recall from Definition
2.6

(c)
the right-part
[(t,\varrho)]^{+}
of
(t,\varrho)
.
To simplify the notation, we set
(t^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime})\!=\![(t,\varrho)]^{+}
and we
recall from Definition
3.18

(c)
that there exists a unique one-to-one map

\psi\!:\!t^{\prime}\!\rightarrow\!\{\varrho_{|p}\,;\,p\!\leq\!|\varrho|\}\cup\{v\!\in\!t\!:\!\varrho\!<_{t}\!v\}
such that
\psi(\varrho^{\prime})\!=\!\varrho

that preserves the relative height and
that is increasing with respect to the lexicographical order;
then, we set
[\Theta]^{+}\!=\!(t^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\!=\!(x^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in t^{\prime}})
where
x^{\prime}_{v}\!=\!x_{\psi(v)}
, for all
v\!\in\!t^{\prime}
. We keep calling
[\Theta]^{+}

the
right-part
of
\Theta
.

(d)

We set
\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\Theta)\!=\![\mathtt{scc}(\Theta)]^{+}\!
; we keep calling
\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\cdot)
the
right-successor
map.
\hfill\square

We next explain a way to generate a free branching random walk from an i.i.d. field that is suited to the successor map. To that end we fix
\mathbf{t}\!=\!(t,u)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
such that

|t|_{\!-}\!=\!-\infty
. Let
\mathbf{o}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{-}
-indexed sequence of mutually independent uniform r.v. on
[0,1]
. Let
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
be a
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\![0,1]}
-valued branching random walk that has conditional law
Q^{{}_{\!}}_{\mathbf{o},\mathbf{t}}
given
\mathbf{o}
as in Definition
3.1

(iv)
. To simplify we denote by
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{t}}
the (unconditional) law of
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
.
Let
\varepsilon_{v}
,
U_{v}
,
v\!\in\!t
be independent r.v. such that
U_{v}
is uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
and
\mathbf{P}(\varepsilon_{v}\!=\!1)\!=\!\mathbf{P}(\varepsilon_{v}\!=\!-1)\!=\!1/2
.
From the field of i.i.d. r.v.
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{0}\!:=\!(t,u;(\varepsilon_{v},U_{v})_{v\in t})
, we now explain how to
construct a branching random walk that has law
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{t}}
.

Recall that
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
stands for the shortest path (with respect to the graph-distance) that joins
v
and
w
in
t
(along with the previous notation
\llbracket v,w\llbracket
,
\rrbracket v,w\rrbracket
and
\rrbracket v,w\llbracket
, introduced at the beginning of Section
3.2
). Recall also that
\rrbracket\!-\!\infty,v\rrbracket
stands for the lineage of
v
: namely,

\rrbracket\!-\!\infty,v\rrbracket\!=\!\{v_{|l}\,;l\!\leq\!|v|\}
.
Recall from (
11
) the definition of the most recent common ancestor
v\wedge w
in
t
.
We first set

\forall v\in t,\quad H_{v}=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\hskip 8.19447ptw\in\llbracket u,u\wedge v\llbracket}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(-\varepsilon_{w})\,+\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\hskip 8.19447ptw\in\llbracket u\wedge v,v\llbracket}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\varepsilon_{w}\;,

with the convention that
H_{u}\!=\!0
.
Note that
H
takes arbitrary negative values on the lineage of
v
. Namely
H
satisfies (
20
). It is easy to see that
(t,u;(H_{v})_{v\in t})
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-valued branching random walk whose spatial motion is that of a simple symmetric random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
and whose initial position is a.s.
H_{u}\!=\!0
. Recall from (
21
) the notation
v(k)
for all integers
k\!\leq\!H_{v}
: namely,

v(k)\!\in\,\rrbracket\!-\!\infty,v\rrbracket
is such that
H_{v(k)}\!=\!\min_{w\in\llbracket v(k),v\rrbracket}H_{w}\!=\!k\!>\!H_{\overleftarrow{v(k)}}
.

As in (
22
), for all
v\!\in\!t
we set
X_{v}\!=\!(U_{v(k)})_{k\leq H_{v}}
.
We denote
X_{u}
by
\mathbf{o}
. It shows that there exists a deterministic map
F
such that

F(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{0}):=\big(t,u;(X_{v})_{v\in t}\big)\;\textrm{, which has law $\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{t}}$.}

(54)

Let
u^{\prime}\!\in\!t
and set
F\big(t,u^{\prime};(\varepsilon_{v},U_{v})_{t\in t}\big)\!=:\!(t,u^{\prime},(X^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in t})
. Then, set
H^{\prime}_{v}\!=\!|X^{\prime}_{v}|
,
v\!\in\!t
.
We deterministically check that

\forall v\!\in\!t,\quad H^{\prime}_{v}-H_{v}=-H_{u^{\prime}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad X^{\prime}_{v}=\varphi_{|X_{u^{\prime}}|}(X_{v})\;.

(55)

We next define
[\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{0}]^{+}
as in Definition
3.18

(c)
: to simplify notation, we set

(t^{\prime},u^{\prime})\!=\![(t,u)]^{+}
and we
recall from Definition
2.6

(c)
that there exists a unique one-to-one map

\psi\!:\!t^{\prime}\!\rightarrow\!\{u_{|p}\,;\,p\!\leq\!|u|\}\cup\{v\!\in\!t\!:\!u\!<_{t}\!v\}
such that
\psi(u^{\prime})\!=\!u

that preserves the relative height and that is increasing with respect to the lexicographical order. Then, we simply define

[\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{0}]^{+}\!\!=\!\big(t^{\prime},u^{\prime},(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{v},U^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in t^{\prime}}\big)
where
(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{v},U^{\prime}_{v})\!=\!(\varepsilon_{\psi(v)},U_{\psi(v)})
for all
v\!\in\!t^{\prime}
and also set

\widetilde{\mathtt{scc}}^{+}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{0})=\big[\mathtt{scc}(t,u);(\varepsilon_{v},U_{v})_{{v\in t}}\big]^{+}.

(56)

By (
55
) and since
X_{v}
is a deterministic function of
((\varepsilon_{w},U_{w}),w\!\in\rrbracket\!-\!\infty,u\rrbracket\,\cup\,\rrbracket\!-\!\infty,v\rrbracket)
, it is easy to check deterministically

F\big(\widetilde{\mathtt{scc}}^{+}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{0})\big)=\mathtt{scc}^{+}(F(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{0}))\;.

(57)

Then, (
18
), (
54
), (
55
) and (
57
)
immediately entail the following lemma.

Lemma 3.19

Let
\mathbf{t}=(t,u)\!\in\!\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\bullet}
.
We assume that
|t|_{\!-}\!=\!-\infty
. Let
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\!=\!(t,u;(X_{v})_{v\in t})
have law
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{t}}
.
Then
\mathtt{scc}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})
has law
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathtt{scc}(\mathbf{t})}
and
\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})
has law

\mathbf{Q}_{\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\mathbf{t})}
.

The next result is the key point in the proofs of the various laws of large numbers
for the range of branching random walks that we prove in the next section.

Theorem 3.20

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\mu(k)\!=\!1
.
Let
\mathbf{o}\!:=\!(U_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}}
be a sequence of independent uniform r.v. on
\{1,\ldots,\mathtt{b}\}
. Let
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\!=\!(\tau^{*}\!,\,\varrho\,;\,(X_{v})_{v\in\tau^{*}})
be a
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
-valued branching random walk whose distribution is the following:
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\!=\!(\tau^{*}\!,\varrho)
is an IPGW
{}^{+}\!(\mu)
-tree as in Definition
2.8

(ii)
and conditionally given
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\!
and
\mathbf{o}
,

\boldsymbol{\Theta}
has law
Q^{{}_{\mathtt{b}}}_{{}^{\mathbf{o},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}}}
as in Definition
3.1

(iii)
. Then

\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})\overset{\textrm{(law)}}{=}\boldsymbol{\Theta}\;,

(58)

where we recall that
\mathtt{scc}^{+}
stands for the right-successor map as in Definition
3.18

(d)
.

Next, denote by
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
the sequence of the vertices that are not direct ancestors of
\varrho
listed in the lexicographical order. Namely,
v_{0}\!=\!\varrho
,

v_{n}\!<_{\tau^{*}}\!v_{n+1}
and
\{v_{n};n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}\}\!=\!\{v\!\in\!\tau\!:\!\varrho\!\leq_{\tau^{*}}\!v\}
.
Then,

\textrm{$\mathbf{P}$-a.s.}\quad\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{n}}\#\big\{X_{v_{k}};1\!\leq\!k\!\leq n\big\}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}:=\mathbf{P}\big(\mathbf{o}\!\notin\!\{X_{v_{n}};n\!\geq\!1\}\big).

(59)

Furthermore, if
\mu
satisfies (
49
), then
c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}\!>\!0
.

Proof.
Let
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{0}\!:=\!(\tau^{*}\!,\varrho;(\varepsilon_{v},U_{v})_{v\in\tau^{*}}\!)
be distributed as follows: conditionally given
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\!
,
\varepsilon_{v}
,
U_{v}
,
v\!\in\!t
are independent r.v. such that
U_{v}
is uniformly distributed on

[0,1]
and
\varepsilon_{v}
is uniformly distributed on
\{-1,1\}
.
Then, we set
F(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{0})\!=\!\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\prime}\!=\!(\tau^{*}\!,\varrho;(X^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in\tau^{*}})

as in (
54
).
Conditionally given
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\!
,
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\prime}
has law
\mathbf{Q}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}}
and Remarks
3.3
allows to take
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\!=\!\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\prime})
that has the desired law.
Lemma
3.19
combined with proposition
2.10
imply that

\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\prime})
has the same law as
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\prime}
and since obviously

\Phi_{\mathtt{b}}(\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\prime}))\!=\!\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})
, we get (
58
).

For all
n\!\geq\!m\!\geq\!0
, we set
R_{m,n}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})\!=\!\#\big\{X_{v_{k}};m\!<\!k\!\leq n\big\}
and we denote by
\mathtt{scc}^{+}_{\ell}
the
\ell
-th iterate of
\mathtt{scc}^{+}
.
Observe that

R_{m,n}(\mathtt{scc}^{+}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}))\!=\!R_{m+\ell,n+\ell}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})
, with an obvious notation.
Then, the r.v.
(R_{m,n}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+})_{m\geq n\geq 1})

satisfy Assumptions (1.7), (1.8) and (1.9) of Liggett’s version of Kingman’s
subadditive ergodic theorem (see Theorem 1.10 in
[
26
]
p. 1280)
that asserts that there exists a
[0,1]
-valued r.v.
R
such that

R_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})/n\!\rightarrow\!R
almost surely (and in
L^{1}
).

We next prove that
R
is a.s. constant. Recall from (
56
) the definition of
\widetilde{\mathtt{scc}}^{+}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{0})
and we denote by
\widetilde{\mathtt{scc}}^{+}_{\ell}
the
\ell
-th iterate of
\widetilde{\mathtt{scc}}^{+}
: namely,

\widetilde{\mathtt{scc}}^{+}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{0})\!=\![(\tau^{*},v_{\ell});(\varepsilon_{v},U_{v})_{v\in\tau^{*}}]^{+}
. Denote by
\mathscr{G}_{\ell}
the sigma-field generated by
\widetilde{\mathtt{scc}}^{+}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{0})
. As a consequence of (
57
),
\mathtt{\mathtt{scc}}^{+}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})\!=\!\Phi_{d}(F(\widetilde{\mathtt{scc}}^{+}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{0}))
, the r.v.
(R_{\ell,\ell+n}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are
\mathscr{G}_{\ell}
-measurable. Note that the sigma-fields
\mathscr{G}_{\ell}
decrease in
\ell
. Next, we set
\mathscr{G}\!=\!\bigcap_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{G}_{\ell}
that can be viewed as the tail sigma-field of the subtrees grafted on the infinite line of ancestors; since additional marks
(\varepsilon_{v},U_{v})
are i.i.d., Kolmogorov’s zero-one law applies and
\mathscr{G}
is
\mathbf{P}
-trivial. Furthermore, the subadditivity for the
R_{m,n}
entail that a.s.
R\!=\!\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n^{-1}R_{\ell,\ell+n}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})
, for all
\ell
. Thus,

R
is
\mathscr{G}
-measurable which implies that it is a.s. constant.

Consequently,
R\!=\!\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{E}[R_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})]/n
. By an elementary argument,

\mathbf{E}[R_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})]\!=\!\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}\!\mathbf{P}\big(X_{v_{k}}\!\notin\!\{X_{v_{k+1}},\ldots,X_{v_{n}}\}\big)\;.

Since the law of
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
is preserved by the map
\mathtt{scc}^{+}(\cdot)
, we get
\mathbf{P}\big(X_{v_{k}}\!\notin\!\{X_{v_{k+1}},\ldots,X_{v_{n}}\}\big)\!=\!\mathbf{P}\big(X_{\varrho}\!\notin\!\{X_{v_{1}},\ldots,X_{v_{n-k}}\}\big)
and thus

\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{E}[R_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})]\!=\frac{1}{n}\!\!\!\!\sum_{\;\;0\leq k\leq n-1}\!\!\!\mathbf{P}\big(X_{\varrho}\!\notin\!\{X_{v_{1}},\ldots,X_{v_{k}}\}\big)\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}

by Cesàro. Finally, observe that
c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}\!\geq\!\kappa_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}
, where
\kappa_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}
is defined in (
50
) in Proposition
3.16
that completes the proof of the theorem.
\hfill\blacksquare

3.6
Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

We fix
\gamma\!\in\!(1,2]
. Let
\tau
be a GW(
\mu
)-tree whose offspring distribution
\mu
satisfies
(\mathbf{H})
in (
1
). We set
\boldsymbol{\tau}\!=\!(\tau,\varnothing)
.
Let
\mathbf{o}\!:=\!(U_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}}
be a sequence of independent uniform r.v. on
\{1,\ldots,\mathtt{b}\}
. Let the
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
-valued branching random walk

\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{d}\!=\!(\tau\!,\,\varnothing\,;\,(Y_{v})_{v\in\tau})
have conditional law
Q^{\mathtt{b}}_{{\mathbf{o},\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
given
\tau
and
\mathbf{o}
as in Definition
3.1

(iii)
.
We also introduce the
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
-valued branching random walk
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+}_{\mathtt{b}}\!=\!(\tau\!,\,\varnothing\,;\,(Y^{+}_{v})_{v\in\tau})
that has conditional law
Q^{+\mathtt{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
given
\tau
as in Definition
3.1

(i)
.
For all integers
0\!\leq\!k\!<\!\#\tau
, we denote

R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathtt{b}})\!=\!\#\{Y_{v}\,;v\!\in\!\tau\!:v\!\leq_{\tau}\!v_{k}\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad R_{k}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+}_{\mathtt{b}})\!=\!\#\{Y^{+}_{v}\,;v\!\in\!\tau\!:v\!\leq_{\tau}\!v_{k}\},

(60)

where
v_{k}
stands for the
k
-th smallest vertex of
\tau
with respect to the lexicographical order
\leq_{\tau}
. Arguing as in Le Gall and L.
[
25
]
, we derive the following result from Theorem
3.20
.

Proposition 3.21

(adapted from Theorem 7
[
25
]
)

Let
\gamma\!\in\!(1,2]
and let
\mu
satisfy
(\mathbf{H})
as in (
1
).
We keep the above notation and we recall from (
59
)
the definition of
c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}
and from (
60
) the definition of
R_{n}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathtt{b}})
. Then,

\forall\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty),\quad\mathbf{P}\Big(\!\!\!\sup_{\;\;\;t\in[0,1]}\!\tfrac{1}{n}\big|R_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathtt{b}})-c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}\,nt\big|>\varepsilon\,\Big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n\Big)\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}0\;.

(61)

Proof.
The proof can be adapted verbatim from the way
Proposition 6 and Theorem 7 pp. 284-289 in
[
25
]
are deduced from Proposition 3 and Theorem 4 pp. 280-284 in
[
25
]
. Here our Theorem
3.20
plays the role of Proposition 3 and Theorem 4
in
[
25
]
and we only give a brief sketch of the proof. Following the same arguments as in Proposition 6 in
[
25
]
,
we first prove that for any fixed
s\!\in\![0,1]
,

\forall\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty),\quad\mathbf{P}\Big(\tfrac{1}{n}\big|R_{\lfloor ns\rfloor}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathtt{b}})-c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}\,ns\big|>\varepsilon\,\Big|\,\#\tau\!>\!n\Big)\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}0\;.

From this limit and the same absolute continuity argument as in the proof of Theorem 7 of
[
25
]
,
we get for any fixed
s\!\in\![0,1]
,

\forall\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty),\quad\mathbf{P}\Big(\tfrac{1}{n}\big|R_{\lfloor ns\rfloor}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathtt{b}})-c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}\,ns\big|>\varepsilon\,\Big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n\Big)\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow}0\;.

We then get (
61
) thanks to the following variant of the second Dini’s theorem.
\hfill\blacksquare

Lemma 3.22

For all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, let
(\Omega_{n},\mathscr{F}_{\!n},\mathbf{P}_{\!n})
be a probability space on which a nondecreasing right-continuous process
(X^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{t}})_{t\in[0,\infty)}
is defined. Let
(x(t))_{t\in[0,\infty)}
be a (deterministic) continuous function. Suppose that for all
t\!\in\![0,\infty)
, the real valued r.v.
X^{{}_{(n)}}_{{}^{t}}\!
under
\mathbf{P}_{\!n}
tends to
x(t)
in law. Then, for all

\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty)
and all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
,
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{P}_{\!n}\big(\sup_{t\in[0,p]}|X^{(n)}_{t}\!-\!x(t)|\!>\!\varepsilon\big)\!=\!0
.

Proof of Lemma
3.22
.
We fix
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
. For all
n,q\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, set
w_{n,q}\!=\!\max_{0\leq k\leq p2^{q}}|X^{(n)}_{k2^{-q}}\!-\!x(k2^{-q})|
. Since the convergence in law to a constant
implies the convergence in probability, we easily get for all
q
that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{P}_{\!n}(w_{n,q}\!>\!\varepsilon)=\!0
. Next set
v_{q}\!=\!\max\{|x(t)\!-\!x(s)|;s,t\!\in\![0,p]\!:\!|s\!-\!t|\!\leq\!2^{-q}\}
that tends to
0
as
q\to\infty
since
x
is continuous. By monotonicity of
X^{(n)}_{t}
, it is then easy to see that for all
t\!\in\![0,p]
, that
|X^{(n)}_{t}\!-\!x(t)|\!\leq\!2v_{q}+3w_{n,q}
, which easily implies the desired result.
\hfill\blacksquare

End of the proof of Theorem
1.1
.
We keep the notation from above and recall from (
59
)
the definition of
c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}
and from (
60
) the definition of
R_{n}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+}_{\mathtt{b}})
. We now derive Theorem
1.1

from (
61
) and Proposition
3.8
that allows to change the state space from
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
to
\mathbb{W}_{\!\mathtt{b}}
.

For all positive integers
n
, we set
c_{n}\!=\!4\log_{\mathtt{b}}n
,

L_{n}\!=\!\#\{v\!\in\!\tau:|Y^{+}_{v}|\!\leq\!c_{n}\}
,
N_{n}\!=\!\#\{v\!\in\!\tau\!:|v|\!=\!n\}
and
C_{n}\!=\!N_{0}+N_{1}+\ldots+N_{n}
. Let
(S_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a simple symmetric random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
with initial position
S_{0}\!=\!0
. By definition of
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+}_{\mathtt{b}}
,
for all
v\!\in\!\tau
, we get
\mathbf{E}[f(|Y^{+}_{v}|)\,|\,\tau]\!=\!\mathbf{E}[f(|S_{|v|}|)\,|\,\tau]
. Then, for all
b_{n}\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
we get

\mathbf{E}\big[L_{n}\,\big|\,\tau\big]=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}N_{k}\mathbf{P}\big(|S_{k}|\!\leq\!c_{n}\big)\leq C_{b_{n}}+\sigma(b_{n},\tfrac{c_{n}}{\sqrt{b_{n}}})\#\tau\;,

(62)

where we have set
\sigma(p,s)\!=\!\sup_{k\geq p}\mathbf{P}\big(|S_{k}|k^{-\frac{1}{2}}\!\!\!\leq\!s\big)
for all
p\!\in\!\mathbb{N}^{*}
and all
s\!\in\!(0,\infty)
.
Observe that any fixed
p
,
\lim_{s\rightarrow 0+}\sigma(p,s)\!=\!0
.
We now take
b_{n}\!=\!c_{n}^{4}
.

We next claim that for all
\eta\!\in\!(0,\infty)
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{P}\big(\tfrac{{}_{1}}{{}^{n}}C_{b_{n}}\!\geq\!\eta\,\big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n\big)=0\;.

(63)

Proof of (
63
).
For all
\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty)
and all
y\!\in\!\mathbb{R}
,
we set
f_{\varepsilon}(y)\!=\!(1\!-\!\varepsilon^{-1}(|y|\!-\!\varepsilon)_{+})_{+}
that is continuous and such that
{\bf 1}_{[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]}\!\leq\!f_{\varepsilon}\!\leq\!{\bf 1}_{[-2\varepsilon,2\varepsilon]}
. Recall from the introduction that
(H_{k})_{0\leq k<\#\tau}
stands for the height process of the tree
\tau
and recall the convergence (
4
). Then note that

\tfrac{{}_{1}}{{}^{n}}C_{a_{n}\varepsilon}\leq Q_{\varepsilon}^{n}:=\int_{0}^{1}f_{\varepsilon}\big(\tfrac{{}_{1}}{{}^{a_{n}}}H_{\lfloor ns\rfloor}\big)\,ds\;.

By (
4
),
Q_{\varepsilon}^{n}
under
\mathbf{P}(\,\cdot\,|\#\tau\!=\!n)
tends in law to
Q_{\varepsilon}\!:=\!\int_{0}^{1}f_{\varepsilon}(H_{s})\,ds
. We recall here that a.s. for all
s\in(0,1)
,
H_{s}\!>\!0
(see D.
[
10
]
or Section
4.4
). Thus
\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}Q_{\varepsilon}\!=\!0
and we get for all
\eta\!\in\!(0,\infty)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{P}\big(\tfrac{{}_{1}}{{}^{n}}C_{a_{n}\varepsilon}\!\geq\!\eta\,\big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{P}\big(Q_{\varepsilon}^{n}\!\geq\!\eta\,\big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n\big)

(64)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\mathbf{P}\big(Q_{\varepsilon}^{n}\!\geq\!\eta\,\big)=0.

Since
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a
\tfrac{{}_{\gamma-1}}{{}^{\gamma}}
-regularly varying
sequence,
b_{n}/a_{n}\!\rightarrow\!0
and (
64
) entails (
63
).
\hfill\square

Since
\tfrac{{}_{1}}{{}^{n}}C_{b_{n}}\!\in\!(0,1]
, it also implies that

\mathbf{E}[\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{n}}C_{b_{n}}|\#\tau\!=\!n]\!\rightarrow\!0
as
n\!\rightarrow\!\infty
. By
(
62
) we get
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{E}[\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{n}}L_{n}|\#\tau\!=\!n]\!=\!0
.
We next apply Proposition
3.8
to
c\!=\!\lfloor 4\log_{\mathtt{b}}n\rfloor\!+\!1
:
(
34
) implies the following.

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathbf{P}\Big(\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\tfrac{1}{n}\big|R_{\lfloor ns\rfloor}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+}_{\mathtt{b}})-c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}\,ns\big|>2\varepsilon\,\Big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\mathtt{b}n^{-1}+\mathbf{P}\big(\tfrac{1}{n}L_{n}\!>\!\varepsilon\,\big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n)+\mathbf{P}\Big(\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\tfrac{1}{n}\big|R_{\lfloor ns\rfloor}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathtt{b}})-c_{\mu,\mathtt{b}}\,ns\big|>\varepsilon\,\Big|\,\#\tau\!=\!n\Big)\;,

which implies (
3
) in Theorem
1.1
by (
61
) since
\mathbf{E}[\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{n}}L_{n}|\#\tau\!=\!n]\!\rightarrow\!0
as
n\!\rightarrow\!\infty
.
\hfill\blacksquare

4
Snake metrics and the Brownian cactus.

4.1
Pseudo-metrics on a closed interval.

Definition 4.1

Let
\zeta\!\in\!(0,\infty)
. We introduce the following spaces.

(a)

We denote by
\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta]^{2},\mathbb{R})
the space of continuous
functions from
[0,\zeta]^{2}
to
\mathbb{R}
that is a Banach space when equipped with
uniform norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert
.

(b)

We denote by
\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
the set of continuous pseudo-metrics on
[0,\zeta]
. Namely, it is the set of

d\!\in\!\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta]^{2},\mathbb{R})
such that for all
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\!\in\![0,\zeta]
,

d(s_{1},s_{2})\!\geq\!0,\;\,d(s_{1},s_{1})\!=\!0,\;\,d(s_{1},s_{2})\!=\!d(s_{2},s_{1})\;\,\mathrm{and}\;\,d(s_{1},s_{3})\!\leq\!d(s_{1},s_{2})+d(s_{2},s_{3}).

(c)

We denote by
\mathbf{MT}([0,\zeta])
the space of continuous pseudo-metrics
d\!\in\!\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
such that
for all
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4}\!\in\![0,\zeta]
,

d(s_{1},s_{2})+d(s_{3},s_{4})\leq\max\big(d(s_{1},s_{3})+d(s_{2},s_{4})\,;\,d(s_{1},s_{4})+d(s_{2},s_{3})\big)\;.

(65)

We call the latter inequality the
four points inequality
.
\hfill\square

We easily check that
\mathbf{MT}([0,\zeta])
and
\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
are closed subsets of

(\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta]^{2},\mathbb{R}),\lVert\cdot\rVert)
. We shall need the following
compactness criterion in
\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
.

Lemma 4.2

For all
\eta\!\in\!(0,\infty)
and all
d\!\in\!\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
, we set

q_{\eta}(d)=\max\big\{d(s,s^{\prime})\,;\;s,s^{\prime}\!\in\![0,\zeta]:|s\!-\!s^{\prime}|\leq\eta\big\}.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a subset of
\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
. Then, the closure of
\mathcal{D}
is compact if and only if
\,\,\sup_{d\in\mathcal{D}}q_{\eta}(d)\longrightarrow 0
as
\eta\!\rightarrow 0+
.

Proof.
For all
f\!\in\!\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta]^{2},\mathbb{R})
and all
\eta\!\in\!(0,\infty)
,
set

\omega_{\eta}(f)\!=\!\max\big\{|f(s_{1},s_{1}^{\prime})\!-\!f(s_{2},s_{2}^{\prime})|\,;\;s_{1},s_{2},s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime}\!\in\![0,\zeta]:|s_{1}\!-\!s_{2}|\!\vee\!|s_{1}^{\prime}\!-\!s_{2}^{\prime}|\leq\eta\big\}

that is the
\eta
-modulus of uniform continuity of
f
. The Arzelà-Ascoli Theorem asserts that the closure of
\mathcal{A}\!\subset\!\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta]^{2},\mathbb{R})
is
\rVert\cdot\rVert
-compact if and only if

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{A}}\omega_{\eta}(f)\!\rightarrow\!0
as
\eta\!\rightarrow\!0+
and
\sup_{f\in\mathcal{A}}|f(0,0)|\!<\!\infty
. The desired result follows from the easy observation that

q_{\eta}(d)\!\leq\!\omega_{\eta}(d)\!\leq\!2q_{\eta}(d)
(here, the second inequality is a consequence of the triangle inequality) and from the obvious fact that
d(0,0)\!=\!0
for any
d\!\in\!\mathcal{D}
.
\hfill\blacksquare

This lemma immediately implies the following tightness criterion.

Proposition 4.3

Let
(\mathbf{d}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of
\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
-valued random variables. Their laws are tight on
\big(\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta]^{2},\mathbb{R}),\lVert\cdot\rVert\big)
if and only if for all
\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty)
,
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{P}\big(q_{\eta}(\mathbf{d}_{n})\!>\!\varepsilon\big)\!\longrightarrow\!0
as
\eta\!\rightarrow\!0+
.

We now prove the following specific proposition that is used later in the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Proposition 4.4

Let
\mathbf{d}_{n},\mathbf{d}_{n}^{*}
,
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, be
\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
-valued r.v. such that

(i)

\mathbf{d}_{n}\!\longrightarrow\!\mathbf{d}
weakly on
\big(\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta]^{2},\mathbb{R}),\lVert\cdot\rVert\big)
;

(ii)

For all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
and for all

s,s^{\prime}\!\in\![0,\zeta]
, a.s.
\mathbf{d}_{n}^{*}(s,s^{\prime})\!\leq\!\mathbf{d}_{n}(s,s^{\prime})
;

(iii)

For all
s,s^{\prime}\!\in\![0,\zeta]
,
|\mathbf{d}_{n}^{*}(s,s^{\prime})\!-\!\mathbf{d}_{n}(s,s^{\prime})|\!\longrightarrow\!0
in probability.

Then,
(\mathbf{d}_{n}^{*},\mathbf{d}_{n})\!\longrightarrow\!(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})
weakly on
\big(\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta]^{2},\mathbb{R}),\lVert\cdot\rVert\big)^{2}
.

Proof.
By
(i)
and Proposition
4.3
,
for all
\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty)
,
\lim_{\eta\rightarrow 0}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{P}(q_{\eta}(\mathbf{d}_{n})\!>\!\varepsilon)\!=\!0
. Since
\mathbf{d}_{n}
and
\mathbf{d}_{n}^{*}
are continuous,
(ii)
actually implies that a.s. for all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
and for all
s,s^{\prime}\!\in\![0,\zeta]
,

\mathbf{d}_{n}^{*}(s,s^{\prime})\!\leq\!\mathbf{d}_{n}(s,s^{\prime})
, which immediately entails

q_{\eta}(\mathbf{d}_{n}^{*})\!\leq\!q_{\eta}(\mathbf{d}_{n})
. Thus,
\lim_{\eta\rightarrow 0}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{P}(q_{\eta}(\mathbf{d}^{*}_{n})\!>\!\varepsilon)\!=\!0
,
for all
\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty)
. By Proposition
4.3
, the laws of the r.v.
(\mathbf{d}_{n}^{*},\mathbf{d}_{n})
are tight in
\mathbf{C}([0,\zeta]^{2},\mathbb{R})^{2}
and we get the desired result because
(i)
and
(iii)
easily entail the weak convergence of the finite dimensional marginals of
(\mathbf{d}_{n}^{*},\mathbf{d}_{n})
to those of
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})
.
\hfill\blacksquare

Induced metric spaces.
Let
d\!\in\!\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
. We define the relation
\sim_{d}
on
[0,\zeta]
as follows:
for all
s_{1},s_{2}\!\in\![0,\zeta]
,
s_{1}\sim_{d}s_{2}
if and only if
d(s_{1},s_{2})\!=\!0
. Clearly
\sim_{d}
is an equivalence relation and we define the quotient space:

E_{d}=[0,\zeta]/\!\!\sim_{d},\quad\mathtt{proj}_{d}:[0,\zeta]\!\rightarrow\!E_{d},\;\textrm{the canonical projection},\;r_{d}=\mathtt{proj}_{d}(0){.}

(66)

We keep denoting
d
the metric induced by
d
on
E_{d}
. Since
d
is continuous on
[0,\zeta]^{2}
,
\mathtt{proj}_{d}
is continuous and
(E_{d},d,r_{d})
is a pointed compact and connected metric space. We also equip
E_{d}
with the pushforward measure
\mu_{d}
of the Lebesgue measure on
[0,\zeta]
via the canonical projection: namely, for all nonnegative measurable functions
f
on
E_{d}
,

\int_{E_{d}}\!\!\!f(x)\,\mu_{d}(dx)\!=\!\int_{0}^{\zeta}\!\!f(\mathtt{proj}_{d}(s))\,ds\;.

(67)

Note that
\mu_{d}
is a finite measure with total mass
\zeta
.

Remark 4.5

Let
(E_{d},d,r_{d},\mu_{d})
be the compact metric space corresponding to the pseudo-metric
d\!\in\!\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
. Let
a,b\!\in\!(0,\infty)
. Set
d^{\prime}(s_{1},s_{2})\!=\!ad(s_{1}/b,s_{2}/b)
,
s_{1},s_{2}\!\in\![0,b\zeta]
. Then, we easily check that
d^{\prime}\!\in\!\mathbf{M}([0,b\zeta])
and
(E_{d^{\prime}},d^{\prime},r_{d^{\prime}},\mu_{d^{\prime}})
is isometric to

(E_{d},ad,r_{d},b\mu_{d})
.
\hfill\square

Real trees.
When
d\!\in\!\mathbf{MT}([0,\zeta])
, the resulting space
E_{d}
is a real tree. More precisely, real trees are
metric spaces that extend the definition of graph-trees; they are defined as follows.

Definition 4.6

let
(T,d)
be a metric space; it is a
real tree
if and only if the following holds true.

(a)

For any
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\!\in\!T
, there is a unique isometry

f:[0,d(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})]\!\rightarrow\!T
such
that
f(0)\!=\!\sigma_{1}
and
f(d(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}))\!=\!\sigma_{2}
. Then, we set

\llbracket\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rrbracket\!:=\!f([0,d(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})])
.

(b)

For any continuous injective function

g:[0,1]\!\rightarrow\!T
, such that
g(0)=\sigma_{1}
and
g(1)=\sigma_{2}
,
g([0,1])\!=\!\llbracket\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rrbracket
.
\hfill\square

It turns out that the four points inequality is a metric characterisation of real trees. More precisely, if
(T,d)
is a connected metric space, then
(T,d)
is a real tree if and only if for any
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4}\in T
,

d(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})+d(\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4})\leq\big(d(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{3})+d(\sigma_{2},\sigma_{4})\big)\vee\big(d(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{4})+d(\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})\big).

We refer to Evans
[
15
]
or to Dress, Moulton and Terhalle
[
9
]
for a detailed account on this property.

Let us introduce some notation about real trees. Let
(T,d)
be a compact pointed real tree. We distinguish a point
r\!\in\!T
that is viewed as a
root
.
We then define the
length
measure
\mathtt{Length}(\cdot)
on
T
as the one-dimensional Hausdorff measure: namely, it is the unique Borel measure such that
\mathtt{Length}(\llbracket\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\rrbracket)\!=\!d(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})
, for all
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\!\in\!T
. Let us next introduce branch points:
let
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\!\in\!T
; then the geodesic paths
\llbracket\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rrbracket
,
\llbracket\sigma_{1},\sigma_{3}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\rrbracket
have exactly one point in common that is called the branch point of
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}
and that is denoted by
\mathtt{br}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})
; namely

\{\mathtt{br}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})\}=\llbracket\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rrbracket\cap\llbracket\sigma_{1},\sigma_{3}\rrbracket\cap\llbracket\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\rrbracket.

(68)

If we view
T
as a family tree whose ancestor is
r
, then
\mathtt{br}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},r)
is the most recent common ancestor of
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
and we use the following notation

\sigma_{1}\wedge\sigma_{2}=\mathtt{br}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},r).

We next introduce the (extended)
degree
of any point

\sigma\!\in\!T
as follows.

\mathtt{deg}(\sigma)
is the (possibly infinite) number of connected components of
the open set
T\backslash\{\sigma\}
.

(69)

We say that
\sigma
is a
branch point
if

\mathtt{deg}(\sigma)\!\geq\!3
; we say that
\sigma
is a
leaf
if
\mathtt{deg}(\sigma)\!=\!1
and we say that
\sigma
is
simple
if
\mathtt{deg}(\sigma)\!=\!2
.
We shall use the following notation for the set of leaves of
T

\mathtt{Lf}(T):=\big\{\sigma\!\in\!T\backslash\{r\}:\mathtt{deg}(\sigma)\!=\!1\big\}\;.

Definition 4.7

Let
\mu
be a finite Borel measure on
T
; then
(T,d,r,\mu)
is a
continuum real tree
in the sense of Aldous
[
3
]

if
T
is compact and if

\mu
is diffuse, the topological support of
\mu
is
T
and
\mu\big(T\backslash\mathtt{Lf}(T)\big)\!=\!0
.

(70)

Lemma 4.8

Let
\zeta\!\in\!(0,\infty)
and
d\!\in\!\mathbf{MT}([0,\zeta])
. To simplify we denote by
(T,d,r,\mu)
the pointed and measured compact real tree induced by the pseudo-metric
d
and we denote by
p\!:\![0,\zeta]\rightarrow T
the canonical projection. We set

U\!=\!\big\{s\!\in\!(0,\zeta):\;\forall s^{\prime}\!\in\![0,\zeta]\backslash\{s\},\;d(0,s)+d(s,s^{\prime})\!>\!d(0,s^{\prime})\big\}\;.

(71)

We assume that
[0,\zeta]\backslash U
is Lebesgue-negligible. Then,
T
is a continuum real tree as defined in (
70
).

Proof.
By construction and since
p
is continuous, the topological support of
\mu
is
T
.
We next show that
p(U)\!\subset\!\mathtt{Lf}(T)
. Let
s\!\in\!(0,\zeta)
be such that
p(s)\!\neq\!r
and such that
\mathtt{deg}(p(s))\!\geq\!2
. Let
s^{\prime}
be such that
p(s^{\prime})
belongs to a connected component of
T\backslash\{p(s)\}
that does not contain
r
. Then,

p(s)\!\in\,\rrbracket r,p(s^{\prime})\llbracket\,
, which implies that

d(p(s^{\prime}),r)\!=\!d(r,p(s))+d(p(s),p(s^{\prime}))
, namely,
d(0,s^{\prime})\!=\!d(0,s)+d(s,s^{\prime})
. Thus,
s\!\notin\!U
. This proves that
p(U)\!\subset\!\mathtt{Lf}(T)
.
Since
[0,\zeta]\backslash U
is Lebesgue negligible,
T\backslash\mathtt{Lf}(T)
is
\mu
-negligible.

It remains to prove that
\mu
is diffuse. Let
s\!\in\![0,\zeta]
. If
s\!\notin\!U
, then
p(s)\!\in\!T\backslash\mathtt{Lf}(T)
that is
\mu
-negligible; thus
\mu(\{p(s)\})\!=\!0
.
Assume now that
s\!\in\!U
and suppose that
s^{\prime}
is such that
d(s,s^{\prime})\!=\!0
. The triangle inequality for
d
implies that
d(0,s)\!=\!d(0,s^{\prime})
. Since
s\!\in\!U
, we get
s\!=\!s^{\prime}
. Thus
p^{-1}(\{p(s)\})\!=\!\{s\}
and
\mu(\{p(s)\})\!=\!0
, which completes the proof of the lemma.
\hfill\blacksquare

Example 4.9

(
Real trees spanned by graph-trees
) Let
\mathtt{T}
be a discrete graph-tree with a special vertex
\rho
viewed as a root. We denote by
d_{\mathtt{gr}}
the graph-distance on
\mathtt{T}
. The real tree spanned by
\mathtt{T}
is obtained by joining neighbouring vertices of
\mathtt{T}
by a unit-length segment of the real line with its own metric. Formally, we can take
\widetilde{\mathtt{T}}\!=\!\{(\rho,1)\}\cup\bigcup_{v\in\mathtt{T}\backslash\{\rho\}}\{v\}\!\times\!(0,1]
and for all
\sigma\!=\!(v,s)
and
\sigma^{\prime}\!=\!(v^{\prime},s^{\prime})
in
\widetilde{\mathtt{T}}
, we set:

\widetilde{d}_{\mathtt{gr}}^{\,\mathtt{T}}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}d_{\mathtt{gr}}(v,v^{\prime})+s+s^{\prime}-2&\textrm{if $v\!\wedge\!v^{\prime}\!\notin\!\{v,v^{\prime}\}$,}\\
d_{\mathtt{gr}}(v,v^{\prime})+s-s^{\prime}&\textrm{if $v^{\prime}\!=\!v\!\wedge\!v^{\prime}$ and $v\!\neq\!v^{\prime}$, }\\
|s-s^{\prime}|&\textrm{if $v\!=\!v^{\prime}$, }\end{array}\right.

where
v\!\wedge\!v^{\prime}
is the most recent common ancestor of
v
and
v^{\prime}
when we view
\mathtt{T}
as a family tree whose ancestor is
\rho
. Clearly,
(\widetilde{\mathtt{T}},\widetilde{d}_{\mathtt{gr}}^{\,\mathtt{T}})
is a real tree and if we identify
\mathtt{T}
with

\mathtt{T}\!\times\!\{1\}\!\subset\!\widetilde{\mathtt{T}}
, we easily check that
\widetilde{d}_{\mathtt{gr}}^{\,\mathtt{T}}
extends
d_{\mathtt{gr}}
.
Note that
(\widetilde{\mathtt{T}},\widetilde{d}_{\mathtt{gr}}^{\,\mathtt{T}})
is compact if and only if
\mathtt{T}
is finite.
\hfill\square

Example 4.10

(
Real trees coded by continuous functions
) Let
\zeta\!\in\!(0,\infty)
and
let
h\!:\![0,\zeta]\!\rightarrow\!\mathbb{R}
be continuous. For all
s_{1},s_{2}\!\in\![0,\zeta]
, we set

m_{h}(s_{1},s_{2})=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\inf_{\,\hskip 16.38895pts\in[s_{1}\wedge s_{2},s_{1}\vee s_{2}]}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!h(s)\qquad\textrm{and}\quad d_{h}(s_{1},s_{2})=h(s_{1})+h(s_{2})-2m_{h}(s_{1},s_{2})\;.

(72)

We easily check that
d_{h}\!\in\!\mathbf{MT}([0,\zeta])
and to simplify we denote by
(T_{h},d_{h},r_{h},\mu_{h})

the induced metric space
(E_{d_{h}},d_{h},r_{d_{h}},\mu_{d_{h}})
as defined in (
66
)
and (
67
);
we also denote by
p_{h}\!:\![0,\zeta]\!\rightarrow\!T_{h}
the canonical projection (instead of
\mathtt{proj}_{d_{h}}
);
(T_{h},d_{h},r_{h},\mu_{h})
is a pointed measured compact real tree that is the
tree coded by the function
h
.

Let
h^{\prime}\!:\![0,\zeta]\!\rightarrow\!\mathbb{R}
be continuous. Then observe that

\forall s_{1},s_{2},\!\in\![0,\zeta],\quad\big|d_{h}(s_{1},s_{2})\!-\!d_{h^{\prime}}(s_{1},s_{2})\big|\leq 4\sup_{s\in[0,\zeta]}|h(s)\!-\!h^{\prime}(s)|\;,

(73)

which shows the continuity in
\mathbf{MT}([0,\zeta])
of the application
h\mapsto d_{h}
.
\hfill\square

Remark 4.11

Let
(T_{h},d_{h},r_{h})
be the real tree coded by the continuous function

h\!:\![0,\zeta]\!\rightarrow\!\mathbb{R}
and the canonical projection
p_{h}
as explained above. Suppose that
h
is nonnegative and that
h(0)\!=\!0
. Then
d_{h}(0,s)\!=\!h(s)
and for all
s_{1},s_{2}\!\in\![0,\zeta]
, we easily get
m_{h}(s_{1},s_{2})\!=\!d_{h}\big(r_{h},p_{h}(s_{1})\!\wedge\!p_{h}(s_{2})\big)
.

However, let
d\!\in\!\mathbf{MT}([0,\zeta])
and set
h(s)\!=\!d(0,s)
,
s\!\in\![0,\zeta]

that is a continuous function. The real tree
T\!=\![0,\zeta]/\!\sim_{d}
is in general different from the the real tree
T_{h}
coded by
h
: namely
d\!\neq\!d_{h}
. But let us mention that all compact real trees can be coded by (many) continuous functions (see D.
[
12
]
for more details on the coding of real trees).
\hfill\square

Gromov-Hausdorff-Prokhorov metric.
Let
(E_{1},d_{1},r_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(E_{2},d_{2},r_{2},\mu_{2})
be two pointed measured compact metric spaces: here
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are finite measures on the respective Borel sigma-fields of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, and
r_{1}\!\in\!E_{1}
and
r_{2}\!\in\!E_{2}
are distinguished points. The pointed
Gromov-Hausdorff-Prokhorov distance
(the
GHP-distance
for short) between
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
is defined by

{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}(E_{1},E_{2})\!=\!\inf\!\Big\{d_{E}^{\textrm{Haus}}\!\big(\phi_{1}(E_{1}),\phi_{2}(E_{2})\big)\\
+d_{E}(\phi_{1}(r_{1}),\phi_{2}(r_{2}))+d_{E}^{\textrm{Prok}}\big(\mu_{1}\!\circ\!\phi^{-1}_{1}\!\!,\mu_{2}\!\circ\!\phi^{-1}_{2}\big)\Big\}.

(74)

Here, the infimum is taken over all Polish spaces
(E,d_{E})
and all isometric embeddings
\phi_{i}:E_{i}\hookrightarrow E
,
i\!\in\!\{1,2\}
;

d_{E}^{\textrm{Haus}}
stands for the Hausdorff distance on the space of compact subsets of
E
(namely,

d_{E}^{\textrm{Haus}}(K_{1},K_{2})\!=\!\inf\{\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty):K_{1}\!\subset\!K_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}\;\textrm{and}\;K_{2}\!\subset\!K_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}\}
, where
A^{(\varepsilon)}\!=\!\{y\!\in\!E:d_{E}(y,A)\!\leq\!\varepsilon\}
for all non-empty

A\!\subset\!E
);

d_{E}^{\textrm{Prok}}
stands for the Prokhorov distance on the space of finite Borel measures on
E
(namely,

d_{E}^{\textrm{Prok}}(\mu,\nu)\!=\!\inf\{\varepsilon\!\in\!(0,\infty)\!:\!\mu(K)\!\leq\!\nu(K^{(\varepsilon)})+\varepsilon\;\textrm{and}\;\nu(K)\!\leq\!\mu(K^{(\varepsilon)})+\varepsilon,\;\forall K\!\subset\!E\;\textrm{compact}\}
); for all

i\!\in\!\{1,2\}
,
\mu_{i}\!\circ\!\phi^{-1}_{i}
stands for the pushforward measure of
\mu_{i}
via
\phi_{i}
.

Remark 4.12

Let
(E,d)
be a Polish space and let
a,b,c\!\in\!(0,\infty)
.
We denote by
d_{\mathtt{Prok}}
(resp.
d^{a}_{\mathtt{Prok}}
)
the Prokhorov distance on the space of finite Borel measures on
(E,d)
(resp.
(E,ad)
) and we denote by
d_{\mathtt{Haus}}
(resp.
d^{a}_{\mathtt{Haus}}
)
the Hausdorff distance on the space of the compact subsets of
(E,d)
(resp.
(E,ad)
).
First note that
d^{a}_{\mathtt{Haus}}\!=\!ad_{\mathtt{Haus}}
.
Then, observe that

d_{\mathtt{Prok}}(b\mu,c\mu)\!=\!|b\!-\!c|\mu(E)
and that

(a\wedge b)d_{\mathtt{Prok}}(\mu,\nu)\!\leq\!d^{a}_{\mathtt{Prok}}(b\mu,b\nu)\!\leq\!(a\vee b)d_{\mathtt{Prok}}(\mu,\nu)
.

Let
(E_{1},d_{1},r_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(E_{2},d_{2},r_{2},\mu_{2})
be two pointed measured compact metric spaces. We set
(E^{\prime}_{i},d^{\prime}_{i},r^{\prime}_{i},\mu^{\prime}_{i})\!=\!(E_{i},ad_{i},r_{i},b\mu_{i})
,
i\!\in\!\{1,2\}
. Then, it is easy to check that
(a\wedge b){\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}(E_{1},E_{2})\!\leq\!{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}(E^{\prime}_{1},E^{\prime}_{2})\!\leq\!(a\vee b){\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}(E_{1},E_{2})
.
\hfill\square

Example 4.13

Let
\mathtt{T}
be a finite graph-tree that is equipped with its graph distance and with its counting measure:
\mathtt{m}\!=\!\sum_{v\in\mathtt{T}}\delta_{v}
. Let
(\widetilde{\mathtt{T}},\widetilde{d}_{\mathtt{gr}}^{\,\mathtt{T}})
be the compact real tree spanned by
\mathtt{T}
(see Example
4.9
). We equip
\widetilde{\mathtt{T}}
with its length measure
\mathtt{Length}
.
Up to obvious identifications, we can assume that
\mathtt{T}\!\subset\!\widetilde{\mathtt{T}}
.
Then, we easily get
d_{\mathtt{Haus}}(\mathtt{T},\widetilde{\mathtt{T}})\!\leq\!1
,
d_{\mathtt{Prok}}(\mathtt{m},\mathtt{Length})\!\leq\!2
and thus,
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}(\mathtt{T},\widetilde{\mathtt{T}})\!\leq\!3
.
\hfill\square

We next recall from Theorem 2.5 in Abraham, Delmas and Hoscheit
[
1
]

the following assertions:
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}
is a pseudo-metric (i.e. it is symmetric and it satisfies the triangle inequality) and
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}(E_{1},E_{2})\!=\!0
if and only if
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are
isometric
, namely if and only if there exists a bijective isometry
\phi\!:\!E_{1}\!\rightarrow\!E_{2}
such that
\phi(r_{1})\!=\!r_{2}
and such that
\mu_{2}\!=\!\mu_{1}\circ\phi^{-1}
. Denote by
\mathbb{M}
the isometry classes of pointed measured compact metric spaces. Then, Theorem 2.5 in Abraham, Delmas and Hoscheit
[
1
]
asserts that

(\mathbb{M},{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}})
is a complete and separable metric space.

(75)

Proposition 4.14

Let
d,d^{\prime}\!\in\!\mathbf{M}([0,\zeta])
and let
E_{d}
and
E_{d^{\prime}}
be the induced pointed measured compact metric spaces as defined by (
66
) and (
67
). Then

{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}(E_{d},E_{d^{\prime}})\leq\tfrac{3}{2}\lVert d\!-\!d^{\prime}\rVert\;.

(76)

Proof.
We use the notation from (
66
) and we set

\mathcal{R}\!=\!\{(\mathtt{proj}_{d}(s),\mathtt{proj}_{d^{\prime}}(s));s\!\in\![0,\zeta]\}
that is a relation on
E_{d}\!\times E_{d^{\prime}}
since for all
x\!\in\!E_{d}
and all
x^{\prime}\!\in\!E_{d^{\prime}}
,
\mathcal{R}\cap(\{x\}\times E_{d^{\prime}})
and
\mathcal{R}\cap(E_{d}\times\{x^{\prime}\})
are not empty. By the triangle inequality for pseudo-metrics, we get

\mathtt{dis}(\mathcal{R}):=\sup\big\{\big|d(x,y)\!-\!d^{\prime}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big|\,;\;(x,x^{\prime}),(y,y^{\prime})\!\in\!\mathcal{R}\big\}\leq\lVert d\!-\!d^{\prime}\rVert\;.

(77)

We next set
G\!=\!E_{d}\sqcup E_{d^{\prime}}
that is a disjoint union, and we define a metric
\delta
on
G
as follows:
\delta
coincides with
d
on
E_{d}\!\times\!E_{d}
and with
d^{\prime}
on
E_{d^{\prime}}\!\times\!E_{d^{\prime}}
and for all
x\!\in\!E_{d}
and all
x^{\prime}\!\in\!E_{d^{\prime}}
, we set

\delta(x,x^{\prime})=\inf\big\{d(x,y)+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathtt{dis}(\mathcal{R})+d^{\prime}\!(y^{\prime},x^{\prime})\,;\;(y,y^{\prime})\!\in\!\mathcal{R}\big\}\;.

We easily check that
\delta
is a separable compact metric on
G
. Thus,
(G,\delta)
is Polish. The inclusions
E_{d}\hookrightarrow G
and
E_{d^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow G
are isometries and since

(r_{d},r_{d^{\prime}})\!=\!(\mathtt{proj}_{d}(0),\mathtt{proj}_{d^{\prime}}(0))\!\in\!\mathcal{R}
, we get

\delta_{\mathtt{Haus}}(E_{d},E_{d^{\prime}})\vee\delta(r_{d},r_{d^{\prime}})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathtt{dis}(\mathcal{R})\;,

(78)

where
\delta_{\mathtt{Haus}}
stands for the Hausdorff distance of the space of compacts subsets of
G
.

Let
K
be a closed (and thus compact) subset of
G
. We set
Q\!=\!E_{d}\cap K
and
Q^{\prime}\!=\!E_{d^{\prime}}\cap K
that are also compact subsets of
G
.
Note that
\mu_{d}(K)=\mu_{d}(Q)
and
\mu_{d^{\prime}}(K)\!=\!\mu_{d^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
. We set

C\!=\!\mathtt{proj}_{d}^{-1}(Q)
,
Q^{\prime}_{0}\!=\!\mathtt{proj}_{d^{\prime}}(C)
and
C^{\prime}\!=\!\mathtt{proj}_{d^{\prime}}^{-1}(Q^{\prime}_{0})
. Since
\mathtt{proj}_{d}
and

\mathtt{proj}_{d^{\prime}}
are continuous,
C
and
C^{\prime}
are compact subsets of
[0,\zeta]
and
Q^{\prime}_{0}
is a compact subset of
E_{d^{\prime}}
. Denote by
\ell
the Lebesgue measure on
[0,\infty)
. Observe that
C\!\subset\!C^{\prime}
, which implies that
\mu_{d}(K)\!=\!\mu_{d}(Q)\!=\!\ell(C)\!\leq\!\ell(C^{\prime})\!=\!\mu_{d^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}_{0})
.
Let
x^{\prime}\!\in\!Q_{0}^{\prime}
. There exists
s\!\in\!C
such that
\mathtt{proj}_{d^{\prime}}(s)\!=\!x^{\prime}
. But note that
x\!:=\!\mathtt{proj}_{d}(s)\!\in\!Q
and thus
(x,x^{\prime})\!\in\!\mathcal{R}
. Set
\eta\!:=\!\frac{{}_{1}}{{}^{2}}\mathtt{dis}(\mathcal{R})
; then we have proved that
\mu_{d}(K)\!\leq\!\mu_{d^{\prime}}(K^{\eta})
where

K^{\eta}\!=\!\{z\!\in\!G\!:\!\delta(z,K)\!\leq\!\eta\}
. Similarly, we prove that
\mu_{d^{\prime}}(K)\!\leq\!\mu_{d}(K^{\eta})
which implies that
\delta_{\mathtt{Prok}}(\mu_{d},\mu_{d^{\prime}})\!\leq\!\eta
, where
\delta_{\mathtt{Prok}}
stands for the Prohorov distance on the space of finite Borel measures on
G
. This inequality
combined with (
78
) implies that
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathtt{GHP}}(E_{d},E_{d^{\prime}})\!\leq\!\frac{{}_{3}}{{}^{2}}\mathtt{dis}(\mathcal{R})
, which entails (
76
) by (
77
).
\hfill\blacksquare

4.2
Scaling limit of the range of biased RWs on trees.

Let
T
be an infinite rooted ordered tree. We fix
\lambda\!\in\!(1,\infty)
and we denote by
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
the
\lambda
-biased RW on
T
whose transition probabilities are given by (
52
). We make
T
a real tree by joining neighbouring vertices by a line isometric to
[0,1]
as explained in Example
4.9
and
we keep denoting by
(T,d_{\mathtt{gr}},\varnothing)
the resulting rooted real tree. We also denote simply by
(Y_{s})_{s\in[0,\infty)}
the continuous interpolation
of
Y
: namely, for all
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
and all
s\!\in\![n,n+1]
,
Y_{s}
is the unique point of the line
\llbracket Y_{n},Y_{n+1}\rrbracket
in the (spanned) real tree
T
such that
